§1 — Why this is its own plan

Over one execution session the fleet display was wrong in five distinct ways, and in every case a person noticed before the tooling did. The lead twice identified a dead worker by looking at GPU utilisation while the ticker reported it running; a peer session watched six finished runs display as 6 working for 38 minutes, and later two more for 13 and 6 minutes, finding each by stat-ing a manifest rather than by being told. Three of those defects were fixed inside clive-local-workers §7, because that is where they were found. Two were not, and they have no owning plan at all — which is the condition this plan exists to end, since a defect named only in a followup is hidden work by the project's own rule.

The reason to separate them is that they are not about local workers. §7 of that plan is titled strengthen the harness around local workers, so the model comparison is a model comparison, and it has been absorbing crew-wide observability work because the local lane happened to expose it. That was correct while the lane was blocked; it is now obscuring both subjects. The qualification questions belong there. Whether the display tells the truth belongs here.

The producer-orphans RCA is the evidence base for §2. It closed two of its three defects; this plan owns the third.

✓ landed 2026-09-04

§2 — The registry disagrees with the machine, and the guard trusts the registry

Liveness is derived from the process table on every read, not remembered: _record_producer_running in reckon/crew/runs.py answers from the pid alone and deliberately not from the seat's held-state, so a live producer holding no lock still reads live — the direction a guard must not be fooled in. _reconcile_watch_record repairs a stale record in place without taking the seat lock, so observing can never deny an arming. Asserted in tests/test_crew_watch_visibility.py in both directions through the dispatch path rather than the helper: a stale record claiming a live watcher refuses, and a running producer whose record says absent is admitted. Landed under commit 896ba48, which wrote no comment against this section. Full record: §2 landed.

✓ landed 2026-09-04

§3 — gc scans the repository it is standing in, not the one it names

All three measures hold. _resolved_gc_repo in reckon/cli.py resolves a named project to its checkout registered in mounts rather than the current directory, refuses an explicit --repo that disagrees while naming both paths, and takes --confirm-cross-repo to state a cross-pairing deliberately. Five cases in tests/test_crew_gc.py cover both directions plus test_gc_result_names_the_repository_and_the_ledger_it_read, so an answer can no longer be attributed to the wrong project by a reader. Landed under commit 45fc1b9, which wrote no comment against this section. Full record: §3 landed.

§4 — What a person should be able to read off the pane

The three sections above are individual defects. This one is the standing obligation they violated, written down so a future change can be checked against it rather than against taste. Twice in one session the honest signal was outside the tool: GPU utilisation, and a manifest on disk. Both times the pane asserted the opposite.

  • A count of working runs means work is in progress. Not that pointers exist. A run whose process is gone, or whose manifest is terminal, is not working, and the display must not add it to that bucket.
  • Activity is generation, not bytes. A stream file's mtime advances on tool progress and system records; one measured run carried 30,461 records of which 156 were assistant turns while it sat blocked on a ten-minute wait. Liveness derived from mtime called that healthy, and so did the orchestrator reading it.
  • The pane says which code produced it. Already landed as the producer version stamp; recorded here because it is part of this obligation rather than a separate courtesy.
  • A wrong answer is worse than no answer. Where a signal cannot be derived honestly, report it as unknown. Absence of evidence has been mistaken for evidence three times in this fleet's short history — a budget signal, a manifest, and a watcher — and every instance cost more than a stated unknown would have.
  • A surface never drops the discriminating value. The obligation above governs a signal that cannot be derived; this one governs a signal the record already holds. Three times in one day the value that would have settled a question existed and the surface discarded it: a reset time parsed out of a rejection into resets_at: null, a manifest status dropped by a reader that returned eight other keys, and a reason truncated before the clause naming its predicate. In none of these was the cost a wrong answer — it was an unanswerable question, which is more expensive, because a wrong answer can be contradicted by a measurement and an absent one sends its reader to look somewhere else. A partial result is the worst form: eight keys and no status is indistinguishable from a manifest whose author omitted the status, so it manufactures a plausible wrong answer, and plausible wrong answers are the ones that survive review.
MeasureRequired evidence
The buckets partition honestlyA run whose process is gone or whose manifest is terminal never appears in the working count, asserted across every phase a pointer can hold including those it never advanced past.
Activity means generationAny liveness or stall judgement is derived from generation events rather than file mtime, asserted against a recorded stream that is byte-active and generation-idle.
The stored state and the rendered row agreeThe transition fold's stored state is re-derived when the row is, so the two cannot hold different answers. Measured 2026-09-03 on r-20260903T122811545240: from 15:06 the row rendered blocked with a full diagnosis while the fold still held abandoned from 14:54, and the disagreement surfaced only at promotion thirteen minutes later, which reported from_state: abandoned. The correct answer was on screen and the wrong one in the store, which made an already-fixed defect look live and cost a peer a false decay diagnosis. Asserted against a pointer whose classification changes between polls: the next emitted transition must carry the state at evaluation, never a label superseded by a reading the display has already shown.
A terminal reading is not un-seen without a stated reasonResolved, and the carve-out stands as written. The staleness guard protects complete and failed and leaves blocked discardable, because blocked is a solicitation a resumed worker can answer. Asked to judge it, the peer who raised the decay concern proposed the discard require the process to be alive and the log to be newer than the manifest — which is exactly the condition already in the code, verified by reading it rather than assumed: alive is True together with log.st_mtime_ns > manifest.st_mtime_ns. A dead process's blocked manifest is therefore never discarded, which was the case they were protecting. Two independent derivations reaching the same guard is the strongest evidence this section has for leaving something alone. What remains worth doing is the smaller half of their suggestion: record that the discard fired, rather than silently emptying the status.
A node's gate is derived, never hand-listedEvery test file that imports a changed module is in the node's gate, computed from the declared write paths rather than remembered by whoever wrote the dispatch. Measured 2026-09-03: a correct guard asserting the exact invariant a node was about to break had been in the tree for two days, and the hand-written gate did not name its file. Proven by extracting the tree at the breaking commit and running that one test — AssertionError: uncounted states: ['unreadable']. The guard existed, was right, and was never reached, which is the same defect this plan documents in the display and the parser, produced here by the orchestrator rather than by the code. Asserted by requiring the gate for a node touching a module to include every test file importing it, and by failing a dispatch whose stated gate omits one.
A budget hold is keyed to the account, not the projectThe same wrong-noun error as the write fence, in a second table, and it makes the fleet under-hold rather than over-hold. Measured 2026-09-03 across four mounted projects on one machine: imas-codex holds claude-opus, reckon holds claude — two backends on one account — and imas-ambix and nova hold nothing at all. So each project that was refused acquired a private, permanent belief about a shared budget, the two that were refused hold different halves of one account's picture, and half the fleet has no belief and will dispatch into a spent account and lose nodes to hard 429s. Whichever project happens to receive a rejection is the only one that learns from it. A backend name is not the key either: claude and claude-opus share an account here while codex-luna, codex-spark and codex-terra share another, and nothing currently records what the account is. Asserted by recording a rejection under one project and requiring a second project's pre-flight on a backend of the same account to see it.
A dead claim is only disregarded when its work is safeAdmitting a second writer over a claim whose process is gone requires that nothing be left unintegrated, not merely that the process ended. Measured 2026-09-03: of six claims held by dead processes, five carried unintegrated work — four with commits beyond base and one with three uncommitted files — so a rule keyed on liveness alone would have admitted writers over all five and left each first worker's commit surviving only as long as its worktree. The predicate is process gone AND no uncommitted change at the declared paths AND no commit beyond base unreachable from the integration branch. Where work exists, refuse and name the run to promote or recover, because a refusal that names the remedy is actionable and a silent admission is not.
A claim does not outlive the run that made itThe second face of the same registry, and it is same-project so the keying bug does not explain it. A write claim is released when its run reaches a terminal state, not only when a coordinator gets round to promoting it. Measured 2026-09-03: three of nine live pointers were held by runs whose process was dead and whose manifest was terminal — two complete, one blocked — holding twelve write paths between them, the oldest dated the previous day. Every one of those paths was unavailable to any other node for as long as its coordinator did not promote, which is unbounded. A peer hit this twice in one session and learned of it only because a worker reported the conflict; nothing refused them. Asserted by finishing a run without promoting it and dispatching a second node on its paths: the second must be allowed, or the refusal must name the terminal run so the operator knows to promote rather than to wait.
Two coordinators both yielding leaves nobody holding the workA first-claim-wins rule needs an arbiter, because mutual deference resolves to zero workers. Measured 2026-09-03: two sessions dispatched overlapping nodes on reckon/budget.py five seconds apart, both were told of the collision, and both stopped in favour of the other. For roughly three minutes a defect that had been latching a lane for seven hours had two workers and then none, and neither session was wrong at the moment it decided. The broker who reports a collision should name who keeps it, or the rule should resolve automatically on a property both sides can read — earliest claim, or the run whose project owns the repository. Recording it because the failure is invisible from inside either session: each believes the other continues.
Write exclusivity is per repository, not per projectTwo runs never hold the same file, whatever project each declares, because the file belongs to a repository and not to a project. Measured 2026-09-03: n-a-spend-hold-expires (project imas-codex) and a-spent-lane-judgement-has-a-shelf-life (project reckon) were dispatched five seconds apart into /home/ITER/mcintos/Code/reckon, both claiming reckon/budget.py and tests/test_budget.py, and both were allowed. The same validator had correctly refused three same-project dispatches on recovery.py earlier that day, so the check works and its key is wrong. Cross-project work into a shared repository is routine here, which makes this the common case rather than the corner. Asserted by dispatching two nodes with different project values and one shared path into one repository: the second must be refused naming the first.
Every state counts somewhere and can wake someoneThe fleet buckets sum to the row count, so a state in the vocabulary is never absent from the counters. Measured 2026-09-03 immediately after adding one: unreadable renders its row correctly and appears in no bucket and in no attention set, so the footer can read 0b · 0u while a run sits needing a person, and an --attention follower is never woken by it. Asserted by summing the buckets against the rows and by enumerating the state vocabulary against the attention set, so a state added to one and not the others fails a test rather than disappearing quietly.
A stored classification carries its own vintageEvery stored state records the moment it was observed, so a reader can tell a current verdict from a stale one without inferring it from what happened to be on screen. Measured 2026-09-03: a stored abandoned from 14:54 sat unchanged behind a row rendering blocked from 15:06, and because neither carried a time, a competent reader ordered them by when they were seen rather than when they were written and diagnosed a decay that ran the opposite way. The reader had even written the mtime-versus-transition discipline for manifests that day and could not apply it, because state records offer no equivalent. Asserted by requiring an observation stamp on the stored state that survives into the transition record, so a label predating a known change is visible as such.
A discarded run is never displayed as promotedRemoving a live pointer without promoting it renders as a discard, not as a landing. Measured 2026-09-03 by accident while correcting a mis-routed dispatch: crew discard on a stopped run wrote no ledger row at all — correctly, since it produced nothing — and the pane rendered its departure as stopped → promoted. The fold infers the terminal label from the pointer disappearing rather than from what disposed of it, so every discarded run reads on the ticker as work that landed. Asserted against a discard and a promotion of the same shape, which must render differently.
Three manifest outcomes, none collapsing into anotherA manifest is absent, readable, or unreadable, and each reaches the operator as its own outcome: absent → abandoned, the worker genuinely died without reporting; readable and terminal → unpromoted, finished and waiting on the orchestrator; present but unreadable → a named refusal that is neither of the other two. Collapsing the third into the second is precisely the defect measured here, and a format fix alone re-creates it the next time a format appears. Note the caller consequence: recovery.py:168 currently catches only OSError, so a new exception type crashes the classifier rather than degrading it — the raise and that catch land together or not at all. Asserted with three fixtures, one per outcome, and a live case for each: n-sign-convention-survives-wrapping died with no manifest and uncommitted work in its worktree (correctly abandoned), against n-codex-isn-pin-0-8-7 and n-catalog-isn-pin-0-8-7, both JSON manifests written 18 and 19 seconds before their runs were labelled abandoned.
A reader refuses rather than half-succeedsA manifest the reader cannot parse raises, naming the path and the format it expected. It never returns a partial mapping. Asserted against a JSON manifest carrying "status": "complete", on which parse_manifest currently returns eight recognised keys and no status — well-formed enough that no caller can distinguish a format mismatch from an author who omitted the field. Measured 2026-09-03: 48 runs across two projects carry a .json manifest_path and every one displays as abandoned from process exit until promotion. Either the reader accepts JSON or dispatch refuses the path; accepting it and misreading it is the one option that must fail.
A reason names its predicate, and is never truncated past itEvery state reason states the specific condition it asserts, and the pane never elides the clause that carries it. Measured 2026-09-03: “the process is gone without a complete manifest” is a testable claim about a named file, while the truncated “the process is gone without a…” reads as a generic liveness remark — an operator ran on the short form for hours, and restoring the sentence produced the diagnosis in one step. Asserted against a reason longer than the render width, which must lose trailing detail rather than the predicate.
One condition has one nameNo two states describe the same run condition. Specifically, complete and completed_unpromoted both mean finished-and-awaiting-promotion, both count into FLEET_UNPROMOTED_STATES, and render as different words — so a footer and a row can describe one run in two vocabularies while the count stays correct. Collapse onto unpromoted, which states what the operator must do rather than what the worker did. Asserted by enumerating the state vocabulary against the display map and the count buckets, so a future state added to one and not the others fails.
A recorded commit is reachable, or is recorded as notPromotion resolves the recorded commit against the integration branch and stores the verdict, never an unchecked commit string. Asserted in both directions: a reachable commit promotes clean, and an unreachable one promotes with the non-reachability stored rather than the promotion refused, because the commit still exists and the worktree must not be released on the strength of an unverified landing.
A time-varying reading carries the time it was takenAny stored reachability verdict is re-derived at the moment it is recorded rather than carried forward from an earlier reading, and is stamped with that moment. Asserted against a wave that moves the integration branch between the check and the write, which is the case that produced a confident wrong answer here with a correctly-aimed instrument.
A durable manifest is not a terminal oneA worker that writes its manifest before starting a long wait — which the durability discipline requires, because a print-mode worker gets no turn after its last one — must be able to say in progress. Today the only non-terminal word available is blocked, so the discipline and the classifier fight each other: measured 2026-09-04 across four of this session's twelve runs, every one of which wrote status: blocked with blockers: implementation and focused gate pending, was classified blocked, emitted a transition carrying that reason to every follower, and was in fact working normally with a log two seconds old. Two of the four then transitioned blocked → working unprompted, and one blocked → promoted. The cost is not cosmetic: an orchestrator reading the ticker cannot distinguish a placeholder from a worker that has genuinely stopped and needs answering, which is the one distinction the blocked bucket exists to make. Asserted by requiring a placeholder manifest — non-terminal status, no commits, no completed gate — to classify as working and to count in the working bucket, while a manifest whose blockers name a condition the worker cannot resolve alone still classifies blocked and still wakes an --attention follower.
A fence names the population it coversA write fence protects against the actor it enumerates and no other, and it says which that is. Contributed 2026-09-05 by the imas-ambix session from three measured instances, all surfacing as a refused merge rather than as an alert. Twice a tool inside a worktree resolved a path into the shared checkout — two unrelated nodes, neither declaring the main checkout, both running a formatter, diffs of 51 and 106 lines and both AST-identical to HEAD; once a coordinator hand-edited a file a live run held as its exclusive scope. The existing per-repository measure assumes the writers are runs; the writer set is larger in two directions, downward into the tools a run invokes and sideways to sessions that never dispatched. The formatter pair is the stronger case because nothing in the dispatch contract could prevent it — a worktree is a directory, not a jail. And the hazard is not the formatting: formatting announces itself, while a semantic edit by the same route would not, leaving a coordinator to integrate one state while the shared tree held another. Asserted three ways: git -C <main-checkout> status --porcelain is empty at the moment a coordinator merges a worker commit, which both formatter instances would have failed; a stray path is classified by AST identity against HEAD rather than by diff size or by resembling formatting, since a semantic edit can be two lines; and the scope validator states its own population in its output, so a reader sees it covers runs and not hand edits rather than inferring a guarantee it does not make. The mechanism is general and the rate is not established — two tool escapes and one hand edit, one day, one project — so no frequency claim belongs here.

§5 — A spent lane should be known before dispatch, and should hand over rather than refuse

The exhaustion was visible all along and nothing asked. On 2026-09-03 the codex account hit its usage limit with a reset eight days out. Every unnamed dispatch kept resolving to it, because default_backend named it, and crew preflight reported it clear. The cause is one missing flag: budget_check was set on codex-spark, codex-luna and codex-terra and not on codex — the highest-volume lane in the fleet at 372 promoted runs, and the one backend a pre-flight could not probe. Without it a pre-flight can only believe what earlier runs recorded, and no run had yet recorded the refusal because the parser that reads a usage-limit event into a budget signal landed afterwards. Enabling the flag changed the answer immediately: the same pre-flight now returns held: true naming codex.

That fixes visibility and leaves the second half open. Today a held backend refuses: crew preflight exits 3 and crew dispatch returns budget-hold. Refusing is correct when a human is watching and wrong when the point is unattended execution — the fleet stops on a condition the configuration could have routed around, and a person has to notice and edit default_backend by hand, which is what happened here and cost most of a session's dispatches.

Let a lane declare where its work goes when it is spent. An ordered fallback, resolved at dispatch when the primary is held, with three properties that keep it from becoming the next silent wrong answer:

  • The substitution is recorded on the run, naming the lane asked for, the lane used, and the hold that caused it — so a calibration slice never attributes a fallback run to the backend the caller named. This is the same reasoning that made a shadow record its substituted configuration.
  • A fallback is declared, never inferred. A backend with no declared fallback still refuses, because guessing a substitute is how a metered account gets spent by a caller who never chose it.
  • Unknown never triggers it. A backend reporting no headroom is not held and therefore never falls back, preserving the rule that absence of a signal is not evidence of exhaustion.

Two related gaps belong with it. roles.<role>.by_spec_level can set effort but nothing maps a spec level to a backend for the Claude lanes, so "route an open node to the frontier tier" is expressible only as a per-call --set — a rule with no bound check, which is this sprint's own thesis. And the host layer briefly carried two claude-opus definitions, added independently by two sessions; YAML takes the last key silently, which dropped budget_check and left the frontier tier with no headroom visibility at all. Nothing validated the collision.

MeasureRequired evidence
A spent lane is known before dispatchEvery metered CLI backend declares budget_check, and a pre-flight against a spent account returns held naming it, its utilisation and its reset. Asserted against a recorded account surface rather than a live one. Necessary and, as written, not sufficient — see the two rows below, added 2026-09-03 after this measure passed while the account was spent.
A spend ceiling is a different surface from a rate limitAn account whose spend ceiling is reached, with rate-limit utilisation still low, returns held. Measured 2026-09-03: budget_check was declared on the claude lane and the pre-flight reported utilisation 1.21% against a 95% ceiling and the lane clear, four minutes before five workers on it died on an individual spend limit. The utilisation figure was not wrong; it was answering a different question, and nothing asked the one that mattered. Asserted against a recorded stream carrying the spend-limit event, with the rate-limit figure held low so the test fails if the check keys on utilisation alone.
A refusal is never recorded as a successA worker stream whose terminal event is type: result, subtype: success while its payload carries a provider refusal is classified as an infrastructure failure, not a completion. Measured 2026-09-03 on all five runs: the harness reported success and the refusal text sat in the result field, so no detector fired and the fleet displayed them as working. Asserted against the five recorded streams, and as a negative against a genuine success carrying no refusal text.
A held lane hands overA dispatch whose resolved backend is held and which declares a fallback runs on the fallback and records the lane asked for, the lane used and the hold; one with no declared fallback still refuses at exit 3; and a backend reporting unknown headroom neither holds nor falls back. Asserted by test in all three cases.
Spec level can name a backendA by_spec_level entry resolves a backend as well as an effort, validated through the layered schema with provenance, so routing an open node to the frontier tier is configuration rather than a per-call override.
A duplicate backend key is refusedA layer defining the same backend twice is refused at validation naming the key, rather than silently taking the last and dropping whatever the earlier definition carried.
An inferred hold carries its own expiryA hold derived from a worker's 429 parses the reset clause out of the rejection text into resets_at and expires on it, exactly as one read from an account surface does. Asserted against a recorded rejection carrying your session limit resets 3:40pm (Europe/Paris), and as a negative against a rejection stating no reset, which must record the absence rather than a guess. The failure this prevents is not only a fence that never lifts: with resets_at null a live hold and a stale one are indistinguishable, and on 2026-09-03 a four-minute-old hold was read as a stale latch and nearly lifted into a lane denied for another three and a half hours.
The fence reads the window figure, not the account figureA hold is derived from unifiedWindows.<period>.utilization against that window's own resetsAt, never from the top-level overage record. One stream carries both and they say opposite things: measured 2026-09-03 on a serving lane, overage read utilization 1.21, surpassedThreshold 1 while unifiedWindows.five_hour read 0.05, and the lane was 54 turns in with zero rejections. The overage counter is the account's calendar-month position — its resetsAt of 1790812800 is 2026-10-01 00:00 UTC, a month boundary — so fencing on it would hold every wave for the rest of September while the gating window sat at five percent. This supersedes the earlier obligation in this section to hold on an over-line overage warning, which was written before both figures had been read side by side. Asserted against a recorded stream carrying both, so a check keying on the account figure fails.
Pre-flight and dispatch cannot disagreeBoth surfaces answer from the same records, so a lane dispatch would refuse is never reported clear. Asserted against a state file carrying an open hold: pre-flight must name that backend in held_backends rather than returning it empty while enumerating a different backend from a record weeks older, and must report the same effective ceiling the refusal cites. Measured 2026-09-03 failing on all three counts at once — held: false with four open holds in the file it named, and a policy ceiling of 100.0 against a refusal citing 95.0.

§6 — A spend limit is a block, because a block is the only state that can be triaged

Directed by the lead on 2026-09-03, immediately after the outage recorded in §4 and §5. A provider refusal must surface as blocked. Not as a completion, which is what the harness reports and what let five deaths pass unnoticed for ten minutes. And not as a failure, which is what the coordinator had to reconcile them as, because it was the only reconciliation available.

Blocked is not a nicer word for failed; it is the state with the affordances the situation needs. Three of them, and no other state has all three. It reaches the coordinator, because blocked is a bucket the follower prints while a failure at starting is not. It is resumable in the same session, so the worker keeps the context it had when it stopped. And it holds a reason, which for this condition is an unusually good one: the account, the limit kind, and a reset timestamp. A spend limit is the most triageable stop a fleet can suffer — it says exactly what is wrong and exactly when it stops being wrong — and the fleet currently converts it into the least triageable thing there is.

Phase and failure classification are different axes, and this needs both. Classifying the refusal as an infrastructure failure is correct for the ledger: it keeps the model's pass rate clean, which is the rule the harness section already states. But classification is what the record charges at reconciliation, not what the coordinator sees while the condition holds. Recording infrastructure-failure and showing blocked are complementary, and today only the first exists.

What it cost, measured on one run of the five. The merged-head verification node had already launched both suite runs and was waiting on them; its manifest read in-progress and its run directory held two suite logs, an attribution map naming the three code-bearing candidate commits, and per-test ledgers at both revisions. It hit the limit three times, said it would wait for the settle notification rather than poll further, and stopped. Killed rather than blocked, none of that could be resumed: the artifacts survived only because they had been written incrementally, and a fresh node had to be handed them as a lead to re-confirm rather than a result to use. Four other runs were redispatched from scratch for the same reason.

The coupling with clive-local-workers §14 is not incidental. That run was waiting on test suites, generating almost nothing, and hit a spend ceiling anyway. A budget denominated in generation would not have charged it for the wait, and a refusal that blocks rather than kills would have let it collect the result it was minutes away from. The two changes address the same waste from opposite ends.

MeasureRequired evidence
A refusal blocks, and does not fail or completeA run whose stream carries a provider spend, usage or quota refusal is classified blocked, appears in the follower's blocked count, and is classified neither complete nor abandoned. Asserted against the five streams recorded 2026-09-03, and as a negative against a genuine completion and a genuine crash, neither of which may become blocked.
The block states what to do and whenThe blocked reason names the backend, the limit kind and the reset timestamp read from the refusal, so the triage decision needs nothing beyond the transition line. Asserted against a recorded refusal carrying a reset, and against one carrying none, where the reason states the reset is unknown rather than omitting the field.
The block is resumable once the limit liftsA run blocked on a refusal is resumable in its own session after the reset, keeping its captured session id, and the resumed attempt is recorded as a resume rather than a fresh attempt. Asserted by test through the resume path.
The ledger still charges it to infrastructureWhether promoted from blocked or after a resume, a refusal-caused stop is recorded as an infrastructure failure and excluded from the configuration's pass rate. Asserted against the capabilities slice for the affected configuration.

§7 — Health is read from work, probed when stale, and the probe writes back

Directed by the lead on 2026-09-03, after this session held a wave for four hours on a reset the provider stated and did not honour. Two questions: can health be known without hitting the endpoint, and should a probe update the log rather than be a separate reading someone has to remember to take. Yes to both, and the answer is a ladder rather than one instrument, because the rungs answer different questions.

Rung one is free and already flowing: every served request returns rate-limit telemetry as a byproduct. A fleet doing work continuously reports its own headroom — utilisation, the window, and whether the request was served, warned or rejected — without a single extra call. This is the non-endpoint check, and for the routine case it beats a probe precisely because nobody has to decide to take it. What is missing is not the signal but its age: the budget record keeps the reading and not the moment, so a thirty-minute-old rejection and a five-second-old one are indistinguishable to the reader.

Rung two costs no tokens and is already declared in the schema, for nothing. BackendConfig.auth_check exists as an argument vector run to test whether a backend is authenticated, as an exit status — described in the schema as how a provider-specific credential check reaches reckon without reckon knowing any provider. All nine backends report no auth_check declared. Declaring it gives a credential check for free. It must not be mistaken for a quota check: an authenticated account can be spend-limited, so this rung answers can I reach the provider and never will it serve me.

Rung three is a one-token request, and it is the only rung that answers the question a dispatch actually asks. Measured: four output tokens, under half a cent, a definitive verdict. Cheap enough to use freely, and still not the routine path, because rung one already covers the routine case at zero cost. A probe is the tie-breaker for when the log is too old to trust and a decision turns on it.

The recorded refusal is rung zero, and it is authoritative only about the past. A refusal proves a lane was spent when it was recorded. Its stated reset is a claim about the future, and this session measured one wrong by three and a half hours. Holding until a stated reset is therefore not a recovery strategy; probing on an interval is.

What ties the ladder together is that every rung writes to one log. The budget record is the single thing the pre-flight reads, and today only run streams write it. A probe that reports to its caller and not to the record leaves the next reader to probe again, which is how a cheap check becomes an expensive habit. So a probe's result lands in the same record with its own timestamp and provenance, and the pre-flight's answer names which rung it came from and how old it is.

MeasureRequired evidence
Every reading carries its age and its rungThe budget record stores the observation moment and which rung produced it — byproduct telemetry, credential check, probe, or recorded refusal — and the pre-flight reports both, so spent is never displayed without as of when. Asserted by test against a record carrying readings of differing ages.
A stale hold is re-derived, not obeyedA hold whose newest evidence is older than a declared staleness bound is not returned as held; it triggers a probe, and the probe's verdict decides. Asserted in both directions: a fresh rejection still holds, and a rejection older than the bound against a serving lane does not. This is the measure that would have prevented the four-hour hold.
A probe writes backA probe's result is persisted to the budget record with its timestamp and rung, so a second reader in the same window reads the record rather than probing again. Asserted by a test that probes once and shows the second read served from the record.
The credential rung is real and is not the quota rungauth_check is declared for at least the metered CLI backends and its exit status is reported by availability; a backend that is authenticated but spend-limited reports authenticated and held together, never authenticated alone. Asserted by test, since conflating the two is how a reachable provider would read as a serving one.

§8 — A transition names the label it last emitted, not the state it was in

Every run's first transition is labelled dispatched regardless of what it actually was. The arrival branch of the transition fold appends {**current[run_id], "state": "dispatched"} as a literal, discarding whatever the snapshot computed; subsequent polls then compare against that stored label. So from_state is the last emitted label rather than the state at evaluation, and a pane reading dispatched → X learns nothing about the phase the run was in when X was decided.

This is not cosmetic, because it makes the ticker unusable as evidence about the detector itself. A peer session reasoned that a dispatched → stalled event proved the pre-fix stall gate could evaluate a dispatched run, and therefore that widening the gate was not the fix for their case. The event is in fact fully reachable under the old gate: the run arrives and is labelled dispatched, its phase later advances to working so the state == "working" gate does fire, the quiet exceeds the window, and the transition is emitted with the stale label as its origin. The inference was sound given what the pane shows and wrong given what the code does, which is the defect — a display that cannot be reasoned from is worse than one that is merely sparse.

The same event carried a second, independent defect. That run was not stalled; it was finished, with a complete manifest, a landed commit and a clean worktree, and the last assistant turn 914 seconds before the event. The detector read the quiet correctly and then chose the wrong verdict, because it consulted elapsed silence and not the terminal manifest sitting beside it. Terminal manifests are checked before the stall gate in the current code, so this needs its own reproduction: either the manifest was unreadable to the classifier at that moment, or the ordering does not hold on the path the producer takes. Reproduce before repairing.

MeasureRequired evidence
An origin state is realA transition's from_state is the state the run was actually in at the previous evaluation, so a first transition from a run already working reports working rather than dispatched. Asserted against a fold whose first observation is a mid-flight run.
A terminal manifest outranks silenceA run whose manifest is terminal is never reported stalled, whatever its quiet interval. Asserted by reproducing the recorded case first, then fixing it.

§9 — Nine commits are reachable, unlanded, and owned by nobody

Cleanup that gates on reachability alone looks safe and is not. A worktree's HEAD is an ancestor of the primary branch whenever the worker committed and whenever it committed nothing, because the base revision it was created at is an ancestor too. So the check that reads as “this work is landed” is satisfied identically by work that never started, and a sweep trusting it deletes the difference without reporting anything. The predicate is the same class of error as a guard trusting a registry over the machine: a true statement standing in for the one that was needed.

The pair that actually sees the difference is a clean working tree and a nonempty git log main..HEAD. Measured against every worktree of this repository, that pair isolates nine commits across seven trees, none of whose content is on the primary branch:

WorktreeCommits aheadLargest known delta
model-and-effort-are-separate-columns1 267 insertions, 86 deletions across the ticker, recovery and a layout test
liveness-is-derived-not-remembered1 69 insertions, 31 deletions in the run reader
the-transition-carries-the-role-it-renders1 34 insertions in recovery and its test
a-spent-lane-judgement-has-a-shelf-life1 25 insertions in the budget reader
the-working-bucket-counts-only-work1 8 insertions, 6 deletions in recovery
the-promotion-gate-reads-the-attribution1 5 insertions adding a failure-attribution field
n-after-capture-evidence1not yet audited
session-keying-test-expectation2not yet audited

Every session that produced them has exited, so no coordinator is holding this work and no transfer is pending. That removes the ownership question and leaves only the engineering one, which is why it belongs here as a section rather than as a followup waiting on somebody. It also sets the order: the deltas must be read before any of them is landed and before any sweep is allowed near those trees, because the worktree is currently the only copy of each. The three shadow- trees are dirty by construction and carry nothing landable; they are not in scope.

A retained delta is not evidence that it should land. Some of these were superseded by later work on the primary branch, and a delta whose surface has since moved is a repair to re-derive rather than a patch to apply. So the audit reports a recommendation per commit and the landing beat honours it per commit; nothing here is merged as a batch on the strength of the table above.

One correction to this section, recorded because the mistake was the section's own to make. Running the clean-and-ahead pair by hand during a live wave reported four in-flight worktrees as reclaimable: each was clean and at a revision the primary branch already held, because its worker had not committed yet. That is the same trap one step further along — a tree is clean and reachable both when the work landed and when the work has not started. A hand-rolled predicate carrying only those two conditions would have deleted four live workers' trees.

The production scan is not that predicate and does not have that defect: it consults the live claims and withheld all five running trees. The predicate this section asks for therefore needs three conditions rather than two, and the third one already exists. What the scan does not do is say why it withheld: every withheld row carried an empty reason, so a reader sees a refusal with no cause and cannot tell a live run from an unintegrated commit from a dirty tree. That is the gap worth closing here, and it is the same shape as a refusal that names no verb.

Done when a report names, for each of the nine commits, its subject, its changed files, its insertion and deletion counts, whether a probe line unique to the delta is absent from the primary branch, and a recommendation of land, re-derive, or discard with the reason; when the documented predicate for worktree cleanup carries all three conditions, so a sweep refuses a tree that is clean and ahead, and refuses one whose run is still live, rather than removing either; when every withheld row names which condition withheld it; and when each commit recommended for landing has been landed under its own audit and gate, or recorded as re-derive or discard with that reason on this section. Asserted by the report existing with nine rows and a recommendation in every one, by a cleanup dry run naming every clean-and-ahead tree as retained rather than disposable, and by this section carrying a disposition for all nine.

§9b — A coordinator lost two finished pieces in its own fleet, because no surface reports unlanded work

§9 says unlanded commits are owned by nobody. Measured again on 2026-09-21, from the coordinator's side rather than the sweep's, and the point is that the person who dispatched the work could not see it either.

A session ran twelve nodes. Two delivered, committed and were never merged — review-gate-covers-every-writing-run (b650bbec, 16 test functions, 22 cases, 0 failures) and prescription-checks-refuse-what-they-were-passing (3b7dbf66). Both were work the lead had asked for by name. The coordinator found them only when asked for a status report, roughly two hours after they landed.

Nothing lied; every surface answered a different question.

What each surface said about a delivered, unmerged run.
SurfaceReportedQuestion it answers
crew list / view="live"scoringis a review attached — not is the work safe
the follower's ticker→ unpromoteda transition happened
the posture linecounts by phasehow many are in each bucket
anything at allnothingis this commit on the primary branch

So the gap is not that the fleet view is wrong, but that merge state is absent from it. A run's classification describes its review lifecycle; a coordinator reading scoring learns nothing about whether the diff exists anywhere but a worktree. That is this plan's own class — a true statement standing in for the one that was needed — and it is the reason §9's nine commits accumulated without anyone choosing to let them.

The fact is cheap and already computed elsewhere. §9 names the predicate: a clean working tree plus a nonempty git log main..HEAD. The same pair answers the coordinator's question, and the run record already carries the commit and the worktree path.

Done when the live view reports, per run, whether its recorded commits are reachable from the project's primary branch, asserted for the three states that differ — delivered and merged, delivered and unmerged, delivered with no commit at all — because the third is the one §9 shows is indistinguishable today; the follower's posture line carries an unlanded count beside its working, blocked and unpromoted buckets, asserted to be non-zero against a synthesised fleet holding one unmerged delivery; and a session ending with unlanded commits in its own worktrees cannot report a clean close without naming them, asserted, since that is the moment the loss becomes permanent.

§10 — A gate can be recorded as passed beside a log that failed

Promotion asks for a gate command, an exit status and a log, and checks none of them against each other. Read at the promotion path: the evidence requirement resolves the commits a run presents and returns rather than raising on a shortfall, deliberately, so a passing verdict is never turned into an exception. Nothing anywhere compares the log's contents with the command filed beside it, or with the exit status asserted for it.

So the ledger can carry a row whose verdict and whose evidence contradict each other, and the row is the durable artifact everything downstream trusts. Two instances are on record. In one, a coordinator attached the wrong log to a run and had to correct a committed row in prose afterwards. In the other, a run was promoted with an asserted exit status of zero beside a log whose entire content is a command-not-found error at exit two — naming a subcommand that does not exist, while the two real entry points both do. Neither was caught by the gate; both were caught by a person reading afterwards.

This is the plan's own subject arriving in the record rather than the display. A pane that lies can be re-read; a committed ledger row that lies is what a later reader reasons from, and calibration, routing and every rework figure in this project are computed from these rows. A verdict whose own evidence refutes it is worse than a missing verdict, because a gap invites a question and a contradiction answers it.

The check is cheap because the material is already in hand at promotion: the command, the claimed status, and the log. What it must not do is re-run anything or parse test output formats, both of which would make promotion depend on the shape of every runner this fleet uses.

Done when a promotion asserting a passing gate is refused if the log it cites is empty, or if the log's own recorded exit status contradicts the asserted one, or if the log contains no evidence of the command having run at all; when the refusal names which of those three it found and the resolving verb; when a promotion whose log and command agree is unchanged; when a non-passing verdict is unaffected, since it already carries its failure; and when the check reads the log as text without parsing any runner's result format. Asserted by promoting the two recorded contradictory pairs and requiring a refusal for each, and by promoting an agreeing pair and requiring it to land unchanged.

§11 — A refusal names the allowlist it checked as if it were the repository

The scope guard for a read-only role builds a list of delivery roots — the run directory, the reports directory, and an absolute manifest path — and treats every other absolute path as a repository path. The refusal it then emits says those paths “resolve inside the repository”. For a path that genuinely is outside the repository and outside the delivery roots, that sentence is false.

Measured 2026-09-09 while dispatching a verification node: a report path under the session's own scratch directory was refused with that wording, and the path is not inside the repository by the same containment test the code elsewhere uses — it simply is not one of the three delivery roots. The directory's name happens to encode the repository's path as a flattened string, which is what makes the message read plausibly.

The decision is right and only the sentence is wrong, which is what makes this worth writing down rather than arguing about. A verifier should write to its delivery roots and nowhere else, so refusing was correct; the refusal asserts a fact it never checked instead of the one it did. That is the third instance in one session of a guard reporting “absent from my list” as “is the thing my list is about”, after a lane fence and a worktree predicate, and this plan exists because that shape keeps arriving in new places.

Done when the refusal for a role that may not write repository paths names what it actually established — that the declared path is not one of the delivery roots this role may write — and lists those roots, so a caller can correct the path in one edit; when a declared path that IS inside the repository still says so, since that case is real and more serious; and when the two cases are distinguishable in the message. Asserted by declaring a path outside both the repository and the delivery roots and requiring the first wording, and a path inside the repository and requiring the second.

Landed 2026-09-25. The defect was reproduced before it was repaired, in a scratch tree cut from the dispatch base: a declared path under a scratch directory is not inside the repository (inside cwd repo : False) while the refusal asserts the opposite (asserts repo : True).

The refusal is now composed from the check that was made rather than from a conclusion about the path. write_scope_refusal in reckon/crew/node.py classifies every declared path as admitted by a delivery root, inside the repository, or outside both, and each refused class carries its own clause: a path inside the repository still reads resolves inside the repository <root>, and a path outside both reads is outside the repository <root> and outside every delivery root this role may write, with the roots enumerated — the run directory, the reports directory and the node's own manifest — followed by the correction they allow. A declaration mixing the two classes carries both clauses rather than being collapsed onto whichever check ran first. The repository a path is judged against comes from the dispatch's own working directory through repository_identity, which collapses a linked worktree onto the repository it was cut from, so two spellings of one repository cannot read as two.

Declared pathRefusal beforeRefusal after
inside the repository role 'test' may not write repository paths, but <path> resolve inside the repository; a verifier writes only its manifest, report and logs, which live outside it — dispatch an implement node for source edits — one sentence for both rows, so the two cases were indistinguishable <path> resolves inside the repository <root>, unchanged, and the root it was checked against is now named in it
outside the repository and outside every delivery root <path> is outside the repository <root> and outside every delivery root this role may write (<runs>, <reports>, <manifest>); declare the path under one of those roots, or dispatch an implement node when the work changes the worktree

The wider cohort including the new file is 41 passed. The new check drives both cases through validate_node and plan_dispatch and reads the wording out of the emitted message rather than a constant, and a synthesised repository plus a synthesised role home are asserted untouched against the real config home. The declared negative control — restoring the single sentence — reddens test_a_path_outside_the_repository_names_the_roots_checked at its first assertion, the one requiring the enumerated roots, with the source restored byte-for-byte afterwards. Landed under commit 27311d59. Full record: §11 landed.

Leaves behind. Whether the merged result holds belongs to a separately dispatched verification node, and the section's status and driving followup are closed by the coordinator rather than by this node.

§12 — Two fields on one manifest line disarm the guard that reads them

The manifest format is one key per line, and a worker that writes two on one line is not refused — it is misparsed, in the direction that removes a protection. Measured 2026-09-09 against the live parser:

WrittenParsed commitsParsed changed paths
the two keys on separate linesthe shaboth paths
the two keys on one line, semicolon separated one corrupt string carrying the rest of the lineempty

The empty half is what matters. The promotion guard that refuses a completed manifest reporting changed paths with no commit fires only when changed paths are present, so a manifest written on one line presents none, and the guard built this session to catch a worker that committed without recording it does not fire at all. A corrupted commit value travels alongside, and it is not a valid object id, so nothing downstream that resolves it would succeed either — but nothing downstream is asked to, because the coordinator supplies the sha explicitly at promotion.

Three of this session's workers wrote the one-line form. Every ledger row is correct regardless, because each promotion named its commit on the command line and the recorded sha was verified against the worktree head before merging. That is exactly what makes this worth a section rather than a note: the protection was absent for three landings and nothing reported it, and the reason no harm followed is a coordinator habit rather than a mechanism.

Two candidate repairs and they are not equivalent. Parsing the second key would make the malformed line work, which teaches the format to accept a shape it does not document. Refusing the line names the defect where it is introduced, at the cost of a refusal a worker meets mid-run. The stronger reason to refuse is the one this plan keeps meeting: a manifest that parses into a wrong value is a confident wrong answer, and one that refuses is an honest absence.

Done when a manifest line carrying a second key after the first value is reported rather than silently misparsed, naming the line and the keys it found; when the promotion guard for changed paths without a commit cannot be disarmed by the field it reads being misparsed to empty, proven by feeding it the one-line form and requiring a refusal rather than a skip; and when the separate-line form is unaffected. Asserted by parsing both forms and requiring the malformed one to be reported, and by promoting a run whose manifest carries the one-line form and requiring the guard to speak.

A manifest line carrying a second key after its value is now refused rather than misparsed. The tolerant reader captured the whole remainder of the line as the first key’s value, so two keys on one line — the form three of this section’s workers actually wrote — turned the earlier value into a corrupted string and left the later field an empty list; that empty half is what disarmed the changed-paths-without-commit guard. The refusal names the line and both keys it found, and leaves two safe shapes untouched: a value that is itself a JSON literal (a structured evidence block) is read whole because the keys inside it are nested data, not a second top-level field, and only the manifest vocabulary is matched so a URL scheme or prose colon cannot raise. The promotion guard itself still swallows a parse refusal as a skip; making it speak is a promotion-layer change carried as a follow-on. Landed under commit 9a2f95e. Full record: §12 landed.

The template-side half of this section is landed; the reader half is the separate node a-second-key-on-a-manifest-line-is-reported and must not merge alone, since a prompt that instructs workers to write one-line forms after the parser refuses them is worse than today. The manifest block the worker prompt hands out now matches the schema the reader enforces: commits and changed_paths sit on separate lines and are never semicolon-joined on one, the status vocabulary offers waiting alongside complete, blocked and failed, and the block carries wait_condition, wait_probe, wait_terminal and resume_brief. The time fence names the same four keys, so the prose instructing a worker to leave a record of what it waits on and the block that makes the record machine-readable no longer disagree. Asserted in tests/test_worker_prompt_contract.py against the composed prompt, with the existing prompt-composition assertions passing unchanged. Landed under commit 8417c0f. Asserted by two cases in tests/test_crew_prompts.py that fail at base and pass at the commit. Landed under commit 9c51724b. Full record: §12 wait fields landed.

Of the 27, 27 now parse and none still refuses. The prescribed five-file gate reads 180 passed with the base reader and 187 passed, 0 failed at the commit, and the section adds no lint findings. Landed under commit 52c04865. Full record: §12 reading-what-the-old-reader-read evidence.

One extraction now answers which watch rows are real arrivals. Filed here at the dispatch’s stated coordinate, and the coordinate does not match the section: this section is authored about manifest lines, and the landed work is a canonical arrival filter over the crew watch streams — recorded plainly so the coordinator can re-home the record rather than read it as evidence about the manifest reader. The work itself: extract_watch_arrivals in reckon/crew/query.py classifies every row as an arrival, a state change or re-inventory, keyed on first appearance per node rather than on event class, and both of the filters fleets wrote by hand are wrong against the live corpus — keeping only event == "transition" re-dates 2,417 of the 3,031 nodes whose first row is a baseline with a null from_state and loses 23 outright, and keeping every baseline row invents 204 arrivals out of that same baseline population because a re-attaching follower re-inventories the fleet in one instant (figures re-derived from the function's own output at 2026-09-18T09:26Z, over 12,446 rows; the earlier 2,317 conflated the re-dated population with the lost one). The burst test that separates those from a genuine same-second wave is explicit, since a node whose only row is a re-inventory has no earlier row to fail a node-level rule. Stamps are the other half and are never assumed: JSON rows return UTC, and 2,314 rendered ticker rows return observed_at_zone: unknown rather than a local wall clock read as UTC. Rendered arrivals entered the extraction's range after review found the function dropped the row it exists to find: a rendered row whose only token left of the arrow is the node carries no source state, and 656 such rows are on disk, 614 of them a node's first row. No command is wired, and the fixture shapes are the corpus's own: the only rendered shapes on disk are an arrow with one token to its left and an arrow with two. Landed under commit d9e18423, fix under f17ab71c; 9 new tests pass and the three prescribed sibling suites are unchanged at 21, with zero added failures against base ee356a93, where the new file fails collection with ImportError. Full record and figure: arrivals extraction landed.

A manifest that carries the wait fields with nothing to wait in them is now read as holding no wait, so a healthy worker stops being escalated on the presence of a field alone. The classifier read the fields’ existence as a declared wait, which put working runs in the waiting column and offered them to the resume sweep: a sweep probe of ["true"] prints nothing, so the observation falls back to ["exit:0"], matches the declared terminal exit:0, and resumes a live worker. Seven recorded field sets from live runs are the falsifier, all written by workers that were healthy with streams seconds old — a condition opening none, a note that the wait is not yet set, a condition stating in plain English that no external condition is awaited (written deliberately as a durable checkpoint before a long compose step, which removing the fields from the stub cannot reach), the ledger’s own no external resource is awaited … (the condition the sweep actually acted on, resuming a worker that was editing its worktree), and two ["true"]/exit:0 pairs around notes about where the worker was. The condition’s own prose and a probe that cannot report a pending state each end the declaration. The second correction is a clock: a declared wait no longer ages while the run is producing output, because one run was escalated waiting → wait-aged at 1804 s while its newest stream file was zero minutes old and still growing, which is why a coordinator had to take a second reading before believing a wait-aged row. The age is now measured from the later of the declaration and the last write to the run’s stream, so a run that is demonstrably producing output never ages, while the same declaration over a stream quiet for its declared horizon still ages on schedule — the negative half is asserted rather than assumed. The orientation write is the other half: the prompt names its first three lines as an orientation write carrying a checkpoint, scoped to not-waiting, and each wait field is offered only when an external condition is actually awaited, so the invitation is withdrawn while the fields stay available for a wait actually held. The falsifier is tests/test_wait_marker_is_not_a_stub.py: at base 30 failed, 7 passed of 37, and the seven are the negative half, the two overshoot controls, the checkpoint-scope guard, and the contrast case in which the same declaration over a quiet stream still ages — which is the shape that shows the repair neither overshoots nor switches the clock off. The declared cohort of five files moves from 1 failed, 125 passed at base to 1 failed, 162 passed, the same single test_refusal_recovery_remedy_survives_promotion at both, so zero added. Landed across commits de8b5e8d and 750bc474 and 0d734fb8.

The declared-wait ledger row for the resumed worker is worth its own sentence: {"checked": 2, "resumed": 1} in ~/.config/reckon/crew/recovery/imas-efit.jsonl, on a condition reading no external resource is awaited, observed exit:0, while the worker held its worktree and was editing it, so a reader who took the pane’s wait-aged at face value was reading a run that was working.

The recorded field sets and both readings of them are recorded in full, with the commits and the gate logs, at §12 the interim wait fields.

§13 — A quiet fleet and a working one look identical, because nothing reports a wave

Directed by the lead 2026-09-09, after a session spent counting completions by hand. Every signal this surface emits is per-run. The follower reports one line per transition and trails each with a Nw·Nb·Nu count, so the information that a wave has drained is present in the stream and belongs to no one: a coordinator learns its wave finished by remembering how many nodes it dispatched and counting promotions until the numbers agree. That is a rule enforced by recollection, which is the failure this plan is named for — the display does not lie here, it declines to speak.

Measured on one session, 2026-09-09. Seven runs across two waves. The drain of the first was visible only as 0w·0b·0u on the trailing counter of an unrelated run's promotion line. Two of the seven — both shadows — reached no follower at all: crew shadow accepts no --session, so each minted a synthetic session id and read mine: false, and the third had to be waited on with a hand-rolled loop against its manifest file. So the qualification work this workstation does most of is the work its monitor cannot see.

A wave is a type, and its membership is open. Not a set declared at dispatch: a wave accretes while the ready queue keeps producing independent nodes, then drains. A closed set would reintroduce the barrier the ship contract explicitly forbids — a wave is a reporting snapshot of the active fleet, never a wait-for-everyone gate — because a node that becomes ready after the wave opened would need either a second wave, fragmenting the signal, or a wait. Measured in the same session: a design-pair shadow was added twenty minutes after its siblings, since the node it pairs against had to land first to become the pair's other arm. Under closed membership that is three waves for one push.

Open membership is why closure needs a lifecycle rather than a counter.

StateAdmits membersWhat zero-working means
openyesquiet, may still grow — not a completion
sealednoevery remaining member is in flight; closure is now derivable
closednoall members terminal — the signal fires here

The coordinator seals, because only the coordinator knows whether more nodes are coming, and the beat it already runs to seal is the session-closure fence. Without the sealed state, zero working is ambiguous in exactly the way a baseline row is mistaken for a transition: the same reading carries two meanings and nothing distinguishes them. Note that the boundary already exists unnamed — the producer's seat is released when a wave drains and a fresh one is armed for the next — so this names a thing the implementation already tracks implicitly.

Unstaffed by default; an agent is an upgrade. The derived signal must work with nobody assigned, or it costs a worker to learn that work finished and will not be armed. Staffed, the wave takes a summarizer whose single job is to compile the receipts its members already wrote — manifest status, commits, tests, baseline_suite and after_suite, failure_attribution, artifacts, evidence_inputs, follow_ons, blockers, and from the committed row gate_check, throughput, worker_seconds, tests_added and no_commit. Nothing needs instrumenting; the fields exist and are read by hand today.

The highest-value field to compile is follow_ons. The closure contract requires every followup and every manifest follow-on to be enumerated and given a disposition from a closed set, and a row with no disposition is an unfinished drain. That enumeration is the most mechanical part of closure and the part where a missed row silently hides work — roughly a dozen entries were collected by hand in one session. A staffed wave's natural deliverable is therefore the drain queue itself, not prose about it.

A wave id on the committed row makes wave cost measurable. Seven runs from one session are seven independent ledger rows with no grouping, so no one can ask what a wave cost, how many nodes a coordinator needed, or how much of a window a push consumed — which is the question the cost-per-durable-node work is trying to answer about burn. The grouping is a field, not an analysis.

The hazard, written down before the feature exists. A summarizer must never become a promoter. What caught real defects in the measured session was adversarial reading, not summarizing: a configuration key fragmented by a display field, and a worker's choice between two comparators that flipped 1.4× slower into no slower at all. A wave overview that reads plausible is precisely what would license skipping that read, which is the same shape as a passing gate written by the worker being judged. The summary compiles receipts and states the disposition split; the decision stays with the coordinator.

A closure signal that only looks backward reads as completion. Every measure below describes the wave's own members. Run that against the halt this section was directed after: at 06:08:30Z a coordinator landed a wave, wrote a full account of it, and its turn ended; the next event in that session is the lead asking why implementation had stopped, 70 minutes and 48 seconds later, with nine plans pending. Its wave was genuinely complete — every member promoted, none unpromoted, none blocked — so the disposition split reports it complete and is right to. The wave was never the ambiguous thing. The remainder was.

So the closing line carries what remains beside what landed, and the figure must differ before and after the wave. That falsifier is the whole of the constraint, because a counter reading identically across a wave cannot discriminate a wave that exhausted the work from a wave that was one of many — which is exactly the discrimination that failed. Plan-level readiness fails it by construction: on the same session roadmap returned ready: 7 before the wave, 7 after it landed green, and 7 after twelve promoted nodes, because a plan stays ready until it is wholly done. Emitting that number would have confirmed the stop while giving it the authority of having been derived, which is worse than the silence it replaces.

The remainder therefore has to be counted per executable section, and nothing counts them today: plan_section_anchors derives its anchors from gates and comments, never from authored headings, so seven sections authored mid-session across two plans moved no number anywhere. §14 owns that inventory and is a prerequisite for the row below rather than a follow-on — shipping the closure signal first produces a confident terminal line, and then the line has been checked.

MeasureRequired evidence
Every run can join a waveBoth crew dispatch and crew shadow accept the wave and the session, and a shadow's run is attributable to the dispatching session rather than to a synthetic id. Asserted through both commands, because the shadow path is the one with no signal today and the reason this section exists. Attribution groups; it never judges. Keying a contamination or exclusion verdict on provenance over-flags, and the figure says how badly: a location-based predicate — did the run enter the main checkout — flagged all three of a measured session's clean effort pairs, while the content-based one, did the run read its primary's own changed paths or its commit, returned zero on each. So a run becoming attributable must not become a reason to exclude it, and the negative case asserted here is a known-clean run that stays unmarked.
The wave-open line carries the aggregate, or it teaches the wrong lessonThis row first recorded the per-worker rate alone and drew the inverse conclusion, which is the mistake the row now exists to prevent. Per-worker throughput falls with occupancy — idle 45.8–81.4, a median of 28.23 at two or fewer peers, 15.87 at four or more — and read alone that says a wide wave costs its members throughput. Sampling the same lane's timeline every 60 seconds and summing whatever was active says the opposite about the thing that matters: aggregate rises monotonically at 21.9, 43.7, 62.0, 60.0 and 69.4 tokens per second for one through five concurrent, while per-worker holds at 21.9, 21.9 and 20.7 to three. The lane does 3.2× more work at five-wide than at one, and scaling to three is nearly free. Each worker slows and the lane speeds up; on owned hardware the lane is the quantity that matters, because an idle accelerator produces nothing. So the line carries the aggregate beside the per-worker figure. A line carrying only the per-worker rate trains coordinators to narrow, which is the one behaviour idle hardware exists to prevent, and the four-wide dip rests on 28 samples against 64 at five and is probably noise. Asserted on a fixture fleet at two occupancies where aggregate rises while per-worker falls, so a reader cannot take either figure for the other.
A cold producer renders as missing data, and the seat's own staleness check is inertThere is a fourth code lifetime and it is the one nobody re-arms. The CLI is current every invocation, a follower reloads in place and keeps its registration, the MCP is cold until reconnected — and the watch producer is cold until re-armed. Measured by a peer: emit code from a producer armed at 07:54 feeding render code reloaded at 17:04, so a pane correctly rendered five unmeasured markers for records that genuinely lacked the fields. New render, old emit, and a working feature reading as broken. Verified on this session too: producer armed 13:30:38Z against an emitting module changed 15:04:36Z, cold by 94 minutes while holding the seat. And it does not self-heal on the next dispatch. Admission at dispatch.py:680 returns the existing state whenever watcher_live holds, replacing a seat only when its observer has died — with a documented reason, that refusing would block work over a producer streaming perfectly. So the one reading the admission path takes, is it alive, stands in for the question that matters, is it alive and current. The check for the second half already exists and is knowingly inert: runs.py's watch_seat_needs_replacement folds observer-death with running-other-code, and its own docstring records that only the first half carries information, because the version half is constant for any seat a given install armed. Confirmed: producer and installed both report the same dev string, since an editable install's version is fixed at install time while every session runs the working tree. So the discriminator has to be the emitting modules' content or mtime, never the package version. State that as a property rather than a preference, or the next reader will try to make the version comparison stricter: the version names the install and not the code — the helper's own docstring says exactly that — so both sides of the comparison read the same installed metadata and it is a constant for any seat that install armed. A signal with no variance, not a threshold that needs tuning, and no install fixes it: the version cannot move when the working tree moves, and the working tree is what every session runs. One wrong mechanism was offered for this and is worth recording, because it points at a repair that would not work: that reckon.__version__ is the literal string dev, making the comparison dev == dev. It is not. __init__ reads importlib.metadata.version and falls back to dev only when that lookup fails, which is what a bare system interpreter with no editable install on its path produces; under the project interpreter both the module and the released seat read 0.2.1.dev1279. On the placeholder story a properly-versioned install would fix it; on the real one nothing does. Two readers reached the placeholder independently, each by importing from the wrong interpreter. Of the two repairs, replacing a stale seat prevents the next instance while rendering the two absences distinguishably makes the current one legible — and the second is what would have helped: a reader had those markers in front of them for twenty minutes with no way to separate the two meanings, and read past them. Two measures: a seat older than the code it emits is replaced at admission, asserted by arming, touching an emitting module, and requiring the next dispatch to replace rather than reuse; and a record predating a field renders distinguishably from a record whose field was measured and absent, since those are the two meanings one marker is carrying today.
Each of the three load figures answers one question and drives nothing elseThree figures now exist and conflating any two produces a confident wrong decision, so each is labelled with what it answers and what it must not drive. Per-worker tokens per second answers whether budgets and ETAs on this lane are currently optimistic, and must never drive admission — it falls by design under desirable load. Aggregate lane tokens per second answers whether the lane is being used, and must never drive per-node sizing. The retry signal — genuine api_retry and rate_limit_event records, and router refusals — answers whether the server is in danger, and must drive nothing else. Asserted by a test per figure that its value moves the decision it owns and not the other two.
A wave's lifecycle is ticker lines, and its id sits where a run id sitsDirected by the lead 2026-09-09, correcting a design this section had settled wrongly with a peer. The wave's states are events, so they render as their own ticker lines — opened, member joined, sealed, closed — not as a per-row column read off every transition. That is the natural shape for a lifecycle a follower already delivers as a stream, and it makes the closure signal a line a coordinator receives rather than a cell it must notice. And the wave id is an identifier, so it occupies the position an identifier already occupies — where the run or worker id goes — rather than earning a new cell. A separate per-pane index was proposed on width grounds, 37 characters against a 36-column node cell, and is the wrong trade: it invents a second naming scheme for the same thing, which is how a pane and a record stop agreeing. A short index is justified only if the point being shown is how members group, which is a different question from identity and should be asked separately. Asserted on the rendered stream: a wave opening, a member joining an open wave, a seal, and a close each produce a line, and the id rendered is the one on the record.
A run joins a wave by default, not by being asked toMeasured after the attribution landed, and it is this section's own measure passing while delivering nothing. The row above says every run can join a wave, and an opt-in --wave flag satisfies the word can exactly: the option exists on both surfaces, the field sits at the pointer's top level, five tests pass. And a peer's census of the live data found the key on 3 of 11 pointers with all three holding the empty string, and absent from 797 of 797 committed rows. Nothing has ever been dispatched with a wave id, so no wave can accrete, seal, close, report a disposition split, or carry a cost. The mechanism is inert and every test of it is green. So dispatch assigns a wave by default — continuing the session's open wave, opening one when none is live — and --wave becomes the override that names a specific wave rather than the switch that enables the feature. Asserted with no flag passed: the pointer carries a non-empty wave, and two runs dispatched into one open wave share its id. Negative: an explicit --wave still wins over the default, so naming a wave is never overridden by the convenience that made naming optional.
