Asked by the lead on 2026-09-03 while the codex account was spent. Measured over this session's ledger: claude-sonnet-5 passed 8 of 8 at a 228-second median, claude-opus-5 passed 2 of 2 at 753 seconds. Sonnet is 3.3 times faster with four times the sample and both are clean, so speed decides the common case. The case for reserving opus is specific rather than general: on two nodes it caught traps its specification did not name — that writing a literal exhausted headroom would be silently downgraded by budget.py's own _is_known and hold nothing, reproducing the defect it was fixing, and that summing per-event usage undercounts generation 67-fold in a way that looks entirely plausible. Sonnet's contribution was different in kind: it declined to add an exception type and explained why, and it corrected a wrong diagnosis of mine mid-implementation. The coordinator has already set default_backend to the sonnet lane, so this ratifies or reverses a change already made rather than proposing an untried one. Note the coupling: reaching opus by specification level is not expressible until by_spec_level can name a backend, which is a measure of this same section.

Locked 2026-09-03 by the lead. This ratifies default_backend already pointing at the sonnet lane and puts the frontier tier where the evidence says it earns its cost rather than everywhere: on open-level nodes, the least-specified work, which is where opus caught two traps its specification did not name. Sonnet is 8 of 8 at a 228-second median against opus 2 of 2 at 753 seconds, so speed decides the common case on the larger sample. Reaching opus explicitly per dispatch was rejected because it leaves 'route an open node to the frontier tier' as a rule with no bound check, which is this sprint's own thesis about what decays. Opus as default was rejected as paying 3.3 times the wall clock on every node for depth the larger sample does not show is needed. The accepted coupling is that this choice is not fully expressible until §5's by_spec_level-names-a-backend measure lands, and degrades to per-dispatch selection until it does.

executable-remainder-source

§13's closing line must carry a remainder whose figure differs before and after a wave. Plan-level readiness cannot: it read 7 before the wave, 7 after it landed green, and 7 after twelve promoted nodes.

reckon-ship already classifies every section as implementable, deferred or done before dispatching, then discards it; persisting that classification costs one field and binds the count to a step that always happens. The zero-new-field alternative was measured, not argued away: authored anchors minus evidence-anchored comments agrees with the hand-set fraction on clive-local-workers (ten sections, evidence on all but one, impl 0.887), but over-counts exactly at the tail where a coordinator decides whether to stop — on binding-rules-need-bound-checks both sections it calls unstarted are a diagnosis section and an out-of-scope note, and on execution-orchestration-hardening it reads three of four unstarted against impl 0.6. A remainder that can never reach zero is a nag, and a nag is waived and then ignored. The per-heading attribute is precise but needs a backfill across 73 plans before the count is trustworthy anywhere, and an unmarked new section defaults wrong. Accepted cost: one new persisted field, which the plan_section_anchors docstring had deliberately avoided — that avoidance is why no section inventory exists. Bonus the alternatives lack: drift between the declared set and the authored headings is the mid-session-section signal the lead asked for, free.

§ Followups

Settle the two decisions, then land the liveness derivation and the gc pairing check

Both sections carry a measured defect and a decision that changes what ships, so the decisions come first. §2's is whether a liveness answer is derived from the process table on every read, reconciled against it, or left to the record that produced the defect; §3's is whether a project and repository mismatch is refused or resolved from mounts. Neither needs new investigation — the evidence is in the producer-orphans RCA and in the measured gc counts, 124 reclaimable worktrees reported as zero.

§4 is a standing obligation rather than a work item and lands as tests against the other sections. Take it last, and treat a failure there as a defect in the section that allowed it.

/reckon-build crew-observability-truth

Both decisions this followup was waiting on are locked, and both sections it names are landed and collapsed: liveness is derived from the process table under 896ba48 and reconciled in place without taking the seat lock, and gc resolves the named project's registered checkout under 45fc1b9, refusing a disagreeing --repo unless the cross-pairing is stated. Neither needed a node - the currency audit found both already implemented under commits that wrote no comment against their sections. What remains is section 4 as this followup describes it, a standing obligation carrying its own measure table, plus sections 5 to 8; a node is in flight against section 4 now. Also repaired here: two sections both carried id s6, so a comment or a --section dispatch targeting it was ambiguous. The transition section is renumbered to section 8 and moved after section 7 so the ids are unique and ascending; all 18 existing s6 comments were checked to be about provider refusals classifying as blocked and stay with the spend-limit section that owns that subject.

Repair the six ledger rows that store a no-commit marker as a commit

Ceded by the peer session that owns the promotion path, measured here before accepting it. Six of 486 committed run records store commits: ['none'] — a one-element list whose element is the literal string none. Any consumer counting commits over-counts by six, and a reader sees a token in the position where a sha belongs.

The mechanism is known exactly, and it is not a caller passing a bad value. The manifest convention lets a commitless node write a prose line, and three of the six still carry theirs: commits: none (read-only investigation; delivery artifact is outside the repository), commits: none — verification-only node, and commits: none — investigate role; assigned artifact is outside the read-only repository. An older parser took the leading token as a sha. All six are read-only, verification, or reuse-map nodes that correctly produced nothing, so the records are wrong in shape and right in substance.

Both ingress paths are already closed, verified rather than read: git rev-parse --verify --end-of-options 'none^{commit}' is refused, so --commit none cannot enter; and the manifest path now resolves each candidate through _commit_resolves_in instead of pattern-matching, with a comment naming this exact convention. The defect cannot grow. What remains is the six-row residue.

The repair is to store an empty list plus no_commit carrying each manifest's own stated reason, and for the three from 2026-08-12 whose run directories no longer hold a manifest, a reason recording that the row predates the resolving parser and its disposition was not captured. Do not invent a disposition for those three; an honest unknown is the point of the field.

Sequencing is the reason this is a followup and not an edit already made. crew.json is version-paired shared state that took a write one minute before this was triaged, with eight live runs promoting into it; a hand read-modify-write would race a peer's promotion and silently drop it. Run this when the wave is quiet, re-read _version immediately before writing, and confirm it is unmoved after.
/reckon-build crew-observability-truth

All six rows repaired, and the sweep it prompted found two worse ones. Each of the six now stores an empty commit list plus no_commit carrying its own manifest's stated reason. All six manifests survived, better than this followup expected, so nothing had to be recorded as an uncaptured disposition; the two whose manifests stated no reason beyond the absence say exactly that, rather than a disposition I would have had to invent. Done with the sequencing this followup demanded: the version re-read immediately before the write and required unmoved, indent 2 and insertion order preserved, row order and members byte-identical afterwards, 619 rows before and after. The S16 coordinator's addition earned its keep at once - resolve every sha you keep, not only the ones you change, because a repair that preserves a fabricated sha launders it into looking verified. Resolving all 619 rows found five kept shas that do not resolve in reckon. Three are legitimate cross-repository work resolving in imas-ambix or imas-codex, one of them the row recovered into this ledger today. Nothing asserts that a fleet state stays visible to the counters

The fix is in and unguarded, which is the state this plan exists to stop trusting. unreadable now renders, colours, holds a column and counts as blocked, all verified. But it fell out of the counters once already, between being added to the classifier and being reconciled into NEEDS_ACTION, and nothing would have caught that. The next state added falls out the same way, silently, because the relationship between the vocabulary and the buckets is a convention rather than a check.

Two assertions, and they are test-only:

First, the buckets sum to the row count. Over any set of snapshots, working + blocked + unpromoted equals the number of rows, so a state belonging to no bucket fails rather than disappearing. Today this holds — one unreadable, one working and one complete give 1, 1, 1 — and nothing keeps it holding.

Second, the vocabulary is a subset of what can wake someone. Every state the classifier can emit is either a working state or in the attention set; a new state that is neither fails the test naming itself. FLEET_BLOCKED_STATES derives from NEEDS_ACTION, so the two assertions together also pin that derivation rather than leaving it the accident that happened to save us here.

Scope is a new test file; no source change is wanted, and if one turns out to be needed that is the finding. This guard would have converted today's silent gap into a failing test at the moment the state was added, which is the whole argument for it.

A live pid outranks every self-reported terminal status, not just blocked

A peer coordinator in another repository measured the hole in the placeholder fix landed here. Its node reported dispatched → failed on the watch stream with a manifest reading status: failed, commits: [], changed_paths: [] and a blockers line naming every gate as outstanding. Every signal terminal, and the peer was one call from resuming it. The process was alive — pid 2807695, three minutes in, mid-baseline-suite and working normally.

The cause is the interaction this plan already named, reached from the other side. Because a worker's process ends with its turn, that coordinator now briefs every node with a long gate to write its manifest before starting the suite. The node complied and picked failed for the not-yet-measured state, which is defensible when nothing has passed yet. Our fix asserts that a placeholder blocked renders as running while the pid lives; failed gets no such treatment, so an identically pre-armed manifest reads terminal purely by which pessimistic word the worker happened to choose.

Two candidate repairs, and the second is the one the peer would take and the one this plan should prefer. Extend the liveness override to every terminal status — a manifest is evidence about intent and the pid is the authority on existence, so failed with a live pid is exactly as wrong as blocked with a live pid. Or give the contract an explicit in-progress value and name it in the dispatch prompt, because the whole defect is that a worker writing an early manifest has no correct word for “armed, nothing measured yet” and must borrow a terminal one. Naming the value removes the choice; the override then becomes a backstop rather than the mechanism. Do both, in that order of importance.

This is the fourth instance of the family: the record carries the judgement, the pointer carries the truth, and a reader who takes one for the other chases a settled question. The earlier three were the handed-off disposition the directory could not see, the same disposition refusing dispatch as unreconciled backlog, and a stored classification with no observation time.

/reckon-build crew-observability-truth

Folded and landed with commit 06e7a5f. A live process now defers complete, blocked and failed manifest outcomes rather than blocked alone, and does so without consulting log-versus-manifest recency, while after process exit each status retains its exact prior classification so the change adds no optimism anywhere. 62 passed and zero failed against a base of 59 passed and 3 failed. The contradicting case was the interesting part: test_observe_and_watch_render_failure_only_after_the_process_stops asserted that watch must retain an earlier alive observation, which is the remembered-liveness defect this plan exists to remove wearing the clothes of a correct intent. It was reconciled rather than deleted - it now rechecks liveness through process_alive and emits working then failed only once the probe changes - and its comment says so, so a later reader sees which half changed rather than finding an assertion that quietly moved. Promotion should resolve an abbreviated sha rather than demand forty characters a worker cannot produce

Measured by the same peer in one wave: two manifests carried a commit sha whose abbreviation was real and whose remaining thirty-one characters were invented. The backend reads git log --oneline, receives nine characters, and confabulates the tail to reach the forty the field wants. crew complete refused both, which is the guard working as designed, and the peer recovered the truth with git rev-parse HEAD.

The guard held here only because the fabricated tails did not happen to name real objects. A confabulated forty-character sha that resolves would be recorded silently, and the ledger's commit field is the durable claim everything downstream audits against.

Two repairs, either sufficient and the second cheaper for workers: require the manifest's commit field to be the output of git rev-parse HEAD and say so in the dispatch contract, or have promotion resolve an abbreviation itself instead of demanding a width the worker's own tooling does not hand it. Prefer resolving: it removes the failure mode rather than asking every worker to remember a flag.

/reckon-build crew-observability-truth

Folded and closed with commit a7bad43, and the finding is that no production change was needed - the absent deliverable was the measure. Promotion already resolves a recorded commit through _commit_resolves_in and git rev-parse --verify rather than matching a width, so the followup's proposal was already the behaviour. Four cases now hold it: a full-width sha resolving to no object is refused before a passing gate is recorded, an abbreviation of the kind a backend reads from git log --oneline is resolved to its full commit rather than refused for being short, an unresolvable abbreviation is refused naming the repository it consulted, and a full sha promotes without its identity rewritten. 23 passed against a 19-passed baseline. This session produced its own instance while the node was in flight: cot-run-state-buckets reported a sha with a correct 7-character prefix and 33 invented characters, the existence guard refused it, and the run was promoted against the real sha read from its worktree - so the guard is measured against a live confabulation as well as against fixtures.

An independent scored review runs before promotion, and outlives the run directory

Directed by the lead 2026-09-06: every landed job enters a scoring state before it is assessed for promotion, and a linked review worker on the local lane performs and attaches a multi-dimensional score. The orchestrator then holds the review and the ledger row when it decides.

Why a second reader is needed at all. The gate a promotion reads today was written by the worker being judged. That is why gate sits at 1.00 on every lane in the committed ledger and cannot separate one lane from another — a saturated measure carries no information. Rework was the replacement, and the lead has since refuted it too: rework counts a node that flagged an adjacent problem identically to a node that left a defect, so a worker that looks past its own fence scores worse than one that silently does as it is told. Both measures fail the same way, one level apart. An independent reader of the landed diff, run before the decision rather than after it, is the first signal in this chain the worker did not author.

ElementShape
Lifecycleterminal with manifest → scoring → review attached → promotable
Namingreview-of-<source-node-id>, role review, so a monitor row names its subject
Scorefive dimensions, 0–20 each: goal fidelity, evidence, scope discipline, durability, fit
Promptreckon/crew/prompts/review.md — a versioned file, not a string in a module
Recordone per reviewed run under the crew config dir, outside any run directory or worktree
Ledgerreview fields on the committed row, so the score survives every cleanup

Logging is a requirement, not a side effect (lead, same turn: it must be inspectable later). Three durable places, chosen because each survives a different loss: the record survives run-directory cleanup, the ledger row survives config loss, and the prompt in git makes the question that produced a score re-readable at the version it was asked. Every record carries the verbatim emitted text beside the parsed scores, so a later reader can re-derive the parse rather than trust it.

Capacity. Median local review wall is 812 s over 9 runs (243–1334). Peak landings measured at 78/day is ≈20.6 worker-hours, or 1.3–5.1 h wall at 4–16 concurrent. Reviews read a landed diff and contend for no source file, so they are immune to the dependency-graph width that binds implementation fan-out — the one class of work that can absorb spare local capacity.

Two failure modes written into the falsifiers. A parse covering four of five dimensions must not silently total over four, because a low score and a short read are then indistinguishable. And an unparsed review must not be stored as an absent one, for the same reason a stale hold and an honest unknown must stay distinct.

First node dispatched (the-reviewer-has-a-prompt-a-schema-and-a-store): prompt, schema, parser, store — deliberately unwired, since dispatch.py, recovery.py and promotion.py are each held by a worker in flight. The scoring state and the promotion read follow once those land.

/reckon-build crew-observability-truth §8

Landed and in routine use. Verified against the followup's own specification rather than by assertion: the prompt is a versioned file at reckon/crew/prompts/review.md (3,186 bytes, in git); 52 review records sit under the crew config dir outside any run directory or worktree, and all 52 carry the verbatim emitted text beside the parsed scores; the scoring state is in the classifier; and 35 committed ledger rows carry a parsed review total. Every one of the eleven nodes promoted on 2026-09-17 passed through it. THE MEASURE IT WAS BUILT TO FIX IS FIXED: gate sat at 1.00 on every lane and carried no information, whereas the 52 reviews span 75 to 100 with a median of 94 across 18 distinct values, so the signal discriminates. Its practical value was larger than the spread suggests, because the findings rather than the totals did the work: three reviews changed what the coordinator did this day, catching a recorded base figure that concealed the falsifier firing, a roster commit that absorbed a second registration, and a fail-open where an empty quoted status parsed silently. Two carve-outs recorded rather than hidden. The ledger row carries the scores but not the verbatim text, so the stated durability logic holds for the scores and not for re-deriving the parse if the config dir is lost. And a review run cannot itself be reconciled, because its deliverable lies outside any repository and promotion refuses changed_paths without a commit, which is recorded on work-does-not-fall-to-the-coordinator and is the one operational cost this mechanism adds.

The scan covers the worktrees inside run directories, and holds the dirty ones

So the scan paid for a traversal and returned a report that did not cover it — the same shape as a guard that never runs.

The safety property is the classification order, not the coverage. dirty is now tested before reachable, so a tree holding uncommitted changes can never be reported integrated. This is the ordering whose absence is recorded in the sibling followup on six stranded worktrees: a worktree sitting at its base revision passes an ancestor check while containing none of the work it was created for, so reachability alone can authorise a removal that destroys the only copy.

Re-run afterwards: pytest -q tests/test_manifest_log_paths_are_durable.py exits 0 with 9 passed, and the check was separately exercised against every manifest on this workstation — 6 flagged of 2,485. So the change is verified and the row points at evidence of nothing.

Why this is recorded here rather than fixed in place. reckon crew exposes no amend or annotate path for a promoted row; repair-completion repairs timing measurements, not gate evidence. So the record cannot be corrected where it is wrong, and a reader of that row has no way to learn this from the row itself. That is a gap worth naming on its own: a ledger that cannot be corrected accumulates errors silently, and the only remedy available tonight is a note somewhere the reader may not look.

What makes it worth writing down. The node being promoted exists because a promotion's cited evidence must not be a path that vanishes. Its own promotion cited a gate that never executed. The failure mode it was built to catch is the failure mode of its own record.

And the same class, twice more in the same beat. Verifying that check took three attempts to aim: the first probe selected a helper by name-matching and read a generator as a verdict, which is always truthy, reporting 220 of 2,485 manifests flagged; the second called the function with the wrong signature; only the third passed the required keyword and produced the real figure of 6. Two wrong measurements were produced and discarded inside five minutes while verifying a fix for wrongly-aimed measurement.

Standing remedy until an amend path exists: read the gate log's last line before passing an exit status to a promotion, and never pass a status the log does not show. An exit 4 with no tests ran is indistinguishable from a pass in every field a row records except the log itself.

/reckon-build crew-observability-truth §7

Its detection half landed as §10, commit 941fc6d: promotion refuses a passing gate whose cited log is empty, whose recorded end-of-log exit status contradicts the asserted one, or which carries the shell's own command-not-found diagnostic — the exact shape of the row this followup recorded. 9 of 9 new cases, zero added failures, and the instrument reads only an end-of-log capture record matched whole so a status token inside runner output is not mistaken for evidence. The second half of this row, that the ledger has no amend path for a committed row later found wrong, is NOT landed and is recorded on §10 with the boundary that produced it: an unreadable log path is skipped by design, so a row citing a vanished log stays acceptable and only a person reading afterwards would catch it. That residual sits in the section where a reader meets the check rather than in this queue.

The budget view reports the general weekly quota for a lane that has its own

Found validating the reconnected MCP surface. crew(view="budget") reports codex-spark with utilisation_pct: 21 on a 10080-minute window resetting 2026-09-15T02:28:53Z. That is the general weekly figure, shared verbatim with the three sibling metered lanes, including an identical burn_multiple of 6.2.

That lane's actual position, read from its own receipt in the same minute by crew(view="lanes"): 97% of a 300-minute window and 43% of a separate weekly window resetting about 3.7 hours later than the general one. So the budget view answers a question about one quota with another quota's reading, and it errs in the dangerous direction, reporting a nearly exhausted lane as clear for dispatch.

Why the two surfaces disagree: the lanes view keys each reading by its own window_minutes, because position in the rate-limit block does not identify the horizon. Budget admission reads a single utilisation per backend and inherits whichever horizon it found, so a lane whose receipt carries two windows loses one and may be described by the wrong one.

The remedy is the keying that already exists: admission must select the reading whose window it intends to judge, and a backend carrying a horizon the ceiling does not cover must read unmeasured rather than borrow a sibling's figure.

/reckon-build crew-observability-truth §1 — make budget admission select the quota reading whose window it judges, and read unmeasured rather than borrow a sibling lane's figure

Landed as §6's quota-attribution node, commit 79cf459: a lane whose receipt carries its own quota horizon is reported and held on that horizon rather than on the account-wide figure, keyed on the declared window length and accepting both witness shapes. 6 new cases, zero added failures. Resolved with the caveat that matters more than the fix: the motivating figure in the implementing manifest was labelled a live view and came from that node's own fixture, and checked afterwards against the live module every metered lane here still reports only the weekly window while the small lane declares no separate horizon at all. So the capability is landed and proven on constructed receipts, and whether any lane on this account actually carries its own shorter window is UNMEASURED. A peer coordinator independently reports the same limitation from its project and will send a five-hour figure if one becomes readable. Recorded on the section rather than left in this queue, because the open question is about the account rather than about the code.

The pointer's session field lags the stream that resolves it, and the standing recovery rule reads the field

Two findings from a peer fleet's coordinator, both about resumability signals rather than the work itself, and both read from a run directory rather than reproduced.

A null session field on a fully resumable run

A live pointer showed session_id: null and phase: "starting" while its worker had produced 239 stream records and written a complete blocked manifest. The standing recovery rule is that a null captured session means the resume path is gone and the pointer may be discarded — which is exactly the wrong call here. crew resume recovered the thread id from the first line of stream.jsonl and continued the session with every turn intact.

Verified here, and the mechanism already exists. Re-reading that same pointer later shows session_id populated with session_resolution.source: "stream", and session_resolution.consulted is ["pointer", "stream", "ledger"]. So the resolver does consult the stream and does write the recovered id back. The defect is narrower than an absent capability and more dangerous than a missing field: the raw field lags, and any reader keying on session_id rather than session_resolution.session_id sees null during that window. The rule an orchestrator follows is one of those readers.

Remedy: every resumability reader keys on the resolved value; the recovery classification and the discard rule name session_resolution explicitly; and either the field is populated from the thread.started record when the stream opens, or the live view derives resumability from the resolution rather than the field.

The retry fence counts scaffolding failures against substance

A run reported two distinct failed launches reaching the retry fence while its own failure_attribution named the cause as a measurement-script API invocation: the worker called a helper with a stale signature, so both failures were in its own throwaway script and the code under test was never reached. The node then presented as blocked on substance that had not been attempted. Nothing in the manifest schema distinguishes a scaffolding failure from a real one, so the fence fires identically for both and an orchestrator who does not open failure_attribution reads the count as evidence.

Remedy: let the retry fence read failure_attribution, or give a worker a declared scaffolding-failure disposition that does not consume a retry. Note that failure_attribution is populated on no promoted row in any mounted ledger, so a fence reading it must treat its absence as unknown rather than as clean.

Both are the same shape as this plan's own findings: a field that mirrors an authority is read as the authority, and the mirror is what a reader reaches first.

/reckon-build crew-observability-truth §1 — make every resumability reader and the retry fence key on the resolved value rather than the raw pointer field, and let a declared scaffolding failure not consume a retry

Three park failure modes present as three different states and none names its cause

A peer fleet's coordinator, whose reports drove most of this plan's landings today, ends with the synthesis rather than the instance. Three park failure modes now present as three different states and not one of them names its cause, while the underlying fact is the same in all three: the wait was never registered.

CausePresents asReads to an orchestrator as
a wait field missing from the declarationabandoneda dead worker
the timestamp written quoted, or with a local offsetunreadablea corrupt manifest
a stale inherited freshness baselineabandoneda dead worker (fixed: shared attempt-chain baseline)

The timestamp case, and why it costs more than a typo should

A worker wrote all five wait fields, every one correctly formed, and its row read unreadable. The single cause was that the stamp was quoted with a local offset rather than bare UTC. That field is parsed directly, so the quotes fail the parse and the whole declaration is then rejected rather than partially accepted — four correct fields buy nothing, and a park that is ninety-five percent right behaves exactly like no park at all.

The format supplies the trap itself: every other field in that manifest is written quoted by convention, and the one that must not be looks the same. So the two cheap remedies are to name the offending field in the message, since the parser already knows which one it choked on, and to tolerate surrounding quotes on the timestamp. Naming the field is the same ask recorded earlier from three unreadable declarations in one hour; this is its second instance and its first identified cause.

A correction in reckon's favour, recorded because it runs the other way

That coordinator built a job-end watch of their own, matched SLURM states exactly, and missed a cancelled job because SLURM reports CANCELLED with a trailing plus. Reckon's wait_terminal matching strips that plus and would have matched correctly, so the hand-rolled version was the defective one. Worth keeping: this plan's findings are overwhelmingly about reckon reporting something other than the truth, and this is one where reckon was right and an independent reimplementation was not.

Also queued from the same exchange

A gate log path under /tmp satisfies the evidence requirement today. Measured across every mounted ledger: 1,535 promoted rows cite a gate log path and 57 are under /tmp, with no durability or path test on that field anywhere in the promotion path. A gate whose evidence a reboot removes has verified nothing durable, and the digest recorded beside it will outlive the file it describes.

/reckon-build crew-observability-truth §1 — name the offending field when a wait declaration cannot be parsed, accept a quoted timestamp, and refuse an ephemeral gate log path

landed — node abandoned-means-the-worker-died at e662009, merged. The three park failure modes shared one cause and it is now fixed at the classifier: a run whose process exited while its manifest declares a wait classifies parked with its resume path, a non-terminal status in any spelling workers actually write classifies working with the vocabulary in one frozenset, and a worktree carrying commits beyond its base is never abandoned because that fact lives in git. Gate 85 to 94, zero added. Measured cause behind it: the wait facility was built across 24 references and five manifests had ever used it against nineteen parks in one day on one project.

N runs ending together on one backend is a lane event, not N worker failures

Measured 2026-09-14. Eight clive runs ended between 06:52:19 and 07:00:20, six within nine seconds, across four sessions. Each stream's terminal record carries subtype "success" while its own text reads "There's an issue with the selected model (deepseek-v4-flash). It may not exist or you may not have access to it." Zero rejected rate-limit events, so it was never quota. The serving job had been replaced by a DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash serve on SGLang, which the ambix-router refuses because its model cards carry no ambix metadata block, injected only on the vLLM launch branch. The follower classified every run abandoned, "the process is gone without a complete manifest" - true of each process and wrong about all eight, costing a peer coordinator an hour of hand reconstruction from timestamps, stream tails and the scheduler queue. The discriminator is measured and it is not the obvious one. stderr carries the unrecognised-model line in 65 of 70 September runs, 59 of them healthy and committing work, so that substring finds nothing. The terminal result record's own text appears in 6 of 70: marker present, 1 of 6 committed; marker absent, 59 of 64 committed. Discriminate on the terminal record, never on the stderr line. Three wirings follow. A correlation window - several runs on one backend reaching a terminal state within seconds - is a lane event and should be reported once rather than N times. A terminal record naming the configured model as unknown is a backend catalog change, distinct from a rate-limit rejection and from a 5xx outage. And for a locally served lane the serving job's presence in the scheduler queue is the cheapest health probe available.

Three verbs collapse into promoted, and a promoted row carries no spend at all

Two defects at one place, found independently and confirmed to compound. First, disposition: completing after a merge, discarding a dead pointer, and stopping a live worker all render as promoted, and only the first means anything landed. A peer coordinator discarded three pointers for runs that committed nothing and the pane reported three promotions. Keep the disposition as its own ledger field - landed, discarded, stopped, superseded - with the row reading it, and reserve promoted for a run whose commits are in the ledger. Second, measured across the whole watch corpus: promoted rows carry zero spend facts in 90 of 90 cases. Not sometimes - never. Promotion removes the live pointer, and the spend derivation reads the run from the live-pointer list, so the transition is written after its own evidence has been deleted. Every promoted row records a folded run count of zero and a measured stream count of zero. The row that claims work landed is both semantically overloaded and empty of everything else, which is why a wall time, a token total and a generation rate are all absent exactly where a reader wants a total. The spend facts must be resolved before the pointer is removed, in the same beat. Note before dispatch that this is a state-name change reaching promotion, the ledger, the transition record, the ticker and every test asserting on state names: the asserting files belong in one write scope, or a worker fences correctly and blocks.

folded into §19, partly landed. The row spends its width on cells that are never populated, and clips the one that matters

Measured over 366 watch rows carrying spend facts. Of the five spend cells, model seconds is non-null in 1.9 percent and the generation rate in 1.9 percent - two dead columns, ten screen columns wide, permanently rendering the absence marker. Charged tokens is 49 percent and the notional dollar figure 18 percent. Wall time is 54 percent, and on dispatched rows, where it is most populated at 84 of 100, it reads about two seconds: a transition into dispatched is at time zero by definition, so the figure is noise precisely where it is present. Meanwhile every row is clipped by the pane, including rows whose reason is empty, so the clip lands in the free-text field carrying the recovery clause. The inset constant is commented as provisional and awaiting calibration against a real pane; this is that calibration and it is wrong. Calibrate by measurement - emit a ruler line once at follower start and read where the pane cuts it - rather than by adjusting a guess. The rule the module lacks is that a cell renders only where the row's state makes the measurement meaningful, blank rather than a marker, because the marker already means unmeasured and blanking noise with it collapses two different facts. Order the grid so always-populated fields precede optional ones: clock, role, node, state pair, model, effort, then the fleet counters, then the optional measures, then the reason last. Cutting model seconds, charged tokens and the dollar figure from the row while keeping them in the record frees eighteen columns and widens the reason from 57 to 75 at the measured pane width. Model time is extractable and its scarcity is plumbing, not measurement: one dialect reports an API duration on the result record directly, the other derives machine seconds from event timestamps with generation as elapsed minus machine. Both need a terminal record, so fixing the promoted-row spend defect above is likely to populate model time and the rate on most terminal rows by itself.

folded into §20 and landed — node row-renders-only-measured-cells at commit b876427, merged. Carry the verbatim review text onto the ledger row, or stop claiming the row survives config loss

The review mechanism chose three durable places deliberately, each surviving a different loss: the record survives run-directory cleanup, the ledger row survives config loss, and the prompt in git keeps the question re-readable at the version it was asked. Measured on 2026-09-17, two of the three hold and the middle one holds only in part.

All 52 review records carry raw_text. Zero of the 35 committed ledger rows do. So if the config home is lost the scores survive and the evidence for them does not, which defeats the stated reason for storing the verbatim text at all — that a later reader can re-derive the parse rather than trust it. A total with no recoverable text is exactly the shape this plan objects to elsewhere: a number that cannot be audited by the person reading it.

Two honest resolutions, and the choice is a design one. Carry the text onto the row, accepting that the ledger grows faster — it is already 6.6 MB over 1,010 runs and its size is a measured cost elsewhere in this sprint, so this is not free. Or drop the claim, and say plainly that losing the config home loses the ability to re-derive any parse, so the row is a summary and never the authority.

Prefer the second if the first is expensive. What must not survive is the current position, where the design states a durability guarantee the storage does not provide.

/reckon-build crew-observability-truth

Cut the wait repair in two halves, with the design already settled and its two dead ends marked

Both halves live in reckon/crew/recovery.py — the classification at :1784 and :2283, the vocabulary at :92 and :123, the wait facts at :1891. Nothing else is involved, and the file was held by another node when this was written, which is the only reason it is a followup rather than a dispatch.

The design took three corrections to reach and the dead ends are worth marking so they are not re-entered.

Half one: the display ages the wrong artifact

Age the wait on the stream's freshness, not the manifest's, or render both. Measured on two runs at one instant: manifest ages 1838 s and 1672 s against stream ages 0 s and 3 s. The manifest is precisely the artifact a busy worker stops touching, so the signal is anti-correlated with what it detects. Confirmed in both directions: the alarm fired while the stream ran, and later cleared the moment the manifest was rewritten, with nothing about the work having changed. This half holds even for an entirely honest wait, which makes it the more general of the two.

Half two: the probe must be able to fail

Dead end, do not repeat: requiring a declared wait to carry a probe that runs and a terminal that can be reached. That was briefed here and is worthless — the two observed blocks were ["echo","pending"] and ["git","rev-parse","HEAD"], both against exit:0. Both run, both terminals are reachable, both are satisfied unconditionally, and that falsifier passes them clean.

The discriminator is whether the probe's result can ever differ: it must reference something outside the worker — a job id, a pid, a port, a path — never a command that cannot fail.

What is not settled

Whether a stale wait should expire rather than age into an alarm. A worker wrote wait_condition: orientation only — superseded by later writes in this turn: it knew its own record was stale and had no way to say so except by rewriting a manifest it was too busy to rewrite. Expiry would fix that from the other end and may make half one unnecessary; it may also hide a real stall. That is a design question, not a repair.

Evidence, with its limits stated

Six instances across four harnesses, both the implement and review roles, and two fleets. One wave measured end to end: five workers, all with stream ages under eleven seconds, two displayed in alarm — zero true positives. That rate is two of five in one wave on one lane and must not be carried further; n=5 is the size that reads convincingly and is overturned by the next sample. An earlier attempt to derive a rate from 389 historical conditions by keyword was abandoned as unsound, because the classifier was scoring its own vocabulary. The fields that would settle it prospectively (wait_probe, wait_terminal) reach the transition event only as of 2026-09-17, so the population starts accumulating from that date and not before.

The gate-log evidence scan refuses a valid log because a test fixture printed command not found

Measured 2026-09-19 promoting r-20260915T093545453615-the-guard-holds-at-the-merged-head: crew complete refused a passing gate because the cited pytest log carried the line 'bash: line 1: rekon crew frobnicate: command not found', which is captured stdout of tests/test_crew_gate_log_agrees.py deliberately running a misspelled command, while the log's own summary reads 518 passed, 20 failed, 2 skipped. The scan treats any command-not-found line as proof the command never ran; a log whose pytest summary line is present and whose exit sentinel is recorded did run. The coordinator promoted by citing the log's digest instead of its path, which bypasses the scan and is a worse record. Remedy: the scan applies the command-not-found rule only when the log carries no pytest summary or exit sentinel, or only to the log's first and last lines, and its refusal names the matched line.

A blocked manifest folds to a complete phase, so crew widen can never reach it

Measured twice on 2026-09-25 by the S22 coordinator. Widen's predicate and its stated invariant disagree on a still-working run

Review of widen-reaches-a-blocked-run (96): the comment near reckon/cli.py:2908 and the docstring near :2967 say only a stopped run that stated a block may be widened, but the predicate widens whenever the fresh manifest reports blocked, even with the phase still reading working. /reckon-build crew-observability-truth — widen refuses a blocked manifest while its worker is still running

Landed at merge 64a9b7d1: widen refuses a run whose worker process is alive, and the predicate, comment and docstring state one rule. Reviewed 98; the gate re-ran green at the integrated head.

§5 added from the lead's question — whether a codex default could delegate to claude on startup, since token exhaustion should be visible before dispatch. It should be, and the reason it was not is one missing flag. budget_check was set on codex-spark, codex-luna and codex-terra and not on codex itself, which is the highest-volume lane at 372 promoted runs and the one backend a pre-flight could not probe. Verified by enabling it: the same pre-flight that reported clear now returns held naming codex. So the visibility half is a configuration omission rather than a design gap, and it is already fixed in the host layer with the reason recorded beside it. Two further defects fixed in the same pass. default_backend still named codex while that account was spent, so every unnamed dispatch resolved to a lane refusing every turn; it now names claude. And the host layer carried TWO claude-opus definitions, added independently by two sessions — YAML takes the last key silently, which dropped budget_check and left the frontier tier with no headroom visibility while also quietly changing its time budget from 40 to 35 minutes. Nothing validated the collision, which is why the section now owes a refusal for a duplicated backend key. The delegation half stays open as design, because it is a real trade: refusing is correct when a human is watching and wrong when the point is unattended execution. The section specifies a declared ordered fallback with the three properties that keep it honest — the substitution recorded on the run so calibration never attributes a fallback to the lane the caller named, declared rather than inferred so a metered account is never spent by a caller who did not choose it, and never triggered by unknown headroom so absence of a signal stays absence. Effort raised from 6 to 9 hours.

A layer repeating a mapping key is refused at parse time naming its dotted path; tests/test_flight.py 59 passed up from 56, and the new loader immediately caught a real latent duplicate in the live host layer

a-duplicate-backend-key-is-refused landed — commit 8c1b637, merged; gate passed; measure tests/test_flight.py 59 passed up from 56, exit 0; 121 insertions over 2 files, 3 tests added, 296 worker-seconds against a 30m budget on the local lane at deepseek-v4-flash, full-diff audited per the write-access lock. A layer repeating a mapping key is now refused while the raw mapping is being constructed, naming the key's dotted path through the file in the same FlightConfigError shape every other violation uses. Catching it at parse time rather than after is not a style choice: yaml.safe_load has already discarded the earlier definition, so no post-hoc check can see the collision at all. The measure proved itself within seconds of being written, which is the part worth recording. The stricter loader immediately refused the live host layer, because backends.clive-glm repeated its sandbox, session_reuse and time_budget trio verbatim — value-identical, so it had cost nothing yet, and exactly the silent-drop hazard the measure exists to refuse. The duplicate sat four lines above a comment documenting the previous instance of this same defect and the one-definition-per-backend rule adopted after it, so the fix for the first occurrence had missed the second. The host file was deduped, with a backup taken first, and the resolved routing was re-read to confirm it loads; that file is outside the repository and outside any node's scope, so it is recorded here rather than committed anywhere.
This section's first measure was falsified by execution and has been extended rather than left to read as satisfied. As written it asks that every metered CLI backend declare budget_check and that a pre-flight against a spent account return held. Both halves were true and the wave still died: budget_check was declared on the claude lane, and crew preflight four minutes earlier reported utilisation 1.21% against a 95% ceiling with the lane clear. The figure was not wrong, it was answering a different question. A rate-limit utilisation percentage and an account spend ceiling are two different surfaces, and the pre-flight only reads the first — the earlier note in this section that the claude backend exposes no account-limit surface to read is exactly the gap, recorded and then not treated as a hole in the measure. Two measures are added: a spend ceiling reached with rate-limit utilisation still low must return held, asserted with the rate-limit figure held low so a check keying on utilisation alone fails the test; and a terminal result event carrying a provider refusal must classify as an infrastructure failure rather than a completion. The five runs are promoted as infrastructure failures so they stay out of the model's pass rate, per the classification rule the harness section already states, and their recorded streams are the fixtures both new measures should be asserted against.
The falsification is sharper than first recorded, and the sharper form is the one to build the test from. After all five runs had died on the spend limit and been observed and promoted, crew preflight --project reckon --role implement still returns held false with the claude lane clear at utilisation 1.21% against the 95% ceiling. So the signal does not merely fail to anticipate the exhaustion — it does not learn it from five recorded refusals sitting in the run streams and the promoted ledger rows. A dispatch to that lane right now would be admitted and would die on the same limit. The measure's phrase asserted against a recorded account surface is therefore already satisfiable by the wrong surface: the streams are recorded, the refusals are in them, and the pre-flight reads utilisation instead. The added measure must key on the refusal event, not on a percentage, and its negative is that a lane with a low utilisation figure and no recorded refusal stays clear.
The Anthropic lane hit a per-account spend limit at 10:02 Paris and §5's argument gains its second, sharper instance. Twelve run directories on this workstation carry the signature: api_error_status 429 with 'You've hit your individual spend limit ... your session limit resets 12pm (Europe/Paris)'. Claude-lane runs are clean through 07:45:48Z and the first denial is 08:02:33Z, with one dispatch at 08:03:00Z still passing, so it is per-request denial rather than a clean cutover. The failure is hard: is_error true, the turn dies, no diff and no manifest, which is why all five were classified infrastructure-failure with empty outcomes. That property matters beyond this incident — a peer session asked whether a degraded backend could have completed a manifest against partial work, and on this evidence it cannot, so promotions merged from a manifest during the window are sound. THE ASYMMETRY IS THE FINDING. A peer's stream carried three rate_limit_event records before the denials, including an overage record at utilisation 1.01 with surpassedThreshold 1 and isUsingOverage false — the account crossing its line while still being served. So on the Claude lane the warning EXISTS in the stream and nothing consumes it into a hold, while on codex the signal was never requested until budget_check was enabled an hour earlier. Unparsed against unasked, one blind spot with two causes, and the peer is currently working around it by reading rate_limit_event by hand before each wave, which is a workaround an operator should never need. The section therefore owes a hold on an over-line overage warning in the same place it holds on recorded utilisation. Worth recording what happened next, because it is the strongest operational argument the local lane has produced: a third coordinator working this plan lost four nodes to the limit and re-dispatched all of them onto clive rather than waiting two hours for the reset, and all three observed are running. THE RESET TIMESTAMP IS A CLAIM ABOUT THE FUTURE, AND IT WAS WRONG BY THREE AND A HALF HOURS. Caught by the lead 2026-09-03. The 11:37 denial said your session limit resets 3:40pm (Europe/Paris). I read that text out of the recorded stream, reported the lane down for four hours, held the wave and repointed everything to the local lane. At 12:09 a live probe returned is_error false with a served answer, four output tokens and a booked cost: the lane had been serving again for some part of the intervening half hour and the stated reset had not arrived. Two consequences, the second of which changes a measure. FIRST, THE ERROR WAS MINE AND IT IS THE ONE I HAD JUST FINISHED DOCUMENTING. Reachability is a reading with a shelf life; so is availability. I read a thirty-minute-old stream event and asserted it as current state — in the same session in which a peer's stale reading was refuted and re-explained as shelf life, within the hour of my writing that re-derivation must happen in the same beat as the assertion. The rule was already on the page and I applied it to the peer's claim and not to my own. SECOND, THE MEASURE MUST PROBE RATHER THAN READ. This section's added measure asks that a spend ceiling reached with low utilisation return held, asserted against a recorded stream carrying the refusal. That is necessary and demonstrably not sufficient in the other direction: a recorded refusal proves a lane WAS spent when it was recorded and proves nothing about now. So a hold must carry the age of the evidence it rests on, and LIFTING a hold must be decided by a probe rather than by comparing the clock against a reset the provider stated and did not honour. The honest instrument is also the cheapest available — a one-token request, four output tokens and under half a cent here, answering the only question that matters. Corollary for the recovery ladder: route to a lane that is serving, and if you must wait, probe on an interval rather than sleeping until a stated reset. That is the route-rather-than-wait advice already in the global protocol, correct for a stronger reason than the one recorded there.
SECTION 5's open measure - the fence reads the window figure, not the account figure - is not merely unasserted, it is actively wrong on the highest-volume lane, and the fence is inert there. Relayed by the imas-ambix session on behalf of nova and imas-codex, who each hit it independently; verified here from the code and this project's own ledger rather than taken on report. THE UNIT. _ClaudeDialect._budget in reckon/_backends.py reads info.get('utilization') and assigns float(utilisation) straight into utilisation_pct. That field is a FRACTION: an overage record reads 1.21 meaning 121 percent, and a five-hour window reads 0.61 meaning 61 percent. So a lane at 121 percent of its monthly allowance is compared against a 95 percent ceiling as 1.21, and the preflight says so in as many words - utilisation 1.21% with burn multiple unknown is below the 95.0% ceiling. A five-hour window at 99 percent would read 0.99 percent. The fence cannot fire on this lane at any utilisation. THE FIELD. The gating quantity is unifiedWindows..utilization against that window's own resetsAt, not the top-level account figure, whose resetsAt of 2026-10-01 is a calendar-month boundary. unifiedWindows appears seven times in this repository and every one is inside tests/fixtures/backends/claude-worked-turn.jsonl, carrying five_hour utilization 0.61 - the correct field is sitting in our own fixture, unread by the parser that fixture feeds. The usedPercent path in the same module is the codex account surface, a genuine percent, handled correctly, which is why codex reads a sane 46 percent while claude reads 1.21. THE ACCOUNT. claude and claude-opus keep separate budget records for one account - 47 and 16 records here, both landing on 1.21 overage nine minutes apart - so an observation on one preset teaches the other nothing. WHY THIS OUTRANKS THE OTHER FLIGHT-LAYER ITEMS, and the relaying session's argument is right: the failure is asymmetric in the dangerous direction. A stale hold is self-announcing, because the fleet stops and someone investigates within minutes. A stale clearance is silent - the wave opens, every turn refuses, and the runs read as worker failures rather than as a fence that never fired. That is the silent-success shape this repository keeps paying for.

zero failures at both the base and after revisions; the parser now reads unifiedWindows.<period>.utilization against that window's own resetsAt and scales the fraction to a true percent, and refuses with headroom unknown when an event carries no unifiedWindows rather than falling back to the account fraction. The fence can fire again. It is NOT yet effective on this workstation, which is the honest half: the stored budget record still reads 1.21 overage because it was written by the old parser minutes before this merged, and it carries a stated reset of 2026-10-01 so the shelf-life ageing will not expire it before October

Fixed at b9562693, and the second half of this note matters more than the first. THE FIX. The parser now reads unifiedWindows..utilization against that window's own resetsAt and scales the fraction to a true percent, and where an event carries no unifiedWindows it returns headroom unknown rather than falling back to the account fraction - so absence stays absence rather than becoming a false clearance. Zero failures at both the base and after revisions. The fence can fire again. IT IS NOT YET EFFECTIVE ON THIS WORKSTATION, and a green gate would have let me imply otherwise. The live preflight still reports utilisation 1.21 with rate_limit_type overage, because the figure it reads is a STORED record written by the old parser - observed_at 2026-09-05T13:41:51Z, minutes before the fix merged, written by one of this session's own nodes running on that very lane. A fix to a parser does not re-parse what the old parser already stored. THE STRUCTURAL GAP BEHIND THAT is the finding worth keeping: there is no record-format version on a budget observation, so a record written by a buggy parser is indistinguishable from one written by a correct parser, and this particular record carries a stated reset of 2026-10-01. The shelf-life ageing only expires a refusal that names NO reset, so this one will not age out until October. A wrong reading therefore persists for four weeks behind a mechanism designed to expire wrong readings, because that mechanism keys on the absence of a reset rather than on the provenance of the record. Either a budget observation carries the parser version that produced it and a reader discards what it cannot trust, or a fix like this one needs an explicit invalidation pass. What clears it meanwhile is narrow and worth stating exactly: one claude-lane run dispatched after b9562693 whose stream carries a rate_limit_event with unifiedWindows. If live events turn out not to carry that block at all, the lane reads headroom unknown - safe, because absence of a signal is never exhaustion, but blind, which is precisely what the imas-ambix session observed independently at 13:15 today.
THE FENCE IS NOW LIVE AND TELLING THE TRUTH, which closes the not-yet-effective caveat recorded above, and the closing evidence is a record rather than an assertion. reckon crew preflight reports for the claude preset: util 83.0, rate_limit_type five_hour, source ledger, observed 2026-09-05T13:47:04Z - a true percent and the gating window, where the same field read 1.21 overage resetting in October a few hours earlier. That record was written by one of this session's own nodes finishing on that lane at 13:47Z and folded through the new parser, so the turnover happened by ordinary use rather than by an invalidation pass. Verified the parser against the real event shape before believing the record: an event carrying unifiedWindows five_hour utilization 0.94 yields utilisation_pct 94.0 with rate_limit_type five_hour and resets_at 2026-09-05T17:00:00Z, the window's own reset; an account-only event carrying overage 1.21 yields headroom unknown with the stated reason that it carried no unifiedWindows. Both halves behave: the fraction is scaled, the window's reset is taken rather than the calendar-month one, and absence refuses instead of clearing. TWO INDEPENDENT OBSERVATIONS OF ONE ACCOUNT, and the peer's provenance correction is what made them independent. They first said their own claude nodes would produce the post-fix reading, then corrected that the stream they had read was imas-ambix's. That correction is what let me treat my own 83.0 as a second source rather than as a possible echo of theirs: 83 percent at 13:47Z from reckon's ledger, 94 percent at 14:09Z from an imas-ambix stream, three sessions on one five-hour window resetting at 17:00Z. A window closing, seen twice. THE READING TO GUARD AGAINST NEXT. At 94 percent against a 95 percent effective ceiling this fence is one point from holding, and when it holds it will be the first time this lane has ever been held. A later reader - including a later me - must not lift it on the grounds that the lane used to read clear. It used to read clear because the figure was a fraction in a percent field; a fence that begins holding immediately after being repaired is the consequence of repairing it. This session holds zero claude-lane work and dispatched none after learning the window position, because the quota is shared with two other sessions that are deliberately conserving it.
A WORKING ESCAPE FROM A SELF-SUSTAINING STALE HOLD, measured by the imas-ambix session and worth more than the diagnosis, which was already documented. The hazard is recorded in this workstation's guidance: only a served run writes a budget record, and a hold blocks every run, so once recorded nothing clears it. What was not recorded anywhere is how to get out. Two things that did NOT work, both tried: closing the hold record, because preflight re-derives from ledger evidence rather than reading a hold flag; and setting budget_check false, which stops the check without refreshing the evidence the check reads. WHAT WORKED: --set budget.resume_reserve_pct=0 for a single dispatch, which let one run be served, and that run wrote fresh evidence. 95 percent stale became 35 percent real. The mechanism is exactly right - the reserve exists so a spent quota keeps enough headroom to answer a stuck worker, and zeroing it for one deliberate dispatch spends that reserve to buy a fresh observation, which is the only thing that can break the loop. Their suggestion stands and I would put it beside the record-format-version finding above rather than as a separate item: an operator-facing way to mark evidence superseded. Both are the same defect - a budget observation carries no provenance and no way to be retired, so it can be wrong indefinitely and can be self-sustaining while wrong. The reserve-zeroing escape is a workaround for the second half; the parser-version stamp would fix the first.
CORRECTION TO THE ENTRY ABOVE, from its author, and it matters because I recorded a remedy without the precondition that makes it work. --set budget.resume_reserve_pct=0 IS NOT A GENERAL ESCAPE from a stale hold. It worked in that instance because the recorded utilisation was EXACTLY 95.0 against an effective ceiling of 95 - the ceiling being 100 less the 5 percent resume reserve - so dropping the reserve moved the ceiling to 100 and 95.0 cleared it. On a lane recording 99 percent, or recording a real 95 rather than a stale one, it does nothing, and it SHOULD do nothing: the reserve exists so a spent quota keeps enough headroom to answer a stuck worker, and a flag that spent it on a genuinely exhausted lane would be a way to lose the last of a quota rather than a way to recover an instrument. So the arithmetic was a coincidence of that reading, not a mechanism. WHAT TRANSFERS is the diagnosis plus one sentence: get a single turn served on the lane by any legitimate means, because only a served run writes fresh evidence and that is the sole thing that breaks the loop. Two things that do NOT work, both tried and both worth knowing because they are what a reader reaches for first: closing the hold record, since preflight re-derives from ledger evidence rather than reading a hold flag; and setting budget_check false, which stops the check without refreshing the evidence the check reads. The general fix remains the one recorded above - an operator-facing way to mark evidence superseded, beside the record-format version - and this correction strengthens the case for it, because the only current escape is a coincidence of arithmetic that a reader could easily generalise into losing a reserve they needed.

The budget observer now reads the exhaustion signal the local lane actually writes. It counts system/api_retry records carrying error rate_limit or status 429 and lets the terminal shape deliver the verdict, so a lane that refused a run is finally visible to the fence that is supposed to hold the next wave. Verified at the merged head against my own recorded streams rather than the node's fixtures: two runs shed tonight now report refusal true with headroom known and a detail naming the count ('died after 18 rate-limit retries (api_retry 429); the lane is exhausted'), while the two runs that each carried SEVEN retries and finished with real work committed report refusal false, and two runs live at the moment of checking report false. THE FALSIFIER IS THE RESULT THAT MATTERS: retrying is not refusing. A fix keyed on the presence of retries would have marked every busy lane spent and held the whole fleet, and would have passed any test written only against the dead runs. The worker went past the brief in two useful directions: it excludes 529 overload retries from the count, a discrimination I had not specified, and it declined to refuse the mid-flight-death shape I listed, on the evidence that a live peer carrying seven retries mid-flight is indistinguishable by stream from a dead one, so that verdict belongs where the process table is known. THE SCOPING QUESTION I FLAGGED IS ANSWERED CORRECTLY AND I VERIFIED IT AGAINST THE RUNNING FLEET RATHER THAN THE CODE ALONE: the counter calls list_live() with NO project filter, and _live_runs_on_backend('clive') returns nine live runs spread across three projects — reckon 5, imas-codex 2, nova 2 — while any single project's view shows a fraction of that. THE UNIT CAVEAT MUST TRAVEL WITH THE KEY, and it is not in the code: this counts LIVE RUNS while a serving lane's admission limit counts SIMULTANEOUS REQUESTS, and the ratio is variable — four sessions were measured producing two concurrent requests, because agentic workers spend most of their wall clock between turns. So an operator who sets this to match a server's in-flight number has not aligned them and will throttle the lane to roughly half its allowance. That is why absent-means-unlimited is the right default. A NEW DEFECT IN THE GC SURFACE, distinct from the repository-scoping one this section landed, found while doing the session's mandatory cleanup. reckon crew gc DOES NOT COMPLETE on this repository. Killed at 100 seconds with no output; a first attempt was killed by the harness at its own ceiling and printed a truncated traceback from inside _inspect_workspace. The cause is scale rather than a crash: the repository carries 183 worktrees, and the two git calls gc makes per workspace cost 396 milliseconds each on this filesystem, so the serial inspection alone is about 72 seconds before any ledger lookup or reachability check. THE FEEDBACK LOOP IS THE FINDING. Cleanup that takes longer than an operator will wait does not get run; worktrees accumulate; the next run is slower. One hundred and eighty-three worktrees is not the starting condition this tool was written against, it is the RESULT of the tool being unusable, and the number grows monotonically with every session that gives up on it. This session's own cleanup was done by hand instead - twenty-three worktrees, each checked for the two conditions the rule requires, clean AND reachable from the integrated branch, then removed without forcing, none refused. That worked and it is not a substitute: a coordinator who does not know the two conditions, or who is tired at the end of a long session, will skip it, which is precisely how the backlog formed. What the surface needs is to report before it inspects everything - a count and a first page rather than a complete answer, so an operator learns the scale before deciding to wait - and to parallelise or cache the per-workspace reads. Recorded against this section because it is the same command, and noted as unfixed: nothing in this sprint touched it.

Killed by the claude account's individual spend limit, reset 12pm Europe/Paris; the stream's terminal event was type result subtype success carrying the refusal text, so nothing classified it as a failure

The canonical instance of this section's obligation, produced by this plan's own execution and caught by the lead rather than by the tooling — which is the fourth time that sentence has been true in this fleet's history. At 10:07 the claude account reached an individual spend limit resetting 12pm Europe/Paris. Five workers on that lane died within seconds of each other. Ten minutes later the pane read 6 working, and the coordinator had just written to the lead that it was waiting on five workers that were already dead. Four independent detectors each failed for a different reason, and every one is already named somewhere in this sprint. First, the stream's terminal event was type result with subtype success, the refusal text sitting in the result field — a provider refusal recorded as a completion, so nothing downstream had a failure to classify. Second, all five died at phase starting and never advanced to working, and the stall and terminal-manifest checks evaluate over the literal working phase, so the runs were permanently ineligible for the check that would have caught them; that is clive-local-workers §7's finding verbatim, reported there by another session and reproduced here. Third, log_age_seconds read 383 with log_fresh true, because freshness is file mtime and the file had been touched by system records after the last generation event — this section's activity-is-generation rule, failing in the direction it predicts. Fourth, the classification was derivable the whole time: crew observe produced dispatched to abandoned for all five the moment it was asked, so the follower had nothing wrong with it and nothing asked. The measurement that separated live from dead took one read of each stream: the surviving run had its last assistant turn at record 333 of 334, the dead ones had a rate_limit_event followed by the refusal. That is the check the display should be making.
A second finding from the same check, in another direction, and it is not this project's to fix. Reading generation age rather than pointer phase across every live pointer in the shared config home surfaced four dead runs belonging to three other sessions in two other projects, last generating between fourteen and fifteen hours ago: two nova runs from session s18-pta, one nova run from s19-relaunch, and one imas-ambix run from pcp-s4b-01. Three of the four hold unpushed commits in their worktrees — two, one and one respectively — with clean working trees, so the work is committed and stranded rather than lost. Promoting or resuming another coordinator's run is the interference this fleet is careful to avoid, so nothing was touched and the finding is surfaced to the lead instead. Two things it establishes for this section. The scale of the display defect is larger than one wave: dead runs accumulate across sessions and projects and nobody is told, because every detector keys on a phase that a run dying early never reaches. And the fix has a natural test corpus already on disk — four pointers whose processes are provably gone, whose streams end without a terminal generation event, and whose file mtimes are fresh enough to have kept them looking alive for fourteen hours. The drain count is correctly scoped and unaffected: crew drain --project reckon reports one live pointer, because these belong to other projects' ledgers.

A run whose process is gone or whose manifest is terminal no longer counts as working; tests/test_crew_recovery.py 11 passed

CORRECTION to this section's earlier comment, which overstated the diagnosis and should not stand uncorrected. That comment said the five dead runs were permanently ineligible for the stall check because it evaluates over the literal working phase. Observation the same hour refutes the permanence: verify-the-merged-head transitioned dispatched to stalled on a 901-second quiet window, from a non-working phase. So the stall check does fire from a pre-working phase, and the true defect is narrower and more useful — the stall window is fifteen minutes while the refusal is legible in the stream immediately. The five were not undetectable, they were undetected for the ten minutes before the window would have expired, and I reconciled them before it did. That strengthens §6 rather than weakening it: a stall window is the right instrument for a worker that has gone quiet for no stated reason, and the wrong one for a worker holding an explicit refusal with a reset timestamp in its own stream.

REFUTING the first of the three peer findings above, measured rather than argued, and leaving it in place because the exchange is the record. The claim is that r-20260903T083356777926 is promoted gate passed with commit 957c042 while that commit is not an ancestor of origin/main, so the ledger asserts a landing the tree does not have. It is reachable, and has been since before that comment was written. git merge-base --is-ancestor 957c042 origin/main succeeds; the merge commit is 862d8cf with parents 56d13fc and 957c042; git log --ancestry-path 957c042..origin/main returns 862d8cf, 4f5c404 and the finding's own commit 0f4d54a; and tests/test_flight.py at origin/main carries fifteen by_spec_level references, so the 69 insertions are in the tree and not only in a dangling object. The likely mechanism is worth more than the correction: the check read a local origin/main ref without fetching first, so it compared a real commit against a stale remote-tracking branch and got a confident wrong answer. That is this plan's own subject in a third direction — after the phase that lagged and the mtime that looked fresh, a remote ref that had not been updated. A reachability check must fetch before it judges, or state that it did not. Two consequences and one thanks. The proposed mechanism stands on its own merits: promotion SHOULD verify that a recorded commit is reachable from the integration branch, or record explicitly that it is not, and this session would have been glad of it in the other direction an hour earlier. The rescue tag rescue/spec-level-names-a-backend-957c042 is harmless and can stay. And the peer's second and third findings are both sound and both self-critical — the release path is genuinely exonerated by a null release field, and the prose-versus-event grep correction, 15 prose hits against 11 real denials, is the aimed-instrument rule applied against its own author's earlier figure, which is the direction that survives review. The data defect it names is mine to own: six promoted runs record the literal string none as their commit rather than an empty list with a stated no-commit reason, and that is a promotion-path defect this plan should fix rather than a reporting slip.
CORRECTING MY OWN CORRECTION, which had the right verdict and the wrong mechanism, and the wrong mechanism prescribed a remedy that would have prevented nothing. I attributed the false reachability finding above to a stale remote-tracking ref and prescribed fetching before judging. Verified independently at the peer's prompting and the peer is right: every entry in git reflog show refs/remotes/origin/main reads update by push, with no fetch entry anywhere, because this checkout carries 20 worktrees over one common ref store at Code/reckon/.git — so a push moves the remote-tracking ref directly and there is nothing to fetch. Fetch-before-judging is hygiene for a separate clone or CI where the ref genuinely lags; here it would have changed the answer by nothing. The actual mechanism is shelf life. The merge commit was created 10:46:24, the ref moved to it at 10:49:05, and the finding was authored 10:50:27 — so the check ran before 10:49:05, when not-reachable was the CORRECT answer, and was published at least 82 seconds after it stopped being true, into a wave putting four pushes on this branch in 21 minutes. The measurement was right and the prose outlived it. So the measure changes shape rather than gaining a fetch: reachability is a reading with a shelf life, not a fact, and promotion must re-derive it in the same beat as it records the verdict and stamp it with that instant, because an unstamped reachability claim gives a reader no way to tell whether it has expired. ONE REFINEMENT THE EXCHANGE EXPOSED AND NEITHER OF US NAMED: reachable is ambiguous between merged-locally and pushed, and the two answers differed by three minutes here. The peer judged against origin/main, which answers is it pushed; my promotion claimed merged, which was true of local main from 10:46:24. Both readings were correct about different questions. So the recorded verdict must name the ref it was taken against, or it is untestable by the next reader even when stamped.
