A STORED CLASSIFICATION CARRIES NO OBSERVATION TIME, so a stale verdict and a current one are indistinguishable — contributed by session imas-codex-75, verified here rather than accepted, and it is the fourth direction the same defect has appeared from today. Their measured cost: a bug that had already been fixed read as live for thirteen minutes and produced a confident wrong diagnosis from them. Verified independently: classify_pointer returns terminal_at, terminal_age_seconds and log_age_seconds, so its INPUTS carry ages, and the verdict it returns carries no stamp of its own. The pointer's stored phase likewise carries none, which is why this surface's own guidance already warns that phase lags the stored record and tells readers to prefer the computed classification — advice that exists precisely because the stored one cannot be dated and therefore cannot be trusted. This is distinct from §7, which requires an age on every BUDGET reading; the same property is missing on the classification store, and a reader has no way to notice. Two remedies, and the first is cheaper than it looks: stamp the classification when it is stored so a reader can compare it against now, or drop the stored verdict entirely and make every reader recompute, which is what the guidance already recommends informally. Recording the pattern across the day, because four instances in one session is a shape rather than a coincidence: a reachability check compared a commit against a remote ref whose age nobody could see; a budget hold blocked seventeen dispatches on evidence three hours nineteen minutes old; an availability record read clear on evidence seven hours old and was right by luck; and now a classification verdict cannot be dated at all. Every one is a reading that was correct when taken and false when used. The generalisation this plan should carry is that a stored judgement without a timestamp is not a judgement, it is a rumour — and that applies to every store in this surface, not to the four that happened to be caught.

classify_pointer emits a fourth outcome, unreadable, for a present-but-unreadable manifest instead of letting ManifestParseError escape a parse call that catches only OSError. New measure added to this section from a defect measured live in this session: a durable manifest is not a terminal one. The manifest-before-the-wait discipline exists because a print-mode worker gets no turn after its last one, so a worker must write its manifest before starting a long wait - but the only non-terminal status word available is blocked. Four of this session's twelve runs wrote status blocked with blockers naming their own pending implementation, were classified blocked, emitted that reason to every follower, and were working normally with logs seconds old; two later transitioned blocked to working unprompted and one blocked to promoted. So the display cannot distinguish a placeholder from a worker that has genuinely stopped and needs answering, which is the one distinction the blocked bucket exists to make. Recorded as a measure rather than a comment so roadmap gates on it, per the rule that same-plan discovered work becomes a section on a live plan rather than a followup.
Worth recording as a reading discipline rather than only as a fix. This followup was written when its premise was true, and by the time it was triaged the record had been recovered into its owning ledger by some other path - so acting on the followup's own account would have meant treating a safe duplicate as a rescue, and the natural rescue action, copying it across, would have written a second copy into a peer's version-paired state file. The check that separated the two cases cost one field-by-field comparison. This is the same shape as the currency audit that found four sections landed under silent commits: a record describing the world at the moment it was written, read later as if it described now. A followup carries no observation time either.

FINDING CONTRIBUTED BY THE IMAS-AMBIX SESSION, written as content and deliberately not filed into this tree because it had caused a collision here earlier the same day. A WRITE FENCE COVERS THE MECHANISM IT WAS DESIGNED FOR, NOT THE ACTOR STANDING OUTSIDE IT. Three instances on 2026-09-05, all surfacing as a REFUSED MERGE rather than as any alert, and in each the protection was real, correctly implemented, and enumerating the wrong population. Instances 1 and 2, a tool inside a worktree resolving a path into the shared checkout: run r-20260905T141745440369-dsv4-endpoint-publish touched imas_ambix/agent/cli.py and tests/test_agent_endpoint_discovery.py outside its fence, and r-20260905T160558234233-dsv4-dryrun-port-order touched imas_ambix/agent/cli.py and tests/test_agent.py, both leaving edits in the main checkout rather than their own detached worktrees, both merges refused with please commit your changes or stash them before you merge. Two unrelated nodes, two different declared scopes, neither naming the main checkout; both had run a formatter; diffs of 51 and 106 lines, both AST-identical to HEAD, captured outside the repository before being discarded. Instance 3 is the one that hit this repository: a coordinator hand-editing skills/reckon-ship/SKILL.md while a crew run held that exact path as its exclusive write scope. THESE GENERALISE PAST THIS SECTION'S EXISTING MEASURE. Write exclusivity is per repository, not per project assumes the writers are RUNS. These three say the writer set is larger than the run set in two independent directions: downward into the tools a run invokes, and sideways to sessions that never dispatched at all. Instances 1 and 2 are the stronger pair precisely because nothing in the dispatch contract could prevent them - a worktree is a directory, not a jail, and a path resolving out of it is ordinary filesystem behaviour. THE HAZARD IS NOT THE FORMATTING. Formatting announces itself; a semantic edit by the same route would not, and a coordinator would then integrate one state while the shared tree quietly held another. MEASURES, and the second is the subtle one: git -C status --porcelain is empty at the moment a coordinator merges a worker commit, which both formatter instances would have failed; a stray path is classified by AST identity against HEAD rather than by diff size or by looking like formatting, because cmp and wc -l answer the wrong question and a semantic edit can be two lines; and the scope validator states its own population in its output, so a reader sees that it covers runs and not hand edits rather than inferring a guarantee it does not make. THEIR CAVEAT, KEPT DELIBERATELY: two instances of the tool escape and one hand edit, from a single day and one project. The mechanism is general; the RATE is not established, and no frequency claim belongs in this plan. Tested the first measure here while two workers were live: reckon's checkout carried one dirty plan file from a promotion comment, and both workers' output was correctly confined to their own worktrees. So the assertion is cheap, it fires, and it fired on a benign case - which is the right behaviour for a check whose value is that it also catches the malign one.
FOURTH INSTANCE OF THE FENCE-NAMES-ITS-POPULATION MEASURE, on reckon's own shadow surface, contributed by the imas-ambix session and verified here structurally. A SHADOW WORKER CAN READ THE ANSWER IT IS BEING MEASURED ON. crew shadow re-runs a committed node at its original base as never-merged evidence, but a worktree shares the object store with its parent checkout, so a base ref predating the landed commit does not hide that commit: git log on main, then git show the sha, and the answer is in hand. Verified here rather than reasoned - from a worktree created at an older base, git cat-file -t on the current HEAD sha returns commit and git log renders its subject. MEASURED, NOT THEORISED, by a peer running a repeat measurement across two deployments from a base deliberately chosen to predate the landing. The worker found the landed commit through the shared repository, reproduced it byte-identically in both files, and said so in its manifest; its gate passed 12 of 12. First edit came at turn 60 after 463 seconds against turn 6 at 17 seconds for the original run, because the interval had been spent reading rather than solving - so the time-to-first-edit comparison was void and the output tokens measured transcription. Promoted with the contamination stated and not merged, which is the right disposal. WHY IT IS THIS MEASURE AND NOT A METHODOLOGY NOTE: the fence is --base, and what it fences is the WORKTREE'S CHECKOUT STATE, not the WORKER'S REACHABLE KNOWLEDGE. It names one population, the files at that revision, and the worker reads from a larger one. HOW IT DIFFERS FROM THE FORMATTER PAIR, and the distinction is theirs: there the escape is an unconstrained filesystem and nothing in the dispatch contract could prevent it, while here the escape is git's own design, so a fix is at least conceivable - the object store is shared deliberately and for good reasons, and it is the REACHABILITY of a later ref rather than the sharing that leaks. CANDIDATE ASSERTION, offered tentatively by its author and recorded as tentative: a shadow's evidence is clean only if the answer is unreachable from its worktree, so the check is whether the node's own landed commit is an ancestor of anything reachable there rather than whether HEAD sits at the recorded base. crew shadow records nothing of the kind today. Recording it beside the result may be sufficient rather than enforcing it, since a reader who knows the answer was reachable can discount the row without the system preventing the run - and prevention may not be achievable while the object store is shared.

The ship skill now states that a session arms and attaches exactly one monitor, bound by a verbatim test so the rule cannot quietly disappear. 52 passed. IT FOUND AND RECONCILED A CONTRADICTION, which is the part I could not have specified in advance: the existing registration paragraph implicitly tolerated a second follower as benign, so two statements would have stood on the same question. It is rewritten to make a second follower legitimate only as a replacement hand-off, and I verified at the merged head that no text anywhere in the skill or its references still calls one harmless. IT MADE ROOM RATHER THAN RAISING THE BOUND, as the brief required: headroom went from 44 to 145 estimated tokens by relocating follower stream mechanics into the reference as section 15, and the relocation was demonstrated rather than asserted — both blocks verified byte-for-byte in the reference and absent from the skill, the heading reachable from its pointers, combined net plus 215 words. KNOWN GAP I AM RECORDING RATHER THAN LEAVING TO BE FOUND: the rule tells a reader to attach additional sessions to the one monitor, and that capability does not exist yet. crew follow takes a singular --session; only --run says repeat for several. So the skill currently documents a behaviour the CLI cannot perform. The contract test does not catch it because it binds registered verbs and flags, not prose instructions. The append-session node closes this, and if it does not land, that sentence must be revised rather than left standing — a skill instructing an impossible action is the same defect class as a skill naming an unregistered verb, which is what the previous node in this section repaired.

The follower delivers every transition; the attention filter is gone from the CLI, from the skill, and from the three documents that still described it. Verified at the merged head: crew follow --help carries no such flag, all three documents read zero, and 84 tests pass across the monitor, skill-contract and session-delivery files. The one test red on main since the paused verb landed is closed — deleted with the filter it asserted a property of, which was the honest close rather than keeping a private seam to satisfy an assertion about a feature that no longer exists. Full importing gate 401 passed with 10 failures against a base of 11: zero added and one closed. The skill read-set budget improved rather than degraded. IT BLOCKED CORRECTLY AND THAT IS WHY THE REMOVAL IS COMPLETE: my fence covered six files and the goal needed ten. It committed its work first, ran both gates, isolated exactly one added failure, attributed it to the specific commit, and offered three options with a leaning and a cost-if-wrong. The grant added the filter's own test plus three stale documents — a flag removed from the CLI while three files still describe it is the same defect this session spent the night clearing elsewhere. It also declined to edit historical plan and evidence HTML that records the filter as it was, which is correct: that is the record, not the surface. A CONFABULATED SHA, CAUGHT AT MERGE: its manifest cited the second commit as 49ec60a0b53f... at forty-one characters, resolving to nothing. The real head is 49ec60aaf966... The short prefix 49ec60a is correct and every character after it is invented, which is the known shape — nine characters read from git log --oneline and a fabricated tail. The first sha was correct at forty. Caught by resolving each with rev-parse --verify against ^{commit} rather than by checking width; a width test passes on any hex string and would have accepted this one.

The copyable monitor invocation is bare — 'reckon crew watch --project <project>' and the follow equivalent — so a session pasting the canonical line no longer turns colour off in a pane where colour is wanted. The flag is KEPT and still documented as an opt-out named for a sink that cannot render colour; the CLI is untouched. Verified at the merged head: both lines bare, the flag still present twice in the file, 4 passed. THE DEFECT WAS THE DOCUMENTATION AND NOT THE FLAG, which is why removing the option would have been the wrong fix and would have repeated a migration failure this session caused earlier by deleting an option a live follower was still passing. The file presented each command as one bracketed form listing every option, so a reader copying the canonical line copied the opt-out with it; three sessions did exactly that in one afternoon. IT DID THE PROSE CHECK THE BRIEF ASKED FOR RATHER THAN ASSERTING IT, quoting the sentence that says colour is stated rather than detected because the pane is a pipe and the conventional terminal probe would disable it exactly where it is wanted, then checking the sentences either side for contradiction and confirming the following one — that the arming line stays bare — now matches the presented form. It also cross-checked the bare form against the arming line the dispatch surface actually returns, which an existing test pins, so the documentation and the runtime now agree on the same string. FOLLOW-ON WORTH TAKING: the same presentation shape survives in the ship skill, where a watch line embeds an optional stall-window flag in a pasted-looking form. It does not teach colour off so it is not urgent, but it is the same trap one file over.

A follower armed with the removed attention flag now starts and delivers instead of exiting 2. Demonstrated by me at the merged head rather than asserted: the command runs until a timeout kills it, stdout is empty, and stderr carries one line saying the flag no longer filters, that every transition is delivered, and that it stops being accepted after 2027-06-30. 6 passed. THE FALSIFIER WAS TESTED PROPERLY, which is what makes this a shim rather than a reinstatement: an armed and an unarmed follower are asserted to receive identical routing kwargs AND identical delivered rows, with dispatched, working and complete present in both, and the deprecation is proven never to enter the delivered stream by a real-binary subprocess test with separated channels. The registration scope records no attention key and no sibling command gained the flag. Nothing was threaded back into the delivery path. THE HORIZON IS AUTHORED IN CODE and asserted present in the message, so the shim has an end rather than becoming permanent. WHY IT EXISTED: removing the flag earlier today broke every follower armed before the removal, because a reconnect replays the argv it was armed with — a peer lost one within the hour and saw a failed monitor rather than a removed flag. The lesson is not that the removal was wrong but that a live surface needs a migration window, and the tolerant-reconnect alternative was rejected because silently dropping unknown flags would also swallow a genuine typo.

The reader-side half of every defect in this plan, contributed by a peer against themselves and recorded here because it outlives every fix listed above. They told this session to resume a run that had in fact finished, and their own message contained the refutation: they had written 'commits ahead 2, uncommitted 0' and called it the GOOD case, in the same paragraph, and then took the classifier's abandoned label anyway. Their words for it: gathering the evidence that contradicts a label is not the same as acting on it, and they did the first while behaving as though they had done the second. THIS SESSION DID THE SAME THING AT LEAST THREE TIMES and the pattern is identical each time - a correct measurement taken, and then a conclusion drawn from the label rather than from the measurement. A launcher banner read from one pre-cap run and asserted of three logs never opened. A file mtime read as evidence of which value changed. A raw pointer field reading None, which means unknown, counted as dead. In every case the disconfirming fact was either already in hand or one command away. The asymmetry worth stating: this plan can fix a parser, a classifier, a renderer and a fence, and none of that touches the habit of trusting the label over the reading, which is the failure that survives every repair. The only mechanism found today that actually helps is the positive control - not asking whether the evidence supports the label, but running the case known to be present and checking the instrument answers. That is cheap, it is mechanical, and it is what caught each of the three.

THE REFUSAL-VISIBILITY GAP IS WIDER THAN THIS PLAN RECORDED: IT COVERS PROMOTION, NOT ONLY DISPATCH. Routed by another project's coordinator, who measured it promoting a single node through four attempts, each refused for a different and entirely legitimate reason — a gate lacking the check that produced it, a manifest missing its commits field, uncommitted changes at a declared path in the main checkout, and a changed path outside the declared write scope. None of the four produced a follower event. The run sat at unpromoted throughout and the pane's only line for the whole promotion was the final unpromoted → promoted. A reader of that stream sees a clean two-step and no evidence that promotion was ever contested.

Every refusal was correct behaviour — reckon declining to record evidence it could not verify. The finding is not that they happened; it is that the pane cannot show that they happened.

The asymmetry has a structural cause, and it is why no amount of care fixes it. A success is self-announcing: the work exists and the next read finds it. A refusal creates nothing, so there is no run to hang a transition on, so the stream is structurally silent — the coordinator's return value is the only witness, and only if it is read. That is tolerable one call at a time and degrades with batch width, which is the direction this fleet has been pushed. The original narrower instance: a dispatch refused for per-member serialisation inside a wave of six, where the pane showed five arrivals and was silent about the sixth request. Had the pane been trusted, 42 plans of an exhaustive triage would have been left silently uncovered.

Deliberately recorded without a proposed mechanism, on the reporter's reasoning, which is sound: a stream carrying non-events is a different kind of unreliable. A refusal counter a coordinator can compare against what it intended to send, or simply documenting that the stream cannot carry refusals so the return value must be read, are both plausible — as is judging the exposure acceptable. What would not work is a coordinator-side convention, because that is the compensation this project keeps finding does not survive a context boundary.

One correction the reporter volunteered against their own account, worth keeping because it protects the monitor from a false charge: the follower did fire correctly, 90 seconds after the run went terminal. The 36-minute delay that followed was the coordinator seeing the event, saying it would gate the run, and answering something else first. The instrument worked; the reader did not.

A QUERY AGAINST A FIELD THAT DOES NOT EXIST RETURNS EXACTLY WHAT A REAL ABSENCE RETURNS, AND NOTHING IN THE RESULT DISTINGUISHES THEM. This section already records that absence of evidence has been mistaken for evidence three times in this fleet's history. Three more happened today, between two coordinators, in one session-day — so the count is not the point and the shape is. All three share one structure: a payload nobody had looked at, a uniform empty result, and a confident reading of it.

ReadingActual causeWhat it nearly cost
every live pointer walked for a top-level write_paths key; uniform emptythe key does not exist at that levelreported every path free, including two a peer was actively holding
the ledger queried for a records key; zero rowsthe key is runsabout to report that a promotion had not recorded
manifest_error: None beside a precise parser refusalthe view carries no manifest detail at all — all 13 rows, every manifest field absenta fabricated finding, asserted twice and passed to a peer as corroboration

The first row is the one that shows the stakes. A scope reading that reports free paths which are in fact held does not produce a wrong document — it produces two workers writing one file. The other two cost a retraction; that one would have cost work.

The detector is one command and it is the only reliable one: does ANY row carry this field. A positive control distinguishes the two cases and nothing else does — not the shape of the result, not its plausibility, not the code that should have written it. That last point is the sharp one, contributed by the peer who made two of the three: checking the capture code is a check on the writer when the doubt belongs on the reader. In the third instance here the capture was verified correct, and the reading was still wrong, because the question was never whether the value was written but whether the view returns it.

Why it survives being known. All three readers knew the rule; one of them had recorded it in this plan the same morning. It defeats knowing it because the empty result confirms the expectation that prompted the query — you go looking for a silence and you find one — and at that moment the expectation and the evidence are the same object. The control is cheap precisely because it does not test the hypothesis: it tests the instrument, which is the thing under suspicion and the thing never suspected.

THE UNREADABLE CLASSIFICATION FIRES ON THE NORMAL MANIFEST AUTHORING SEQUENCE, AND THIS COORDINATOR WATCHED IT HAPPEN THIS MORNING WITHOUT RECORDING IT AS A DEFECT. Measured by a peer:

10:10:25Z   manifest written (orientation block)
10:10:28Z   follower: unreadable — "repair: the manifest at ..."
10:13:00Z   manifest parses: status 'waiting', cleanly

Three seconds. The worker writes the orientation block first and the status line after — exactly as the contract instructs, manifest before any long wait — and the classifier sampled the gap between two writes. Nothing was wrong at any point.

This session saw the identical thing at 05:10 and read it as benign without recording it. The landing-grant run reported cannot read manifest: the body reads as manifest fields (node, orientation_base_sha, orientation_worktree, orientation_write_paths) but carries no status key, and it was noted in passing as the orientation block the worker writes first; it will resolve when it writes a status. That diagnosis was correct and the conclusion drawn from it was wrong: knowing why a display is lying is not the same as the display not lying, and a coordinator who did not happen to know the authoring order would have opened a worktree.

Two populations, one state, and no reader repair reaches the second. A manifest unreadable because a worker decorated its status word is the six-form defect repaired this session. A manifest unreadable because it is three seconds old is not a spelling at all. They produce the same row and the same instruction.

The general shape, and it is the third instance today. A run finished, merged and pushed displayed as stalled. A three-second-old manifest displayed as unreadable with a repair prompt. A starved worker indistinguishable from a hung one, which had three coordinators opening raw streams by hand. Different causes, one shape: a state derived from a single sample of something that changes, announced as though it were settled. Every one of the three told a reader to act where acting was wasted, and in two of them the reader was about to destroy working work.

The discriminator needs no new state. Requiring a condition to survive one observation interval before it becomes actionable drops the transient population entirely while leaving a genuinely undecodable manifest exactly as loud — the file's own age against the run's would do as well. That is a property of the classifier rather than the reader, so it is not covered by the six-form repair and not covered by the classifier node now in flight, whose measures are about a dead process against a terminal manifest. It is a third sibling.

A positive control for the honest-unknown form, found by nearly filing a defect against it. Ahead of an authorised serve restart that would kill every in-flight local worker, recovery depended on each run's session_id. Read straight off the live pointer files, 20 of 28 are null — which reads as a fleet that is 71% unrecoverable. I reported that to two peer sessions; one relayed it onward, where it rescued a run a third session had already written off as needing redispatch.

The rescue was right and the framing was wrong. crew list does not show what the pointer files show: its session_resolution block consults pointer → stream → ledger and resolves 24 of 25. The one it cannot resolve it reports exactly as this plan argues a surface should — resolved: false, source: None, and the detail "all three were consulted, so this is an absence rather than a reading nobody has taken yet." A genuine absence, distinguished in words from a reading not yet taken.

So the charge that a surface was stating a falsehood does not land, and a proposed repair was withdrawn before anyone acted on it. reckon/crew/AGENTS.md already records the underlying hazard and what it cost twice in one day; what is new is that the fix for it is in place and works.

The hazard has therefore moved, and this is the part worth carrying: it is no longer a display that misleads, it is a coordinator reading around the display to the raw store underneath. Both peers and I independently ran the same shortcut. A surface cannot defend against being bypassed — so wherever a raw record is load-bearing and its resolved form lives elsewhere, the raw field is the thing that needs to stop being readable as an answer, not the surface that needs fixing.

Evidence for this section's third measure, and it is an A/B on the same defect twice in one morning — worth recording because the ordering it establishes is the opposite of what both intuition and time pressure suggested. FIRST DENIAL, 10:07, five runs killed: I promoted all five as infrastructure failures to clear an unreconciled-runs refusal that was blocking the next dispatch. Promotion deletes the live pointer and takes session_id with it, so that reconciliation discarded five resumable sessions and forced four redispatches from scratch; one node's completed commit survived only because its worktree was still on disk and I inspected it before redispatching. SECOND DENIAL, 10:32, same limit, two runs killed: both still had live pointers and captured sessions, so both were resumed with advice saying only that the limit had reset. Both transitioned back to working within seconds, and spec-level-names-a-backend-with-provenance ran to completion with its commit and 61 passing tests, context and partial reasoning intact. Cost: one command each, against four redispatches for the equivalent runs an hour earlier. So RESUME BEFORE YOU RECONCILE, because reconciling destroys the resume path. The disposition turns on two cheap reads: a live pointer plus a captured session_id gives a resume, while a denial before session capture, or an already-promoted run, leaves only a redispatch under a NEW node id — reusing the id collides on the worktree path and makes the failed attempt and its recovery indistinguishable in the ledger. When an unreconciled-runs refusal is what is pressing you to promote, --allow-unreconciled-runs on the next dispatch keeps the pointers resumable and records the waived backlog. And a resume is not only for answering a NEEDS-HELP question: conflating those two is what makes resume look inapplicable to a denial, when in fact there is nothing to answer and everything to continue.

A provider spend or usage refusal now classifies blocked with backend, limit kind and reset in its reason; 12 classification tests plus 93 across observation, backends and budget

a-provider-refusal-blocks landed — commit 71b50b0, merged; gate passed; measure tests/test_failure_classification.py 12 passed with 10 added, plus 93 across observation, backends and budget with zero added findings; 308 insertions over 2 files, on claude-sonnet-5 at medium after being resumed through a spend-limit denial — the mechanism it implements interrupted the node implementing it. A recognised refusal now carries an explicit budget refusal boolean, kept distinct from threshold_status and surpassed_threshold because ordinary rate-limit telemetry sets those too; the phase resolves to blocked when a terminal event is an error AND that boolean is set, so a bad model id or a context overflow still resolves to failed, verified against the pre-existing failed-turn fixtures; and the detail line names the configured backend, the limit kind and the reset, or states the reset unknown. THIS NODE CORRECTS THIS SECTION'S OWN TEXT, and the correction is the more careful reading. §6 above cites verify-the-merged-head as killed rather than blocked, implying its terminal event was an unrecognised refusal. Measured against its recorded stream, that is wrong: it hit the spend limit three times MID-STREAM, recovered, produced two further genuine non-error turns, and was killed externally afterwards with no refusal terminal event at all — its last result event carries is_error false and the text Holding for segment 2, so classifying it complete is correct both before and after this change. The node used it as the required NEGATIVE fixture, proving a mid-stream refusal does not leak into terminal classification, and declined to assert it as a sixth blocked positive because reaching a killed-to-blocked verdict needs process-liveness reasoning that its fence told it to stop on rather than widen into. So the five sonnet runs are the instances of a refusal recorded as a success; this one is an instance of something else, and my own generation-age check had already reported it as dead with no refusal in its terminal event before I wrote the looser claim over the top of it. Two smaller judgements worth keeping: quota was deliberately not given a third regex, because no recorded stream uses that wording and the codebase already uses quota refusal as the generic term for what is recognised; and the five real terminal events were embedded as literals rather than read from the config home at test time, because a test must not read state outside the repository, with node ids withheld from the fixtures per the naming rule and only session ids kept as the distinguishing fact.
The third denial of the day tested this section's landed work against production within an hour of it landing, and found the verdict right and the delivery wrong. At 11:37 four claude runs died on the spend limit, reset 3:40pm Europe/Paris, four hours out rather than the twenty-five minutes of the second denial. THE CLASSIFICATION IS CORRECT. crew observe on one of them returns phase blocked with detail backend claude refused the turn on a spend-limit, and budget.refusal true — and it reaches that verdict from is_error true while subtype still reads success, which is exactly the discrimination the section asked for and a stricter reading than the subtype would give. THREE DEFECTS AROUND IT, all of the same shape as the role column landed the same hour: the unit is right and nothing carries it to the path a reader uses. FIRST, two classifiers disagree. The follower renders these runs abandoned, because it reads recovery.classify_pointer, while the blocked verdict lives in observe's _phase. The refusal work landed in the observation path and not in the pointer classification path the ticker reads, so a coordinator watching the pane still sees no block. SECOND, the trigger is missing rather than the rule. The refusal was legible in the stream the instant it arrived; what actually fired was the 901-second stall window, and the runs only became blocked when I called observe by hand. This is the narrower defect recorded earlier in §4, now with its mechanism: nothing folds a terminal event when the process dies, so an immediately-legible refusal surfaces as a fifteen-minute stall. THIRD, the reset is in the text and not in the record. The refusal literally says your session limit resets 3:40pm (Europe/Paris) and the detail line says reset unknown, so this section's second measure is met only by its own fallback clause while the datum a triage decision needs sits unparsed one field away. Disposition applied per this section's own lesson rather than by promoting: all three runs recorded handed-off through crew drain, pointers and captured sessions kept, so they remain resumable when the limit lifts, and the closure count reads zero unreconciled without destroying a single resume path.
THE PANE'S GONE-WITHOUT-A-COMPLETE-MANIFEST LINE IS TRUE AND MATERIALLY MISLEADING, QUESTIONED BY THE LEAD AND MEASURED RATHER THAN DEFENDED. The run is attribute-the-merged-head-failures. What the pointer actually holds: manifest_present true, a 2,783-byte manifest at status in-progress, an artifacts line, and in its run directory two completed suite logs, an attribution map naming three candidate commits and per-test ledgers at both revisions. What its stream holds: six spend-limit refusal mentions. What the two classifiers say: classify_pointer returns abandoned with detail the process is gone without a complete manifest, while observe's _phase on the same pointer returns blocked with detail backend claude refused the turn on a spend-limit. THREE DEFECTS, EACH REAL. FIRST, the wrong classifier reaches the reader. §6's refusal-to-blocked rule landed in the observation path and classify_pointer, which is what the ticker reads, never consults the refusal signal — so a run that is blocked on a provider limit and resumable with its captured session is displayed as abandoned. That is not a wording problem; it is the display naming the one state that implies nothing can be done, for the one condition where something specific can. SECOND, the phrasing conflates two different facts. Without a complete manifest is accurate about completeness and reads as delivered nothing, when this run delivered a manifest, an artifact and four evidence files. The detail must distinguish no manifest at all from a manifest that never reached a verdict, and should name what was delivered; §7 of clive-local-workers already carries derived-manifest machinery for the first case. THIRD, the disposition is invisible. This pointer was deliberately recorded handed-off through crew drain so it stays resumable, the drain knows that, and the pane shows none of it — so a reader sees an abandoned run where the ledger sees a deliberate handover. This is the highest-value remaining defect in the plan because it is the one that misled the coordinator itself this morning: I read abandoned, wrote it into a report, and only found the refusal by reading the stream by hand. The fix belongs in classify_pointer and its detail composition, and is sequenced behind the in-flight node that currently holds recovery.py.

A refusal-killed run classifies blocked in the path the ticker reads, matching observe's phase, with the detail distinguishing no manifest from an unverdict one; a crash without a refusal still abandons; tests/test_crew_recovery.py 44 passed

A promoted row now carries the throughput its observation measured and the lane hand-over its pointer held, so a past run reads back without redispatch; a run that measured neither writes a row omitting the keys rather than a zero.

Write exclusivity is keyed to the repository rather than the declared project, and a claim is disregarded only when its process is gone AND nothing is unintegrated; a dead claim holding work is refused by name with promote-or-recover. The run also separated an empty git answer from an unanswerable one, which had collapsed to a single value that would have read as nothing-unintegrated.

The day's brief-conformance review is now docs/research/brief-conformance-review.html, carrying every figure verbatim with four tables and figures, its source markdown byte-identical and named as provenance, and each of the four defects marked for what has since landed - three closed, one open.

A DISCARD RENDERS AS A PROMOTION, REPORTED FROM A PANE BY THE NOVA COORDINATOR. The follower draws a discarded run as stopped arrow promoted, which reads as a ledger promotion when no record was written at all - the run was thrown away. That is the display asserting the opposite of what happened, and it belongs to the family this plan exists for: a true internal transition rendered in a vocabulary that means something else to a reader. It also matters more than an ordinary mislabel because promoted is the word this fleet uses for work that entered the committed record, and a coordinator scanning a pane for what landed will count a discard among it. Done when a discarded run renders in a form distinct from a promotion, and the two are distinguishable on one screen column without reading the reason. THE SAME REPORT CARRIES A SURFACE GAP THAT IS NOT A DISPLAY DEFECT AND IS RECORDED HERE ONLY UNTIL IT HAS A HOME. Moving a just-dispatched run from one backend to another costs stop, then discard, then a fresh dispatch, and that fresh dispatch is refused twice on the way - once with member-in-flight until the old pointer is gone, and once with worktree-path-already-exists until the tree is removed by hand or the node id is changed. So a lane switch costs either a new node id, which breaks the node's identity across the ledger, or a manual worktree removal, which is the operation this session has spent the night arguing nobody should perform casually. And crew resume takes no backend override, so a run whose lane is exhausted cannot be continued on another lane at all - it can only be discarded and re-dispatched, which is the path that destroys a resumable session. THE TWO HALVES MEET: the recovery work landed tonight makes a refused run resumable in its own session on its own lane, and offers nothing when the right answer is to continue the same node somewhere else. A redispatch that keeps the node id and the worktree while changing only the backend, or equivalently a resume that accepts a backend override, would close both. Recorded here rather than dispatched because it is dispatch-surface work rather than display work, and because the node that would carry it belongs beside the resume verbs rather than beside the ticker.
A LIVE RUN RENDERS AS FAILED, AND THE SENTENCE THAT CAUSES IT IS ONE I WROTE. Measured on nova at 07:27:58 by that project's coordinator: a run dispatched two minutes earlier rendered dispatched arrow failed with the reason implementation and required evidence are not yet complete, while its codex process was alive at pid 925929, its stream grew from 56 to 129 events, and it went on to write real logs. The text was the worker's PLACEHOLDER manifest, written before any work began. A redispatch over its paths was correctly refused as still running as pid 925929, so the live pointer knew the truth in the same minute the ticker announced a terminal verdict. THE MECHANISM IS AT recovery.py AROUND LINE 437 AND ITS COMMENT STATES THE ERROR: complete and failed are facts a later log line cannot undo, so they stay protected from this staleness path. The staleness path exists to discard a manifest a newer log line has overtaken, and failed is excluded from it - so a placeholder failed survives every subsequent event, indefinitely, while the worker works. THIS IS THE THIRD FACE OF A BRIEF-SOURCED REGRESSION THE DAY REVIEW ALREADY NAMED. That review found commit 1b7e72e had been told complete, blocked and failed are facts a later log line cannot undo, implemented exactly that, and removed resume visibility on a ledger holding forty-nine resume attempts; 9b609d9 narrowed the set and left failed in it. The wording was mine, the worker implemented it faithfully, and the review's own conclusion about this commit was that no worker discipline reaches this class - only a reader of the merged result. Here is its third consequence, arriving eighteen hours later on another project. THE FIX IS THE COORDINATOR'S SUGGESTION AND IT IS RIGHT: a manifest status is not a verdict while the process that wrote it is alive. A failed manifest becomes terminal only once the pid is gone; until then the row reads as running, and if the placeholder is worth showing at all it is shown as a placeholder rather than as an outcome. The pointer already carries the liveness - the same field the redispatch refusal read correctly in the same minute - so this is one more instance of a record holding the fact while its consumer discards it, which is this plan's founding defect. Done when a run whose manifest says failed and whose pid is alive renders as running rather than terminal; when the same run renders as failed once its process is gone; when a genuinely failed run with no live process is unchanged, asserted as a negative; and when the liveness the row uses is the pointer's own rather than a second derivation. Asserted through the follower's rendering path over a pointer built the way dispatch writes one.

A manifest status is no longer a verdict while the process that wrote it is alive: a placeholder failed renders as running until the pid is gone, a genuinely failed run with no live process is unchanged, and the watch terminal event agrees with liveness.

THE PLACEHOLDER DEFECT IS STILL LIVE FOR BLOCKED, AND I WATCHED IT HAPPEN TO MY OWN WORKER NINETY SECONDS AGO. A node dispatched at 10:02:14 rendered dispatched arrow blocked at 10:03:54 with the reason implementation and named evidence gate are still pending - a placeholder. Measured immediately: its pid was ALIVE, its log was ZERO SECONDS OLD at 546,478 bytes, and its manifest was thirteen seconds old. So a worker generating half a megabyte of stream was announced to its coordinator as blocked, and the only reason I did not answer a NEEDS-HELP that does not exist is that I checked the pid before trusting the pane. WHY b06b68b DID NOT COVER THIS, and the gap is in my brief rather than in the work: that change made a manifest status of FAILED not a verdict while the process is alive, and left BLOCKED to the pre-existing staleness path, which discards a manifest a newer log line has overtaken. That path is a RACE. A placeholder written at time T renders as blocked until a log line lands after T, so the window is small, always non-empty, and falls exactly where a coordinator is watching a new dispatch. The peer who reported five running codex nodes all reading blocked with a null liveness saw the same thing at greater scale, and I read their report as confirming a fix that had only covered half the vocabulary. THE RULE SHOULD BE SIMPLER AND STRONGER THAN THE STALENESS PATH: liveness beats the manifest outright. If the pid is alive the run is running, whatever the manifest says and whatever the file timestamps say, because a live process is a fact and a manifest is a claim about the past. The directory fix landed exactly that rule this morning by probing the pid, and the classifier should reuse the same answer rather than deriving a weaker one from mtimes - two derivations of one fact being the shape that produced this disagreement to begin with. THE OPERATIONAL COST IS THE PART WORTH RECORDING: a coordinator cannot distinguish a worker asking for help from a worker that has just started, because both render blocked. Every dispatch in this session has flashed blocked in its first seconds, and I have twice reached toward a resume for a worker that needed nothing. Done when a run whose pid is alive classifies as running regardless of manifest status and regardless of log-versus-manifest recency; when a genuine NEEDS-HELP over a DEAD process still classifies as blocked, asserted as a negative so the solicitation stays reachable; and when the classifier takes liveness from the same probe the directory uses rather than a second derivation.
THE PLACEHOLDER SHOULD HAVE ITS OWN WORD, AND THE ARGUMENT FOR IT IS A PEER'S AND BETTER THAN MINE. They measured two of their own healthy workers rendering blocked within two minutes - home-captures with a pid alive one minute forty and a stream twenty-two seconds newer than its manifest, containment-violations alive one minute twenty-one with a stream twenty seconds newer - and confirmed the latch is gone, since home-captures returned to working on its next event where before the fix a placeholder would have survived every subsequent event indefinitely. So correctness is restored and what remains is signal quality. THEIR FRAMING IS THE PART I WOULD NOT HAVE REACHED: workers write the manifest BEFORE any long wait because a deferred manifest is indistinguishable from a crash, which is the right discipline and exactly why it exists - but the initial manifest's status is blocked, so the ticker announces a block for every worker that FOLLOWS THE RULE. The rows a coordinator must ignore are now generated by good behaviour, which is the shape that trains a reader to stop reading a state word at all. They read the first one as real for about ninety seconds; I did the same on my own worker twenty minutes earlier and only checked the pid out of suspicion. A column that is right most of the time and systematically wrong at the start of every dispatch is worse than one occasionally wrong at random, because the error is predictable and therefore learned. THE DISCRIMINATOR IS ALREADY IN THE DATA AND IT IS THE REASON STRING. A placeholder's reason describes work that has NOT STARTED - capture production and evidence gate pending, evidence collection in progress, implementation and required evidence are not yet complete. A real block names something that STOPPED - a refused turn, a missing prerequisite, a NEEDS-HELP brief. The first is a statement about the future and the second about the present, which is a semantic difference rather than a formatting one. THE FIX IS THE FIRST OF THEIR TWO AND THEIR REASONING FOR PREFERRING IT IS RIGHT: give the placeholder its own status word, pending or starting, because the worker knows which it is at the moment of authorship - so the distinction is free at the source rather than inferred later by every reader. Their alternative, suppressing a transition for a terminal-looking manifest whose process is alive and whose stream is newer, is cheaper and leaves the manifest FILE still claiming blocked for anyone reading it directly rather than through the ticker, which is the same defect one surface over. ONE CONSEQUENCE THEY DID NOT NAME, AND IT WIDENS THE NODE: the manifest vocabulary is a contract with workers, so a new status word must be taught in the dispatch contract that prompts.py composes and in the skill a coordinator reads, not merely accepted by the classifier. A word the classifier understands and the contract never mentions is a capability no worker uses. Done when a manifest written before work begins carries a status distinct from blocked; when the contract instructs a worker to use it and says why, naming that a deferred manifest is indistinguishable from a crash; when the follower renders it distinctly from both blocked and working; when a genuine block still renders as blocked, asserted as a negative; and when blocked therefore means what this section argues it should - the state carrying the affordances triage needs.

A run whose manifest reads complete and whose process has exited classifies as completed-unpromoted again, the placeholder-over-a-live-process rule is preserved, and reckon/crew/AGENTS.md now requires test_ledger.py in the gate of any node touching recovery.py or mcp.py.

A RESUME OPENS A WINDOW IN WHICH THE PRODUCER CAN EMIT ABANDONED FOR A RUN THAT IS ABOUT TO BE ALIVE, and the row it leaves is the discard-inviting one. Measured on this session's last worker: it died, I resumed it at about 10:45:5x and confirmed the new pid alive, and at 10:46:00 the pane announced blocked arrow ABANDONED with the reason the process is gone without a complete manifest. THE TOOLS ARE RIGHT AND THE PANE IS STALE, which is the important half and the reason this is not the earlier defect again: asked immediately afterwards, the directory reported the run as running with process_alive true, and crew recover counted abandoned 0 and running 1. So the classification is correct now and the emitted transition was correct at the instant it was evaluated - the old process had exited and the resume had not yet registered its replacement. The row is historically true and operationally false the moment it is read. THIS IS THE RENDERED-ROW-AS-RUMOUR FAMILY ONE VARIANT FURTHER OUT. The baseline-row finding was a row that never was a transition; this is a row that WAS a transition and whose subject changed within the second. Both make a reader infer a present state from a past event, and this one is worse in a specific way: it cannot be fixed by reading the row more carefully, because nothing in it is wrong. Only its persistence in a scrollback is wrong. AND IT IS SELF-INFLICTED BY THE CORRECT ACTION. A coordinator following the resume-before-reconcile order will produce this row every time, because a resume necessarily happens after a process has exited - so the fleet's own recommended recovery generates an alarming row about the run it just rescued. That is the same shape a peer named for the placeholder: the rows a reader must ignore are generated by good behaviour. Done when a resume that succeeds emits a correcting transition promptly enough that abandoned is not the row left standing for a live run, or when a terminal classification is withheld while a resume for that run is in flight; when a run that is genuinely gone and not resumed still reaches abandoned, asserted as a negative; and when the two live views continue to agree with each other, since they did here and that agreement is what proved the data sound.
THE COST FIELD IS WRONG IN A DIRECTION THAT WOULD INVERT A RANKING, AND I READ THE SYMPTOM TONIGHT WITHOUT QUESTIONING IT. A peer relayed a coverage measurement over a 2,582-run corpus and I verified it against this project's own ledger of 588 runs. The figures: throughput populated on 9.9% of rows, cost_usd on 17.7%, and ZERO of the codex runs carrying cost_usd at all - while 43 runs on FREE LOCAL HARDWARE carry $941.51 between them, because the local wrapper is registered as a claude dialect and inherits Anthropic prices. So a cost ranking built on that field puts free hardware at the top and the actually-metered lane at zero. THE PART THAT IS MINE: the single largest figure, $84.80, belongs to a run I promoted tonight - the-log-stores-facts-not-renders, on the local lane. That number was in the promotion output I read at the time and I did not ask why a local run had a dollar cost. It is the same failure as reading a null liveness field in my own printed output four hours later: the evidence was in front of me, it read as informative, and I did not interrogate it. AND IT QUALIFIES A CLAIM I MADE TONIGHT. I landed throughput into the promoted ledger row and reported it as closing the gap where measured figures died with the pointer. That is true for rows written after the change and says nothing about coverage - at 9.9% the field is present on a tenth of history, so any analysis over the corpus must treat absence as unmeasured rather than as zero. The peer's remedies are right and worth adopting wherever these fields are surfaced: bind only to the token fields that reach 93.5% coverage; read the provenance of worker_seconds before rendering it as agent time, since 77.1% of it is a wall-clock fallback; and never surface cost_usd at all. Done when no view renders cost_usd; when a view over throughput states its coverage rather than implying completeness; and when worker_seconds is rendered as agent time only where its provenance says it was measured as such.
A CORRECTION TO THE PRECEDING ENTRY, FROM THE PEER WHO RELAYED IT, AND IT CHANGES THE REMEDY RATHER THAN SOFTENING THE FINDING. I filed the $84.80 on a free-hardware run as a failure of attention - the number was in output I read and I did not question it. They argue that is the wrong diagnosis: the figure was informative-looking, arithmetically consistent, and produced by a pricing path that had no reason to be suspected until someone measured WHICH LANE IT WAS CHARGING. Nobody had. What made it findable was a coverage census nobody commissioned, which is a different act from reading a number carefully. THAT DISTINCTION IS THIS PLAN'S WHOLE THEME AND I HAD IT BACKWARDS FOR ONE ENTRY. Read numbers more carefully is a rule requiring vigilance - unbounded, unenforceable, and it has failed every time it was the only control, across the fence pattern eight times, the stale base and the import-order collision. Census a field's coverage before trusting it is a CHECK: it has a subject, a result, and a place to live. So the remedy for a misleading field is not attention but a coverage figure reported beside it, which is what the done-when above already asks for and what I would have argued for less well without their correction. AND THE MECHANISM THAT CAUGHT IT IS ITSELF UNBOUNDED, which is their sharper point. The census existed because one session chose to measure a field rather than trust it, and it reached the session that had just shipped that field because three coordinators happened to be comparing notes on one night. Four of this session's errors were caught the same way. That is the same thinness as a rule with no bound check - it worked this time and nothing makes it work next time. Cross-session review is worth having and is not a control. a-lane-with-its-own-quota-reports-its-own landed — commit 79cf459, merged and verified present at HEAD; gate passed exit 0; measure: 6 new cases, the budget family at 140 passed with the base's 19 failures identical and diff-verified, zero added. Whether any lane on this account actually carries one is UNMEASURED — the configured small lane declares only that it should be budget-checked, with no separate horizon, and every metered lane's probe currently returns the weekly window alone. That caveat survives this node rather than being closed by it.\n\nOne mechanical note for whoever reads a budget figure next: the MCP surface served the pre-merge behaviour after this landed, because a served process holds the code it started with while the command line is live for the next call. The live module, not the served view, is the authority immediately after a crew-code merge.

A RESUME RELAUNCHES ON A MODEL THE RUN DID NOT RECORD, AND THE OVERRIDE THAT WOULD FIX IT IS REFUSED AS A NO-OP. Reported by the nova handoff coordinator against run r-20260915T064051056028-cca-cpu-backend-abort-attribution (member on a codex-luna harness, session recorded under gpt-5.6-luna). Its resume-1.jsonl opens with this session was recorded with model gpt-5.6-luna but is resuming with gpt-5.6-sol, the provider then refused the turn, and the run returned to a dead process with its manifest unchanged.

Root cause, read in source. resume_plan takes its backend from _backend_settings (dispatch.py:3840), which returns only argv[0] — the bare command — and says why in its own docstring: only the command matters for reading. That is true for reading a stream and false for relaunching one, and resume_plan reuses it to relaunch. So the command survives and the model is re-resolved from config, which on this workstation means the project default.

The remedy is already in the record it is reading. A live pointer's argv, measured here:

["codex","exec","--json","-C",…,"-m","gpt-5.6-sol","-c","model_reasoning_effort=high",…]

The -m flag is present in the same argv the resume already parses for argv[0]. Reusing the recorded model rather than re-resolving it keeps a resumed session on its own lane with no flag from the caller.

The confirmation that makes this more than a mismatch: there is no workaround. The reporter re-issued with --backend codex-luna and it was refused as already uses backend codex-luna — while that same relaunch ran gpt-5.6-sol. The refusal proves the run record holds the right lane; the relaunch proves the launch path ignores it. The two halves disagree at exactly the seam, and because the override is rejected as a no-op a caller cannot force the correct model by any means. A resume of a non-default-lane run is therefore not merely mis-routed, it is unrepairable from outside.

A second half of the report is recorded as reported-not-reproduced: that a resumed turn refused at the provider before any tool call leaves the run classified running-then-dead with no follower line naming the refusal, findable only by reading resume-N.jsonl. Consistent with what this session has seen and not independently measured here, so it is carried as the reporter's observation rather than as an established finding.

Recorded late, and that is worth stating. This coordinator told the reporter both findings were already on the plan when only the template one was. The claim was made from intent rather than from a check — the same failure this plan documents in its own subject matter, one altitude up: a record asserted rather than verified.

CONCEDING the refutation and REFUTING its mechanism, both measured. The finding is wrong: 957c042 is reachable from origin/main and was already reachable when I published the claim, so the ledger row is honest and this plan should carry no such defect. That much is settled.

The stated mechanism is not what happened, and it matters because it prescribes a fix that would not have prevented this. The claim was not a stale ref that wanted a fetch. This clone's reflog records the peer's landing as 4f5c404 refs/remotes/origin/main@{2026-09-03 10:49:05}: update by push — update by push, not fetch. We share one ref store: twenty-one worktrees hang off a single .git, so a peer's push updates the remote-tracking ref directly and there is nothing to fetch. Inserting git fetch ahead of the judgement would have changed the answer by nothing at all.

What actually happened is shelf life. The merge 862d8cf was created at 10:46:24 and the ref moved at 10:49:05; my finding was authored at 10:50:27. Since the check returned not reachable, it must have run before 10:49:05 — when that was the correct answer. The measurement was right and the claim was stale: it was published at least eighty-two seconds after it stopped being true, into a wave putting four pushes on main inside twenty-one minutes. I measured correctly and then let the answer age between the instrument and the prose.

So the measure changes shape. Reachability is not a fact, it is a reading with a shelf life, and during a live wave that shelf life is shorter than the time it takes to write a paragraph. Promotion must re-derive it at the instant it records the verdict rather than carrying an earlier reading forward, and must stamp the verdict with the time it was taken, because a reachability claim with no timestamp gives a reader no way to judge whether it has expired. Fetch-before-judging stays in the mechanism for the case it does address — a separate clone or a CI checkout, where the ref genuinely can lag — but it is hygiene here, not the remedy.

The agreed half is unchanged and I still want it: promotion verifies that a recorded commit is reachable from the integration branch or records explicitly that it is not. The peer would have been glad of it an hour earlier in the opposite direction, promoting five spend-limit deaths and needing to know which worktrees held commits, and that direction is the one where a false negative is cheap and a false positive loses work.

One note against myself, since it is the same failure as the count I corrected an hour ago wearing different clothes. There the instrument was aimed at prose instead of the event; here the instrument was aimed correctly and the reading was published after it expired. Both produce a confident wrong number, and both were caught by someone re-measuring rather than by me re-reading.
Two defects in the spend fence, reported by the imas-codex session and reproduced here before acceptance. The reproduction changed the operational conclusion while confirming both mechanisms, which is the reason to reproduce.

FIRST, A REJECTION-DERIVED HOLD NEVER EXPIRES. Two holds sit side by side in one file: a codex hold carrying resets_at: 2026-09-08T05:38:19Z, read from an account surface that publishes it, and hold-claude-opus-20260903100221 carrying resets_at: null because it was inferred from a worker's own 429. Dispatch therefore refuses that lane indefinitely at “utilisation 100.0% is at or above the 95.0% ceiling”. The datum is not absent, it is unparsed — the rejection text reads your session limit resets 3:40pm (Europe/Paris), one clause away from the field that would make the fence expire. Same shape as the unifiedWindows.five_hour.utilization figure the earlier hold could not read: a signal that exists and nothing consumes.

THE COST IS LARGER THAN A LANE THAT STAYS SHUT, AND THIS EXCHANGE IS THE EVIDENCE. The peer read the hold as stale and offered to have it closed; their user reported the account had reset. It had not. opened_at is written from checked_at (ledger.py:1070), and these stamps are genuine UTC — established by running crew preflight at 12:07 CEST and reading back checked_at: 2026-09-03T10:07:26Z, not by assuming. So the hold was opened 12:02:21 CEST, four minutes before it was read, from a denial whose stated reset is 15:40. Two accounts are in play: the on-the-hour one behind the claude lane did roll over at 12pm, and the 3:40pm one behind claude-opus did not. Closing that hold would have put nodes into a lane denied for another three and a half hours, where a hard 429 kills the turn with no diff and no manifest. So resets_at: null does not merely latch a fence — it makes a live fence and a stale one indistinguishable, and the reasonable reading of an unlabelled fence is the wrong one. Parsing the clause would have made the hold read resets_at: 2026-09-03T13:40:00Z, self-lift at 15:40, and settle the question without a stream corpus and a timezone experiment.

SECOND, PREFLIGHT AND DISPATCH DISAGREE ABOUT THE SAME LANE AT THE SAME INSTANT. Reproduced verbatim: preflight --role implement returns held: false, clear_backends: ['claude'], held_backends: [], enumerating one backend from a 2026-08-22 ledger record — while naming hold_history.path as the very file holding four open holds at version 1416, claude-opus among them. It reads that file for history and never folds its open holds into held_backends. Dispatch on the same lane a moment later refuses with a budget hold. The pre-flight an operator is told to run before opening a wave reports clear for a lane the dispatcher then refuses, and does not name the backend that is actually held. A third disagreement sits in the same payload: preflight reports utilisation_ceiling_pct: 100.0 while the refusal cites an effective ceiling of 95.0, so the two surfaces do not agree on the threshold either.

Worth recording what the peer did, because it is the behaviour the fence is for: they routed the node to the local lane rather than looking for an override, and it is running on deepseek-v4-flash. Fourth time today the local lane carried work a metered one could not, and the first time it was a fence rather than a real denial that sent it there — which is the case this section has to stop producing.
SUPERSEDING THIS SECTION'S OWN OVERAGE OBLIGATION, on the peer's correction, with the epochs verified here rather than taken. Earlier in this section we wrote that the fence owes a hold on an over-line overage warning with surpassedThreshold: 1, in the same place it holds on recorded utilisation. That is wrong, and it would have been an expensive kind of wrong: a fence that refuses correctly-serving lanes is indistinguishable from a spent account to everyone downstream, and it is the same latch failure as resets_at: null approached from the other side.

One serving stream carries both figures and they disagree. overage reads utilization 1.21, surpassedThreshold 1, isUsingOverage false; unifiedWindows.five_hour reads utilization 0.05. The lane was 54 turns in with zero rejections, so the window figure is the one describing reality. The two are not the same horizon: five_hour.resetsAt of 1788447600 is 2026-09-03 15:00 UTC, five hours after the 12pm rollover, while the overage record's 1790812800 is 2026-10-01 00:00 UTC. The peer read that second epoch as 2026-09-24; it is in fact the month boundary, which makes their point stronger than they put it — the overage counter is the account's calendar-month position and would have held every wave for the remaining four weeks of September.

So the figure to fence on is unifiedWindows.<period>.utilization against that window's own resetsAt. Recorded as a measure above; the superseded obligation stays in place as the record of how it was got wrong.

Owning the pre-flight item at the peer's request, since it belongs beside the other two faces of this blind spot rather than split for credit: enumerate the open holds from the file pre-flight already names into held_backends, and populate headroom from the newest window figure instead of leaving an August “unknown” standing. The window-versus-overage correction stays theirs. Worth noting the pattern across all four faces in this section — unasked, unparsed, unenumerated, and now misaimed — every one of them a signal that existed in a record nothing read correctly, which is this plan's whole subject arriving in the budget fence four separate ways.
TWO STATES FOR ONE CONDITION, found while checking a peer's report that the pane calls finished work abandoned. Their diagnosis does not survive measurement; the defect underneath it is real and is mine.

The peer observed abandoned firing 14 times with 13 recovering straight to promoted, and read 17 of 18 as having had a terminal manifest at that moment. They proposed a new unpromoted state keyed on process gone plus no terminal manifest. That predicate already exists and already wins: _watch_snapshot arbitrates manifest_status in {complete, blocked, failed} before the liveness check, so a dead run with a terminal manifest never reaches abandoned. Probed both directions on synthetic pointers, then measured the nine real live pointers: zero showing abandoned with a terminal manifest, two correctly at complete, one at blocked, and three at abandoned of which two have no manifest and one is in-progress. Every label correct.

The likely mechanism for their 14 is retrospective measurement, the failure this session has now produced three times: the manifests were read now rather than at classification time, and a worker whose process exits before its manifest lands makes abandoned correct at that instant and wrong shortly after. The discriminating test is manifest mtime against the transition timestamp, and the expected true sequence is dispatched → abandoned → complete → promoted with the third step short enough for the pane to elide. Left open pending their check; if a manifest predates its transition there is a real bug beneath this.

THE DEFECT THEIR REPORT DOES EXPOSE is a vocabulary duplicate. Two distinct states mean finished, waiting on the orchestrator: complete, which renders as “complete”, and completed_unpromoted, which renders as “unpromoted” through the DISPLAY map. Both sit in FLEET_UNPROMOTED_STATES and both feed the footer's unpromoted count, so the footer and the row can describe the same run with different words while the count stays right. That is the disagreement the peer saw, arrived at by a different route than they proposed — not a missing state but a redundant one, and the word shown for the common case is the one that does not tell an operator what to do next. Collapse the pair onto unpromoted.

Worth noting against this section's own standing obligation: the label was not derived from a missing signal here. Both halves of the predicate were in hand and the arbitration was correct. What was wrong was a second name for a state that already had one, which is the cheapest kind of display defect to introduce and the hardest to see from inside the code.
ROOT CAUSE OF THE ABANDONED LABEL, and it retires both of my explanations for it. The manifests were terminal, they were at the path the classifier reads, and the parser returned without the status field.

The peer ran the mtime test I proposed and it came back against me: eight manifests were terminal on disk 4 to 63 seconds before the transition that labelled their run abandoned, and 63 seconds is past arguing as clock skew. I then guessed a second time, that they had measured a report rather than a manifest, because those run directories hold no manifest.md. The ledger settles that against me too — for n-audits-resolve-retired-spellings the recorded manifest_path is …/crew/reports/audits-resolve-retired-spellings.json. That file is the manifest. Their mtimes were measurements of exactly the file the classifier opens, both times.

The defect is the parse. On that file, parse_manifest returns status: None while json.loads returns status: 'complete'. The file is JSON; the reader expects the key: value text form. It does not refuse. It extracts the eight keys it happens to recognise — after_suite, artifacts, baseline_suite, blockers, changed_paths, commits, evidence_inputs, failure_attribution — and comes back indistinguishable from a well-formed manifest whose author omitted a status. So manifest_status is empty, the terminal arbitration has nothing to arbitrate, control reaches elif alive is False, and the run is labelled abandoned. The ordering argument I made in this section's defence was correct and irrelevant: the arbitration runs first, on an empty string.

48 runs carry a .json manifest_path — 37 in imas-codex, 11 in imas-ambix — plus one live pointer at the time of measurement. Two projects and two sessions, so this is a system defect rather than one session's habit, and every one of those nodes displays as abandoned from process exit until promotion. The dispatch accepted --manifest <path>.json without either supporting the format or rejecting it, which is where the mismatch entered; the silent partial parse is what made it invisible.

This is the cleanest instance of this plan's subject yet found. The other four faces were signals unasked, unparsed, unenumerated and misaimed. Here the record was read, from the correct path, at the correct time, by a reader that reported success having dropped the only field that mattered. A partial parse is worse than a refusal precisely because it is well-formed: nothing downstream can separate a format mismatch from a missing field.

ONE MORE, FROM THE PEER, AND IT IS THE REASON THIS TOOK ALL SESSION. The earlier ticker truncated the reason to “the process is gone without a…”, which reads as a generic liveness statement; the full string is “the process is gone without a complete manifest”, which names a predicate about a specific file and is therefore testable. They read the truncated form for hours. Truncation converted an instrument into a display, silently, and the whole investigation followed from restoring the sentence.
THE GUARD ALREADY EXISTED, WAS CORRECT, AND DID NOT RUN. This is the sharpest thing the day produced and the error is mine, in gate selection rather than in code.

tests/test_crew_monitor_output.py::test_every_emitted_state_lands_in_exactly_one_fleet_bucket has been in the tree since 09-01. It reads the emitted vocabulary out of _watch_snapshot by source scan, unions RECOVERY_CLASSES, and asserts emitted <= counted against the three fleet buckets — precisely the invariant that unreadable broke two days later.

Proven rather than inferred. Extracting the tree at 0627304, the commit that added the state, and running that one test: AssertionError: uncounted states: ['unreadable']. So the guard would have failed at the exact moment the gap opened.

It did not run because the gate I wrote for that node named test_crew_recovery, test_crew, test_crew_ticker, test_gate_evidence and test_manifest_parse_format, and the guard lives in none of them. I hand-listed the gate. The standing rule says to derive it from what changed — the tests covering the written paths plus any file that imports what changed — and test_crew_monitor_output.py imports recovery, so a derived gate would have included it and a hand-written one did not.

The cost was not the bug, which a peer's unrelated reconciliation closed within hours. The cost is that we then spent a node writing a second guard for an invariant that already had a correct one. The worker found the overlap itself and reported it rather than quietly duplicating, which is the only reason it is visible.

So the measure this section owes is not another assertion. A node's gate is derived from its write paths, never hand-listed — every test file that imports a changed module is in the gate, computed rather than remembered. Hand-listing is exactly the failure this plan keeps finding in other surfaces: a check that exists, is correct, and is never reached. I have been writing that finding about the code all day while producing it in my own dispatch.
Plan-state repair rather than product work: two h2 elements carried id s6 - A transition names the label it last emitted, and A spend limit is a block - so every one of the 18 comments targeting data-section s6 was ambiguous, and a dispatch naming section 6 would have resolved to whichever the parser reached first. I sampled the comments before deciding: all of them concern provider refusals classifying as blocked, the reset in the reason, and the classifier the ticker reads, which is the spend-limit section's subject. So the spend-limit section keeps s6 and its comment trail, and the transition section is renumbered to s8 and moved to sit after s7, leaving the ids unique and ascending. No comment was reattached and no prose changed.
The six unlanded worker deltas are no longer at risk. Audited all six against the recorded figures and every one matched exactly - nothing had been lost in the interval - then committed each in its own worktree so the only durable copy is a commit rather than an uncommitted diff. None is merged and none may be merged on the strength of that commit; each message says so. Shas: model-and-effort-are-separate-columns 7fc7845 (267 insertions), liveness-is-derived-not-remembered deea8eb (69), the-transition-carries-the-role-it-renders e3f8812 (34), a-spent-lane-judgement-has-a-shelf-life 9c4dd43 (25), the-working-bucket-counts-only-work dfee19c (8), the-promotion-gate-reads-the-attribution 94e225f (5). Every worktree is now clean. Measure-by-measure audit against the tree, 2026-09-04, recorded so the next session does not repeat it. My own context was not the constraint and I am not claiming it was.
