LANDED cde933c — a manifest key is read at the top level only, and a bracketed list decodes to clean elements. Reported by an imas-codex fleet with the receipts, reproduced here before dispatch, verified here after merge. THE DEFECT: reports.py stripped each line before applying the key regex, so an indented key matched exactly like a column-0 one and overwrote it. Worse than reported — _is_block_indicator on an empty value returns False, so a parent like failure_attribution never opened a block at all and every nested key under it was read as top level. Reproduced in three distinct manifest shapes; all three overwrote. WHY IT WAS EXPENSIVE RATHER THAN UNTIDY: a well-formed manifest cites, under failure_attribution, the commits the node was INVESTIGATING. Those were reported as the node's own by both crew live and crew drain, and the rendered next action offered them as a runnable command with the JSON brackets still in it. A coordinator followed that instruction and committed a ledger row naming two wrong shas. The defect therefore selected for the manifests that do failure attribution properly, which is to say the best ones, and the surface did not merely display a wrong value — it instructed a person to write it down. VERIFIED AGAINST MY OWN REPRODUCTION rather than the node's tests: all three shapes now return the top-level commit, including the one whose parent key carries an inline value and the one where a blank line precedes the indented key. The regression a naive column-0 anchor would have caused is absent — indented list items still append to their key, and a key following such a list still parses, which matters because real manifests carry test_logs as indented hyphen items. Its own manifest, read through the parser it changed, resolves to its own sha with no bracket or quote surviving. Suite judged on delta: 17 failures identical at base and head, all environmental mount, watcher and sandbox-delivery cases that cannot pass in a worker tier, none in manifest parsing, zero added.
LANDED 9e681aa — a spent local lane can now be known. THE DEFECT was a guard that could not fire at any level of exhaustion: _ClaudeDialect.observe read exactly one record kind for budget, rate_limit_event, and the clive local backend never emits one. It writes system/api_retry records carrying error rate_limit and status 429. So a run that died of rate limiting was recorded with detail 'no rate-limit event in the stream yet', refusal false, headroom unknown — and since unknown correctly never holds a wave, the next wave dispatched straight back into the lane that had just shed workers. Measured tonight across four dead runs in two projects. This is the asymmetric direction: a stale hold announces itself, a blind one is indistinguishable from a clear lane. THE FIX counts the retry records and lets the terminal shape deliver the verdict. VERIFIED at the merged head against real recorded streams rather than fixtures — two runs shed tonight report refusal true, headroom known, and a detail naming the count; two runs that each carried SEVEN retries and finished with work committed report refusal false; two runs live at the moment of checking report false. THE FALSIFIER IS THE RESULT THAT MATTERS, because retrying is not refusing: a fix keyed on the presence of retries would have marked every busy lane spent and held the entire fleet, and it would have passed any test written only against the dead runs. TWO THINGS THE WORKER GOT RIGHT BEYOND THE BRIEF. It excludes 529 overload retries from the count, a discrimination nobody specified. And it declined to refuse the mid-flight-death shape the brief listed, on its own evidence that a live peer carrying seven retries mid-flight is indistinguishable BY STREAM from a dead one — that verdict needs the process table, so it belongs in the classification layer, outside this node's fence. It named the layer, stopped at its boundary, and surfaced the retry count in budget.detail so that layer has the signal when it is built. Refusing there would have been the same over-firing error one layer along. DO NOT read a retry count as a magnitude to threshold: ten is the retry budget for a single API call, not for a run, and the four dead runs carry 10, 15, 18 and 20.
LANDED 1af9bb1 — a resumed run no longer reports itself blocked on the limit that ended its predecessor. Reported by a peer session watching one of its own runs: refused on a spend limit at 20:46, resumed at 21:12, and while the resumed process generated normally the live view still read blocked with the original spend-limit detail. THE MECHANISM was a two-line interaction across two files, and the interesting part is that it was not where the reporter looked. _stream_refusal_block tests the pointer's PERSISTED budget first and only falls through to reading the current log when that is absent; resume() updated pid, phase, attempt, attempt_kind, attempt_started_at and log_path, but left budget untouched. The log_path field already pointed at the resume stream, so the fall-through would have returned nothing — it simply never ran. resume() now resets the fold to the honest unknown-headroom state, because a new attempt has made no observations and should not inherit its predecessor's. VERIFIED, and the third assertion is the one that carries the risk: a resumed run whose NEW stream itself records a refusal blocks again on that fresh evidence. A change that merely stopped reporting refusals would have satisfied the two obvious tests and silently destroyed the blocked state this section exists to create — the same over-firing error the budget-instrument node refused to make one layer along. The control holds too: a refusal that was never resumed still classifies blocked with its reason and its resume action intact. Suite delta across 18 dispatch-importing files: 20 failures and errors identical at base and head, none added, none removed. THE HAZARD IT ENDS is not cosmetic — a coordinator scanning the live view for blocked rows would have tried to resume a run that was already working.
LANDED 431653e — one predicate now decides what unreconciled means, and both fences call it. closure_disposition_valid lives in recovery.py and is called by overdue_unreconciled_runs and by drain, replacing two independent definitions that had never consulted each other: drain honoured a recorded handed-off disposition and ignored age; dispatch judged on classification and age and never read closure_disposition at all. THE DEFECT, measured by an imas-codex fleet: drain reported unreconciled_runs 0 for three pointers carrying valid handed-off dispositions while dispatch refused those same three with exit 6. This is worse than a disagreement between displays. The ship skill directs a coordinator to read the drain view and require zero before closing a session, so the fence a closure signature rests on was the PERMISSIVE one — their outgoing coordinator signed a sprint closure and committed it, attesting to a state the other surface denied. A fence satisfiable on one surface and refused on the other is not a fence. VERIFIED across all eight cases, and the two that carry the risk both hold: a terminal pointer with NO disposition is still refused on both surfaces, which is the forgotten-work protection the dispatch fence exists for and precisely what a careless relaxation would have destroyed; and a still-working disposition on a pointer that has since gone terminal expires on both, so dispatch does not become the weaker surface. An unrecognised disposition fails closed. Suite delta across 32 importing files: 33 failures identical at base and head, zero added. SCOPE NOTE, and the error is the coordinator's: the fence named recovery.py and the test file, but the stated goal — one predicate both surfaces call — was unreachable inside it, because drain lives in runs.py. The worker made the minimal change the goal required, six lines confined to drain(), and disclosed the deviation in its manifest rather than leaving it to be found. Promoted with the path accepted. A done-when must not span files the node is not fenced to write; this is the second time that error has been made here and the first time a worker resolved it by disclosure rather than by blocking.
WAVE VERIFICATION — zero attributable regressions across the eight commits, measured together for the first time. Each had been gated only in its own worktree against its own base, which is exactly the measurement that cannot see what a merge does. Full suite at the merged head: 3273 passed, 107 failures. At base 7351b63: 3223 passed, 111 failures. 104 failures are identical at both revisions and are the sandbox tier. Added at head 3, removed at head 7. WHAT MAKES THIS REPORT TRUSTWORTHY IS THAT NEITHER NUMBER WAS PRESENTED AS A WAVE EFFECT. The seven removals are an artifact of the verifier's own method — it extracted the base with git archive, so that tree has no .git and the hatch-vcs version tests error there. It identified this itself and refused to claim seven fixed tests, which a less careful census would have reported as improvement. The three additions are browser cases in files no commit in the wave touches, with a sibling width variant passing in the same run. COORDINATOR VERIFICATION, and it reaches the same conclusion by a different route than the census used, which is the point of checking: the failure text names its own cause rather than merely timing out. It reports BrowserProbeError navigation-never-completed, places the diagnosed boundary at Chrome's Network Service above Internet stream-socket creation, and prints inotify instances 28 of 128. This project has both browser-gate causes written down — inotify exhaustion at the 128 ceiling, or the loopback-HTTP navigation fault — and 28 of 128 rules out the first. The second carries a standing evidence report, and the assertion itself states the case remains owed against a host runtime change. So it is a known host condition, not a flake and not a regression. SIZING NOTE, the coordinator's error: the node ran 2h42m against a 45m budget. Its two full suites alone are 941s and 1408s, so no part of the work could have fitted the number I gave it. STATE CORRECTION to the standing followup about six worktrees holding unlanded deltas. It says the content is uncommitted and that the worktrees are the only durable copy. That was true when written and is no longer: the deltas were committed in place by a later session, and a worktree audit tonight reports all six as clean with unintegrated heads — for example 7fc7845 'split the joined model-effort cell into separate columns', deea8eb 'derive watcher liveness from the process table', dfee19c 'keep a stopped run out of the working bucket'. The risk therefore changed shape and got smaller. It was that a cleanup gating on reachability alone would discard uncommitted work while reporting success; it is now that eight commits exist which no branch contains. Both need action, but not the same action and not with the same urgency — the first is a race against any cleanup, the second is a merge decision that will wait. Tonight's cleanup removed twenty worktrees and held all sixteen of these, eight dirty and eight unintegrated, because it gates on clean AND integrated rather than on reachable; none of the six was ever a candidate for removal. THE GENERAL POINT, and it is this plan's own subject: a followup is a record with an observation time, and this one had been true for long enough to read as current. A note describing state must say when it was measured, because the reader cannot tell a stale description from a live one, and this one would have sent someone to rescue work that had already been saved.
ROOT-CAUSE LEAD, found while landing 4d9e71b and worth more than that node. Six of the seven pre-existing failures in the measured set are in tests/test_crew_finished_display.py — the contract tests for how a run is classified — and they are byte-identical in the census logs at base 7351b63 and at the merged head, so they predate this session entirely. One of them is named test_dispatched_with_no_manifest_and_a_dead_process_resolves_to_abandoned. THE TESTS THAT ENCODE HOW A RUN WITH NO MANIFEST SHOULD BE CLASSIFIED HAVE BEEN RED FOR SOME TIME, which is the answer to why a classifier that calls live runs abandoned shipped without anyone noticing. A guard whose own contract tests are failing is not a guard; it is a record of an intention. TWO DEFECTS FOUND IN THE SAME NINE LINES, and an imas-codex fleet paid for both tonight with two duplicated nodes on a metered lane. First, classify_pointer evaluates its terminal arm before its aliveness arm and the terminal arm never consults liveness — and its stated action is to inspect the worktree and redispatch. So the surface does not merely fail to notice a live run, it advises duplicating one, and a competent reader following that advice is behaving correctly. Second, and more general: process_alive returns False on PermissionError, but a PermissionError from os.kill means the process EXISTS and belongs to another user. That is proof of life recorded as death, and on a workstation carrying several fleets it is the normal condition for a peer's worker rather than an edge case. THE EVIDENCE IS PRESERVED AND DISCRIMINATING: three runs, one genuine absence and two that were alive and finished 17 and 24 minutes after being declared dead. In both live cases the manifest predates the stream's final record by about a minute, so nothing was written late and no poll interval would have helped — which rules out a re-read as the remedy and leaves positive proof of death as the only fix that works. Where liveness cannot be proven the classifier should say so and leave the pointer alone: an honest unknown is annoying, a confident abandoned gets acted on.
CORRECTION TO A LEDGER ROW I JUST WROTE. The promotion outcome for lane-refusal-tests-follow-the-meteredness-split states that "both original names survive". They do not. The two tests were RENAMED: test_rate_limit_retries_ending_in_an_error_result_report_a_refusal became test_rate_limit_retries_ending_in_an_error_result_report_lane_backpressure, and test_claude_retries_speak_the_same_wire_language_as_the_local_lane became test_identical_wire_records_resolve_by_meteredness. Eight test functions either side, the other six untouched, nothing deleted, skipped or xfailed. THE SUBSTANCE OF THE CLAIM HOLDS AND THE SENTENCE DOES NOT, which is why this is worth writing down rather than letting stand: each test kept its claim and changed its expectation, and the renames are an improvement, because a test named for a verdict it no longer asserts is a worse defect than the rename. Had they kept the old names, the first would be called report_a_refusal while asserting backpressure. HOW I CAME TO WRITE SOMETHING UNVERIFIED INTO A PERMANENT RECORD, because the mechanism is more useful than the error: I put the grep that checks the names and the crew complete that asserts them in the SAME tool call. The check's output therefore arrived after the claim had already been written, and the grep returned nothing — which was the answer, and I could not act on it. A verification bundled with the action that depends on it is not a verification; it is a formality that runs alongside. This is the same family as reading a result that matches what you expected: the fix is not a better check but a separated one. Run the check, read it, then act.
SIX DEFECTS QUEUED BEHIND recovery.py AND runs.py, each measured on a live fleet today and recorded here so they survive a context boundary rather than living in a message channel. A node currently holds both files for the state-vocabulary repair; these follow it. (1) LIVENESS READS A ZOMBIE AS ALIVE. process_alive uses os.kill(pid, 0), which SUCCEEDS against a zombie because the process-table entry still exists, so a dead run awaiting reap is reported running and crew resume refuses it. Confirmed against a real defunct pid. This is the THIRD defect in that one call today: it returned False on PermissionError, reading a live process owned by another user as dead, and now True on a zombie, reading a corpse as alive. Both were confident wrong verdicts from a single syscall that cannot distinguish the cases. The remedy is to consult the process state and treat Z as dead. (2) A RESUME LEAVES ITS CHILD UNREAPED. The zombie above existed because a resume-ready sweep launched a child that finished and was never waited on. Better than tolerating zombies: the resume should waitpid its child or spawn it detached, so no corpse exists for any liveness check to trip over. (3) wait_terminal SPLITS A STRING ON COMMAS WITHOUT JSON-DECODING while its neighbour wait_probe does decode, so a manifest written with the natural JSON list produces terminal values that can never match and the run parks forever. Cost a fleet a failed wake; the comma form is the only one that works today and is confirmed by a sweep-driven wake on another lane. (4) wait_started_at IS OPTIONAL WHEN ABSENT — mtime is the fallback — BUT REJECTED WHEN PRESENT AND UNPARSEABLE, so omitting the field is safe and writing it quoted is fatal, and a YAML-minded worker writes it quoted. The parser should strip surrounding quotes. (5) THE condition-overdue LABEL FIRES ON AGE ALONE even when the probe matched nothing, so it reads as 'the condition has been met for too long' and hides the real cause. (6) A PARKED RUN RENDERS AS STALLED with 'stream quiet for 901s' while its manifest says waiting — the waiting arm must outrank stream-quiet, because a declared wait is precisely a stream that is quiet on purpose. ALSO QUEUED, on the display rather than the classifier: a transition row renders the role as '?' because the event's role field arrives empty from the snapshot while a dispatch row carries it; roles are abbreviated to four characters where the pane has room for the word; and the agent label is built from different fields on the two paths, so one row reads an alias with one separator and the next reads a raw model with another.

The checkpoint repair landed, was reviewed at 96/100, and did not change the behaviour it was written to change. Measured on the first worker dispatched after it reached HEAD, about twenty minutes later.

the-checkpoint-slot-is-offered-to-every-worker widened the manifest guidance so the checkpoint key is offered to any worker recording progress, not only to a resumed one whose wait is met. Its gate asserted the introduced literal appears in both the manifest checkpoint line and the time fence, which it does, and its review found nothing.

The next node dispatched after the merge, dispatch-does-not-recompose-the-project-per-claim on the codex lane, received a prompt carrying that literal twice — confirmed by grep against the run's own prompt.txt — and wrote this:

status: waiting
wait_condition: Initial profiling and implementation are in progress; this
  checkpoint ends when the base cost is reproduced and the scoped repair is
  committed.

It is holding no wait. It is describing where it stands, which is the checkpoint case exactly, and it used the word checkpoint inside the wait_condition while setting status: waiting. The guidance was present, correct, and read; the behaviour is unchanged.

What this says about the measure rather than the worker. The node's falsifier asserted the presence of a literal in composed prompt text. That is a true statement about the prompt and it is not the outcome the section wanted, which is that a worker recording progress leaves a checkpoint rather than a false wait. A measure that reads the instrument instead of the effect passes whether or not the effect occurs — the same shape as a gate the judged worker writes. The repair is not wrong and should stay; what it lacks is any consequence for declaring a wait that is not one.

This converges with the imas-ambix report (see work does not fall to the coordinator): their #8 is that a declared wait carries a success terminal and no failure terminal, and their #11 is a run sitting invalid-waiting for four hours. Three instances now, two fleets, one cause — nothing refuses a wait block that declares no wait. Offering a better alternative does not displace it, because the wait block costs the worker nothing and is never rejected. The next repair in this family is a refusal, not more guidance.

One off-vocabulary status word strands a run forever, and the cost is paid by someone else, days later. Measured on a live instance today, and it independently confirms a report from the imas-ambix fleet.

Run r-20260915T061057147322-a-send-to-a-worker-is-refused, session rca-worker-channel-20260915, has been classified unreadable since 2026-09-15. Its manifest is well-formed and complete. Its only defect is one word:

status: done

cannot read manifest: status 'done' is not a recognised manifest status
(recognised: blocked, complete, derived, failed, in-progress, in_progress,
 pending, running, …)

The reader refuses the whole manifest, so manifest_reported_status is None and the run classifies as unreadable — indistinguishable from a corrupt manifest, which is exactly the report imas-ambix filed from their own fleet. unreadable is not a terminal state the lifecycle can drain, so the run cannot leave it and its live pointer never clears.

What that pointer then does to everyone else. Its declared write scope names reckon/cli.py. Two days later that stale claim refused an unrelated promotion in a different session, on a node whose own work was already merged. The chain is worth stating whole, because no link in it is obviously a defect: a worker writes a plausible synonym, the reader refuses the manifest, the classifier reports a corruption that did not happen, the state is undrainable, the pointer survives, and the claim blocks a stranger.

Verified before reconciling it, since a claim is owned by its worktree: clean tree, zero commits beyond base, HEAD an ancestor of main, no ledger row, and its declared deliverable already present in main by another route. Nothing was discarded.

Two repairs, and they are independent. The vocabulary should either admit done or the refusal should name the run as mis-stated rather than unreadable, so a reader can tell a synonym from a corruption. And unreadable needs a drain path regardless, because a state with no exit accumulates claims by construction — this one held for two days without anyone noticing, and only a refusal made it visible.

A fourth instance of the wait block that declares no wait, on a different harness from the other three. That is the fact that settles the repair, and it came from the imas-ambix fleet rather than from here.

The three measured in this session were codex-dialect workers. Theirs is run r-20260917T100806889701-deploy-recipe-record on the local clive backend — a different model family, client, dialect and prompt composition path — and it produced the identical shape at minute one of a forty-five minute node, while working normally with a stream age of zero:

status: waiting
wait_condition: initial recon in progress

Four workers, three harnesses, one behaviour arrived at independently. Guidance cannot reach that. This session already measured the guidance half directly: the widened checkpoint wording reached the next worker's prompt twice and changed nothing. The wait block is simply the cheapest way for any worker to say “in progress”, and nothing refuses it — so the repair is a predicate that makes a declared wait falsifiable, not more instruction.

The operator-facing cost, which is the half that had not been named. Their instance escalated to wait-aged, rendered as an alarm with a bang: ! investigate: waiting 901s on initial recon in progress. The follower was correctly flagging a stall that did not exist. Their words: they nearly went and investigated, and the only reason they did not is that they already knew the defect. An alarm that fires on healthy nodes makes a real stall unreadable, which is this plan's own subject — a display that cannot lie — reached from the wait block rather than from liveness.

What is not yet established, and why. A population figure for how many declared waits are false. An attempt here to classify 389 distinct run-and-condition pairs from the watch logs by keyword was abandoned as unsound: most conditions name a subject and describe progress, so the classifier bucketed 373 of 389 as “names an external subject” on matches like suite and test, which measures the regex rather than the fleet. The honest position is four instances by direct observation and no rate.

The instrument for the rate did not exist until today. the-wait-facts-reach-the-event landed this morning and carries wait_probe and wait_terminal into the transition event, so a wait that names no runnable probe becomes distinguishable from one that does — prospectively, on events written from now on. That is worth knowing before anyone tries to derive the rate from the existing 395 wait transitions, none of which carry those fields.

A resumed run's pointer carries no trace of the attempt that failed, so the artifact a diagnostician most needs is the one the recovery destroys. That sentence is the imas-efit coordinator's, and it is the generalisation of three separate wrong diagnoses made across three sessions today — including two of this session's own.

The occasion: a worker refused client-side at admission with "Prompt is too long", terminal_reason: blocking_limit, 300 ms, every token counter zero and model: <synthetic>. Its prompt.txt is 10,291 bytes against a 480,000-token window, so the node contract was never the large term. This session diagnosed it confidently as a resumed long-lived transcript invisible to the context-fit estimator — the cause found here earlier in the day — and was wrong on every supporting fact.

Read asActually
argv carries --resume <id>, so the dispatch resumed a sessionthe pointer is attempt: 2, attempt_kind: "resume", started 10:46:50Z against the run's created_at of 10:39:10Z — the --resume is the recovery, and attempt 1's own system/init shows that session being created by the attempt that died
session_reuse: True confirms a session was reusedmeasured across every live pointer on this workstation: true on 51 of 51, and 45 of those carry no session_id at all — it reports that reuse is enabled, never that it occurred
the estimate was too lowthe estimate does not order the failures: the refused run computed 63,980 while two runs at 203,687 and 147,955 work normally on the same lane and model

session_reuse is a constant and should be treated as a defect in its own right. A field true in every case discriminates nothing, which is precisely why this plan retired the gate measure that sat at 1.00 on every lane. It is worse than a missing field, because a reader reaches for it and it answers.

What is established about the failure itself is narrow and stays that way: a brand-new session, a 10,291-byte prompt, a computed estimate of 63,980 against a 480,000 window, refused client-side in 300 ms. No root cause. The earlier resumed-transcript finding stands on its own evidence and has simply lost the cross-project confirmation that was making it feel solid.

The rule, which belongs in the classifier repair rather than in a note. Recovery and diagnosis are in direct tension and nothing in the surface says so: a coordinator following the documented remedy — resume rather than redispatch — overwrites the record of what it is remedying. So a run refused at admission needs a classification of its own carrying those four marks, and the failing attempt's record must survive the resume that follows it. At present the only durable evidence is three lines of stream.jsonl and a stderr.log the pointer no longer describes.

The generic abandoned bucket has a measured cross-fleet cost besides. Reading that death as a known local-lane defect, the reporting coordinator told a third fleet they had a second instance of a serve reliability problem; that fleet's own discriminator scores zero on both of its terms, so it was never that shape, and the report was retracted. A specific classification would have stopped it.

Three checks fired on correct content today, and the cheapest of them was the most costly, because it taught its reader to ignore it.

CheckFired onCorrect?
md-bold, in doccheck.pya literal asterisk pair inside <pre> and <code>no — 19 of 19 occurrences in this plan are legitimate: 12 in <pre> sample data and 7 in <code> spans, one of them Python dictionary unpacking
fully-specified, the node contractthe client's no-model placeholder token, which is a word in angle bracketsno — a literal value a backend emits, read as an unfilled placeholder
single-goal, the node contracta goal joining clauses with and or thenyes for this session's goal, which genuinely carried two deliverables; arguably not for the peer's, which was one deliverable in two ordered steps

The shape is one thing: a check that cannot distinguish a literal from its own syntax. The plan it fires on hardest is the one whose subject is manifest formatting, so the document most likely to quote a spelling is the document most likely to be told the spelling is a defect.

The compounding cost is the finding, not the false positive. This session deferred that audit failure four separate times across the day — each time correctly, each time reasoning that the errors were pre-existing and not introduced by the edit in hand — and by the fourth had stopped reading the category at all. It came within one step of hand-editing correct content to satisfy it. That is the same failure as a waiver required on every dispatch and a field that answers true in every case: a signal that fires regardless stops carrying information, and the bill is paid by the next real one.

Repair dispatched for the first only (an-audit-error-a-reader-cannot-clear), and deliberately not for the other two. single-goal earned its keep on a goal that really did carry two deliverables, and the placeholder check correctly caught a genuine unfilled placeholder in a done-when earlier the same day. Routing around those cost one rewording each; weakening them would cost more than that. The discriminator between the two groups is whether a correct document can ever satisfy the check — not how often it fires.

The first measurement of the false wait with a denominator, by direct observation. One wave, five workers on the local lane, every node's displayed state compared against the mtime and record count of its own stream at the same instant.

All five actively working. Both alarms false; zero true positives in the wave. Their declared conditions were review complete and orientation only — neither is a wait, both are descriptions of being partway through the worker's own job, and the follower correctly aged each into ! investigate: waiting 90x s fifteen minutes later.

The rate is stated with its sample size and should not be carried further than that. Two of five workers, one wave, one lane, one session. That is not a claim that two in five declared waits are false: n=5 is precisely the size that reads convincingly and is overturned by the next sample, which a peer coordinator demonstrated against themselves the same day with bins at n=2, 11 and 24 reading 1.40x, 1.16x and 1.35x. What this measurement is: the first with a denominator at all, by observation rather than by the keyword classifier attempted earlier here, which bucketed 373 of 389 on matches like suite and test and was measuring itself.

Two facts that do not depend on the sample size. Both workers that declared a wait were holding nothing. And both alarms rendered in alarm colour with a bang while the three rows doing nothing unusual rendered plainly — so a real stall in this wave would have been the least conspicuous row on the display. That is this plan's thesis reached from the wait block rather than from liveness, and now measured rather than argued.

Worth recording that the coordinator was prompted to investigate both and did not, for the same reason the peer fleet did not: prior knowledge of the defect. Neither of us was protected by the surface, and prior knowledge does not survive a context boundary.

Correction and refinement to the comment above, from the imas-codex fleet, and it breaks the repair this session had committed to. The finding recorded in c-the-wait-alarm-scored-zero-in-a-measured-wave is sound — both alarms were false and both workers were mid-turn — but the diagnosis attached to it was only half the mechanism, and the half that was missing is the one that invalidates the proposed fix.

The display ages the wrong artifact. wait-aged ages the manifest field, and the manifest is precisely the artifact a busy worker stops touching. Measured here on the same two runs, at one instant:

review-of-a-nested-key-stays-nested    manifest age 1838s   stream age 0s
an-audit-error-a-reader-cannot-clear   manifest age 1672s   stream age 3s

So the signal is structurally anti-correlated with what it detects: the longer a worker genuinely works, the more certainly it is reported stalled. The evidence that settles it is already in the run directory and is not consulted. The remedy is cheap — age on the stream's freshness, or render both so a reader sees 1838s since manifest, 0s since output and the contradiction resolves itself. This holds even for an entirely honest wait, which is what makes it the more general of the two defects.

And the probe is a constant, which is the layer that breaks the planned repair. The brief prepared here required a declared wait to carry a probe that runs and a terminal that can be reached. Reading the two blocks:

wait_probe: ["echo","pending"]          wait_terminal: exit:0
wait_probe: ["git","rev-parse","HEAD"]  wait_terminal: exit:0

Both probes run. Both terminals are reachable. Both are satisfied unconditionally. That falsifier would have passed them clean, and the repair would have shipped as a fix that fixes nothing. The discriminator is not well-formedness but whether the probe's result can ever differ: a wait must reference something outside the worker — a job id, a pid, a port, a path — never a command that cannot fail.

One of the two workers wrote wait_condition: orientation only — superseded by later writes in this turn. It knew its record was stale and had no way to say so except by rewriting a manifest it was too busy to rewrite. That sentence is the whole defect in the worker's own words.

Adjacent, from the same reporter and worth carrying wherever these states are documented: on that backend an orientation-time wait row is weak evidence in either direction, and three transient unreadable samples there self-corrected within about 40 seconds. Those were genuine mid-write reads, not defects — the difference between an instrument being wrong and an instrument catching a file mid-write, where only the first is a fault.

A run reported ! decide: the manifest reports a blocker over a manifest that says blockers: none. Measured here on a-run-record-says-whether-it-resumed, and it completes a family this plan has now seen three times in one day.

status: blocked
blockers: none
checkpoint: orientation done; defect confirmed by measurement …
            Next: rename the record's capability field and add the
            launch-carried observation, plus the focused test file.

The worker was not blocked. It ran out of turn mid-task, wrote an accurate checkpoint naming exactly what remained, and set a status word that did not match the blocker field beside it. The display took the word. The coordinator was then asked to decide something with no subject, and the cost was a read of the manifest to discover there was nothing to decide. Resume with advice returned it to working in forty seconds, with its session and every turn intact.

The family, stated once. Three distinct states today were inferred from a field that is not the authority for them:

StateRead fromThe authority it should read
wait-agedthe manifest's agethe stream's freshness — the manifest is what a busy worker stops touching
session_reusethe backend's capability flagwhether this run's own launch carried a prior session
blockedthe status wordwhether a blocker is recorded

Each is confident, wrong, and indistinguishable from correct at the point of reading — which is this plan's subject exactly. The blocked case is the cheapest to repair and the easiest to state as a rule: a status of blocked with no blocker recorded is a checkpoint, not a decision, and should classify as one rather than raising a demand.

Worth noting where the worker was right, since a day of defect-recording distorts the picture: it re-measured the population rather than trusting the brief's figure, and improved it — 51 of 51 pointers carrying session_reuse true with 47 holding no session id against the brief's 45, and exactly one carrying a resume flag in argv. The brief told it to re-measure because the population moves; it did, and the single positive case it found is the right thing to build the falsifier around.

Five separate defects were found in the manifest reader in a single day, all silent, and three of them reach commits — the field a promotion verifies a run by. Listing them together because the fifth was found by reading a worker's own manifest, and at that point the count stops being a series of incidents.

DefectEffectFound by
a quoted scalar kept its quotesthe whole manifest refused over one field's formattinga plan section
an off-vocabulary status wordthe whole manifest refused; run permanently unreadable, its claim blocking a stranger's promotion two days latera stuck run
a nested key hoisted to top levela commits entry indented under another parent became the run's declared commitsa follow-on drain
a block scalar swallowed every later fieldin a wholly indented manifest, commits returned emptyan independent review
a list of mappings flattened to stringscommits parsed as ['sha: 7f7541d3…'] — the line, key included, which can never resolve as a git refreading a worker's manifest

This is not five incidents. It is one format loose enough that workers improvise inside it, and one bespoke reader that absorbs each improvisation differently. Every one of the five was written by a worker doing something reasonable: quoting a scalar the way YAML allows, choosing a plausible synonym for a status, indenting a block, using a mapping list to carry a commit with its subject. None was malformed by any standard except this reader's.

The cost is concentrated rather than spread. commits is reachable by three of the five, and a promotion records what the reader returns — so a manifest written in any of those three shapes yields a ledger row whose commit reference is wrong, empty, or unresolvable, reported as a successful landing. This project's ledger already carries 9 unresolvable or unmerged commit references out of 879, and three of those resolve in sibling repositories, which is a different defect again.

The decision this raises is whether the sixth repair should be a repair at all. Each of the five was individually small and each was correctly scoped, but the sequence is evidence about the format rather than about any one patch. Two honest directions: adopt a strict parse with an explicit schema and refuse anything outside it, which makes workers' improvisation loud instead of silently reinterpreted; or keep the permissive reader and give it a conformance suite over the shapes workers actually emit, harvested from the manifests already on disk. The first costs worker retries, the second costs a corpus nobody has built.

What should not happen is a sixth patch landed on the assumption that the fifth was the last one. Four of these were found in a single day by looking, and nobody has yet looked systematically.

A sixth defect in the manifest reader, found four hours after the fifth, and it corrupts commits again — the fourth of the six to do so. Recording it because it arrived after the decision above was written, which is itself the argument.

A worker committed three changes and wrote them into its manifest. The reader returned five entries:

["c754cfe2 feat(crew): narrow or release a live run's write claim through a checked module",
 "bd79bf77 test(crew): a claimed path is released through the module and still refuses a live worker",
 "09a668dd test(crew): cover partial release",
 "an undeclared path",
 "a no-op narrowing and the promotion control"]

The third commit's subject is “cover partial release, an undeclared path, a no-op narrowing and the promotion control”. The reader split it on its own commas. Two of the five entries are prose fragments that can never resolve as git refs, and had that run been promoted as written, its ledger row would have carried them. That is a plausible origin for at least some of the nine unresolvable or unmerged commit references already sitting in this project's ledger, alongside the three that resolve only in sibling repositories.

Why this forces the decision rather than adding to it. The five defects listed above were found by looking; this one was found by reading one worker's manifest in passing, hours later, with nobody searching. Six defects in one reader in one day, four of them reaching the single field a promotion verifies a run by, and the sixth discovered incidentally — that is not a backlog being worked down, it is a sampling rate. Nobody has yet read the corpus of manifests on disk, and the last two defects were found without trying.

The worker was told to work around it by writing bare forty-character shas with no subject on the line, and told explicitly that this is a reader defect rather than a correction of what it wrote. That workaround is a coordinator carrying a parser's gap in a dispatch brief, which is precisely what this plan's sibling exists to stop.

The six defects above are one defect. Measured directly rather than inferred: parse_manifest returns a string for every value, and list-valued fields are recovered afterwards by splitting that string on commas.

WrittenReturned
flat scalar'value' — correct
inline JSON object'{"a": 1, "b": 2}' — a string, never parsed
nested mapping'' — silently empty
list of mappings'a: 1, a: 2' — flattened, keys retained
list of scalars'one, two' — comma-joined

And the recovery, which is where the damage lands:

commits:
  - abc1234 fix(x): a change, with a comma
  - def5678 fix(y): plain

-> ['abc1234 fix(x): a change', 'with a comma', 'def5678 fix(y): plain']

So every one of the six is the same mechanism seen from a different field. A nested key leaks because there is no nesting. A block scalar swallows later fields because there is no block. A mapping list keeps its keys because there are no mappings. A commit subject splits because a list is a comma-joined string. A quoted scalar kept its quotes because the value was never a value. The reader is a flat string map wearing a structured interface, and every consumer reconstructs the structure it needs by string surgery, independently and differently.

Two consequences worth stating separately. First, the damage concentrates on commits because that field is list-valued, contains free text, and is the one a promotion verifies a run by — so it is simultaneously the most likely to contain a comma and the most costly to get wrong. Second, a nested mapping returning the empty string is worse than any of the others, because it is the only one that loses evidence silently and completely: a manifest carrying a full, well-formed tests block reads as having run no tests at all. That was measured on a node whose real result was 8 failed 310 passed at base against 8 failed 320 passed at head, zero added, and whose gate evidence the reader reported as absent.

This settles the decision recorded above. A seventh patch would repair one more field's string surgery. The choice is between giving the reader real structure — parse the document once, return mappings and lists as mappings and lists — or declaring the format flat and forbidding structured fields outright, so workers stop writing what cannot be read. Both are defensible; continuing to patch consumers is not, because the consumers are not where the defect is.

A resume that dies before writing anything leaves a zero-byte log, and the session resolver then reports the run unresumable while two surviving logs carry the answer. Measured today on a run with green tests in its worktree.

The sequence: a node was resumed moments before the local lane was taken down for a restart. The launch reached a dead backend, wrote nothing, and left resume-2.jsonl at 0 bytes. A later resume was refused:

no session id in any authority: no session id in the live pointer, the run's own
stream or the promoted ledger row; all three were consulted, so this is an
absence rather than a reading nobody has taken yet

Both older logs carried it:

stream.jsonl     4,197,269 bytes   session_id=476ca4aa-3108-418a-9008-5fd2205c43e6
resume-1.jsonl     279,574 bytes   session_id=476ca4aa-3108-418a-9008-5fd2205c43e6
resume-2.jsonl           0 bytes   (no parsable record)

The refusal is worse than a silent absence because it asserts its own thoroughness. That final clause exists to tell a reader not to go looking — it explicitly distinguishes a real absence from an unmade reading, and it is wrong in exactly the way it promises not to be. A coordinator who trusts it discards a recoverable run; this one only looked because the sentence was too confident to be worth believing after a day of instruments that answer adjacent questions.

The remedy is narrow. The resolver consults the newest log per source; it should consult the newest log that parses, or fall back through the run's logs in order. A failed launch is exactly when a resume is most needed and exactly when the newest artifact is emptiest, so the two conditions coincide by construction rather than by coincidence.

Recovered by restoring session_id to the pointer from the value both surviving logs carry, which is what the resolver would have done had it looked past the empty file. Verified first that an earlier hand edit to that pointer had not caused it: diffed against the backup taken before that edit, and session_id was already null there, with node the only field changed. Given the coordinator had edited shared state minutes earlier to work around a missing command, ruling that out was the first obligation rather than the last.

Related to the followup above on the pointer's session field lagging its authority: that one is a field trailing a resolver that works, this one is the resolver itself aiming at the wrong artifact. Same family, opposite halves.

Held unmerged: the arrival extraction drops the row it was built to catch. Run r-20260917T165959552736 delivered three commits and an independent review scored it 72/100 — goal_fidelity 12, durability 12, evidence 13 — with seven findings. It is not merged and not promoted, and its live pointer is deliberately preserved so the session can be resumed rather than redispatched.

The headline finding, verified here rather than taken from the review. In _rendered_row the transition branch reads if len(left) < 2 or len(tokens) < 2: return None. A rendered transition row whose only token left of the arrow is the node name — which is precisely what a node's first appearance looks like, having no source state — gives tokens of length one and returns None. The arrival is dropped. The reviewer's sample is direct and reproducible: in the imas-ambix stream, line 1 reads 16:29:36 clive-reuse-map -> dispatched and is discarded, and extract_watch_arrivals returns line 4 instead. The function's own docstring promises the rendered form is marked as carrying no establishable stamp rather than dropped silently, so the code contradicts its own stated contract.

Two further findings that would have survived a merge indefinitely. The only rendered baseline fixture in the seven new tests is a bullet line, a shape that occurs zero times across all six live streams — so the suite ranges over a form the corpus never produces and could not have caught the defect above. And the evidence record states that 2,317 of 2,931 nodes present no transition row, where the corpus gives 14 of 2,951: a figure wrong by two orders of magnitude, published as a landed measurement.

The remaining findings concern a population count that tallies nodes carrying a JSON row rather than the nodes the function sees, and a burst test whose failure direction is itself a dropped arrival.

Disposition. Scope discipline was clean at 20/20 and the worktree is clean, so nothing here is a boundary problem — the node did what it was asked, and what it was asked does not hold. Resume with advice naming the one-token case as the falsifier, rather than redispatching, because the session holds the context and a redispatch discards it.

The resume advice, recorded here so it is not reconstructed from memory: a rendered transition row whose only token left of the arrow is the node name is an arrival and must be returned, not dropped. Write that falsifier first and confirm it fails at the current head. Then replace the bullet-line fixture with shapes drawn from the live streams, naming the source stream for each. Then re-derive the 2,317-of-2,931 figure and state the derivation beside it. Do not promote until the one-token case passes; promotion deletes the pointer and takes the resumable session with it.

A regression this plan's own work introduced, and the node repairing it. The declared-schema reader that landed on 2026-09-17 fixed four measured defects and broke twenty-seven manifests the line-scanning reader had accepted. The repair node is the-declared-schema-reads-what-the-old-reader-read, and this comment exists so that node's reason survives the session that cut it.

How it was measured. The previous reader was extracted from eb74752d~1 and both readers were run over every manifest under the crew configuration home — 3,694 files. Two thousand eight hundred and ninety-five parse under both. Thirty that the old reader refused now parse, which is the change doing its job. Twenty-seven that the old reader accepted are now refused, in four shapes: thirteen on test_logs, eleven on artifacts, one on follow_ons and one on commits, each reporting that the field carries a mapping where the schema expects a list. A twenty-eighth is not a refusal but a crash, TypeError: cannot use 'dict' as a dict key.

The corpus is durable rather than in a session scratchpad. The twenty-seven paths, one per line, are at ~/.config/reckon/crew/reports/reader-regression-corpus.txt. Any later reader can re-derive the comparison from it without rebuilding the extraction.

Blast radius, and why this was not urgent. Zero live runs were affected when measured: all twenty-seven are historical manifests, so no follower was misreading a working fleet because of it. That is the reason the repair was queued behind other work rather than jumped ahead of it, and it is worth recording because the same defect with live runs in the set would have been a different decision.

The negative half the repair must keep. The four defects the schema was introduced to fix stay fixed: a commits list whose third subject contains a comma reads as three entries and not four, and a tests field written as a nested mapping reads as that mapping rather than the empty string. A repair that restores the twenty-seven by returning the reader to line scanning is a failure, not a pass.

Four defects recorded on this plan today are one defect: a state word that was true when written, read as though it were true now, with nothing on the row carrying the age of the claim. The framing is the peer coordinator's; the instances were found separately, by two sessions, on four surfaces, none of us looking for a pattern.

Every one is individually repairable and repairing them individually is the wrong response. Each has an obvious local fix — resolve promoted against a ledger row, read compaction before declaring abandonment, key liveness on stream freshness, reconcile phase with the manifest. Four fixes, four tests, and the fifth instance arrives next week in a state word nobody has enumerated, because the thing they share is not in any of them.

What they share is that a state is stored without its observation time. A classification is a measurement, and a measurement without a stamp cannot be compared against anything — not against the process table, not against the stream, not against a later reading of itself. The surface then has no way to express this was true at 16:31 and I have not looked since, so every reader supplies now for free, and is wrong in proportion to how long the row has sat.

The evidence that it is one defect rather than four is how they were found. No instance was found by looking for it. The discard came from a peer checking a counter it did not trust; the compaction from a diagnosis of mine that the run refuted by resuming; the dead-but-running from running the peer's own synthesis as a query against a live fleet; the blocked-but-writing from a glance at an unrelated field. Four surfaces, two coordinators, one afternoon, no search. A defect that dense is a property of the model rather than a run of bad luck in four modules.

The falsifier for treating it as one, and it is cheap. Give a stored state its observation time and require every reader to compare that stamp against the fact it is about — the process table, the newest stream mtime, the ledger. If the four instances then reduce to one rule and no new local checks are needed, the unification is real. If any of them still needs its own repair afterwards, it was four defects and this comment is wrong.

It is recorded here rather than under any of the four sections because it belongs to none of them, and because a plan titled for a display that cannot lie should carry the reason its display keeps lying in one place.

THE SECTION'S SECOND MEASURE DEMONSTRATED ITSELF WITHIN MINUTES OF BEING WRITTEN, AND THE HOLD TURNS OUT TO BE SELF-SUSTAINING. Measured 2026-09-03 at 10:15:58Z. A dispatch to the claude lane was refused budget-hold at utilisation 100.0 percent, type spend-limit, threshold exhausted, source live-run. Three readings from the same payload settle it. The evidence is stamped observed_at 10:02:01Z while the pre-flight ran at 10:15:58Z, so the refusal rests on a reading fourteen minutes old. resets_at is None, so §6's unparsed-reset gap means the fence cannot even compute whether the stated reset has passed. And a live one-token probe at the same moment returned is_error false with a served answer. The record said exhausted, the endpoint said yes, and the record won — which is the measure a-stale-hold-is-re-derived-not-obeyed, failing in exactly the shape it was written to forbid. THE DEADLOCK IS THE REAL FINDING. The budget record is written only by served runs, source live-run in every reading here. A hold blocks every run. So once a hold is recorded, nothing in the ordinary path can ever clear it: the only writer is the thing the hold forbids. It is the same bootstrap shape the concurrency plan already recorded for the watcher guard — watch exits on an empty project while dispatch refuses without a watcher, so the first dispatch of a wave can never have one — and it means this section's third measure, a probe writes back, is not a convenience for saving a duplicate probe. It is the deadlock's only exit, and should be stated that way. A second defect surfaced beside it: there is no waiver for a budget hold. Every other fence in this surface carries a recorded escape — allow-unreconciled-runs, no-watch, waive-suite-delta, waive-boundary-refusal, allow-execution-mismatch — and §6 of clive-local-workers records what happens when a guard has none, where a wrong verdict blocked six runs across three sessions for an hour because it could not be overridden. A stale hold with no override is that incident's shape a second time. The wave was routed to the local lane, which is unmetered and therefore never subject to this, and the two monitor nodes are running there.

Stopped 30 seconds in and reshaped: its design made the written record primary with a probe as a staleness escape hatch, and the lead inverted it — availability is polled, the record is history. Landing the weaker architecture would have been harder to undo than restating it

THE RECORD IS HISTORY, THE POLL IS THE AUTHORITY — the lead's inversion, and it supersedes the ladder's ordering above. This section had the written record as primary with a probe as a staleness escape hatch. That is backwards, and today settles it: the record was wrong in BOTH directions on the same afternoon. The claude lane read HELD at 100 per cent on evidence stamped 10:02:01Z while the pre-flight ran at 13:21:40Z, three hours nineteen minutes stale, and a live probe at that moment was served — seventeen consecutive dispatches were routed away from a working lane. Its sibling read CLEAR on evidence from 06:23:58Z, seven hours older still, and happened to be right. Something that can be wrong in either direction is not an authority; it is a log, and which reading blocks a wave was luck. SO THE DESIGN INVERTS. A wave's availability is decided by polling the lane. The record keeps what was observed, when, and by which rung — for calibration, for reporting, and for the four axes — and gates nothing. A reading may be reused within a wave under a declared short cache life measured in seconds or a minute, and such a reuse is a CACHE carrying its age rather than a verdict; nothing hours old ever decides anything. THE COST IS MEASURED AND IT SETS THE GRANULARITY, which is the one place this needs care. Two probes today cost 0.37658 and 0.37927 US dollars each. That is not the two-token prompt; it is 28,922 to 40,470 tokens of cache creation, because a probe pays to build the prompt cache from cold. So a probe is cheap per WAVE and not cheap per NODE: at roughly a hundred nodes in a day, per-node polling is about thirty-eight dollars while per-wave polling is cents. Poll when a wave opens, cache for the wave, and let a node inherit the wave's reading. Two cheaper rungs remain available and both are already noted here: auth_check, declared for none of the nine backends, answers reachability at zero tokens and never quota; and the rate-limit telemetry every served request already returns updates the log for free, which is what makes the log good history and still not an authority.

Ceded to a prior claim on the same two files. The read-side half - age out a known exhaustion judgement carrying no reset time - is handed to the peer's node so one node carries both halves. The dispatch defect is mine and it is a real hole: the scope validator enforces exclusivity per project on a resource that is per repository, so two runs declaring different projects can fence the same repository file and the check passes.

A hold parses the reset time its refusal names and expires on it; a hold naming none ages out of a declared bound into headroom unknown; an absent reset is recorded as absent; and utilisation is read from the window rather than the account overage figure.

No caller was wired: the seat stamp names the install rather than the code, measured by the stamp holding still across eighty-five commits to the package including that module, so the false docstring is corrected and the limitation is asserted instead of a dead call site being added.

ROOT CAUSE OF GONE WITHOUT A COMPLETE MANIFEST, AND THE CLASSIFIER IS NOT AT FAULT. The lead reported the note recurring on a peer project's pane at 17:23:35 and 17:25:11, on two sonnet runs. Both were measured directly from their run directories and the verdict is accurate in both: the process is gone and there is no complete manifest. The defect is one layer upstream and there are TWO MECHANISMS behind the one message. MECHANISM ONE, THE HARNESS BACKGROUND-WAIT CEILING. The run n-drain-closes-the-detachment-tail wrote to its stderr: 'Background tasks still running after 600s; terminating. Set CLAUDE_CODE_PRINT_BG_WAIT_CEILING_MS=0 to wait indefinitely.' Its final result record is a clean turn - subtype success, is_error false, stop_reason end_turn, three turns - carrying the text 'Continuing to wait for pool-level (compose/review) activity from the drain.' So the worker finished a turn while waiting on background work it had started, and the harness killed the process group at a six hundred second ceiling before any later turn could write a manifest. Three runs across two days carry that stderr line. MECHANISM TWO, AND IT NEEDS NO CEILING AT ALL. The run n-callers-pass-the-run-to-the-detachment has an EMPTY stderr, fifty-six turns, and a final result reading 'Waiting for the background full tests/standard_names suite run to complete before finalizing the manifest.' Nothing terminated it. It ended its turn intending to be given another one, and a print-mode invocation has no next turn - the process exits when the model emits end_turn. The worker's plan required a future that its own process lifetime does not contain. THE COMMON CAUSE, which is the sentence worth keeping: A WORKER TREATS A BACKGROUND TASK AS SOMETHING IT CAN WAIT ACROSS, WHILE ITS PROCESS LIFETIME ENDS WITH ITS TURN. The ceiling is only the variant where the wait lasts long enough to be killed; the end-of-turn exit is the same error arriving sooner and more quietly. AND THE EVIDENCE IS ON DISK AND NOTHING READS IT, which is the seventh instance in this repository of a record carrying the fact while its consumer discards it. A grep for the ceiling string, and for the environment key it names, across the whole of reckon returns nothing. The stderr file sits beside the stream in every run directory and the final result record carries the worker's own account of what it was waiting for; the classifier reads neither, so it reports the true but useless fact that a manifest is absent instead of the actionable one that the run was waiting on background work and can be resumed. TWO FIXES, DISPATCHED SEPARATELY BECAUSE THEY ARE DETECTION AND PREVENTION. Detection, on the recovery classifier: a run whose stderr names the background-wait ceiling, or whose final result text says it was waiting on background work, classifies as its own outcome naming the wait, and the disposition tells a coordinator to resume rather than redispatch - because in both measured cases the session is intact and a resume would have collected the manifest. Prevention, on the dispatch contract: the contract must state that a worker's process ends with its turn, so a manifest is written BEFORE any background wait begins and updated after, never deferred until the wait completes. That rule already exists in this workstation's guidance for multi-hour campaigns, as launch then harvest with incremental checkpoints, and the two measured runs show it is needed at the scale of a single test suite rather than only a slurm job.

A run whose stderr names the background-wait ceiling, or whose terminal text says it was waiting on background work before finalizing, classifies as blocked rather than abandoned, so the recorded remedy is resume; a run gone with neither signal keeps its classification, asserted as a negative.

The contract states the process ends with the turn and keys the manifest instruction to starting a background wait rather than to finishing work, naming resume as the recovery so a run out of turns leaves a record of what it was waiting for.

A SEAT RECORD OUTLIVES ITS PROCESS, FOUND IN A WORKTREE AUDIT RATHER THAN IN A RUN. Closing this session's worktrees turned up nineteen trees, thirteen clean and reachable and removed, five holding uncommitted duplicates of work already landed, and ONE holding a capability that reached no commit and exists nowhere in the tree. Its symbol is absent from every file in reckon and tests, so it was never merged and would have been lost with the worktree. THE CAPABILITY IS A LIVENESS FIX AND IT BELONGS TO THIS SECTION'S FAMILY. The liveness check reads a seat record, confirms the pid against the process table and the recorded start time, and returns the verdict - and when the answer is dead it leaves the record in place. The unlanded work erases that record at the moment the process is confirmed gone, writing the same payload an explicit unwatch writes, lock-free and best-effort so that a concurrent arming or teardown is never clobbered. Its own docstring states the reason better than a summary would: the record is what a reader trusts between arming and process death, and left alone it keeps answering a question the process table has already contradicted. THAT SENTENCE IS THE DAY'S RECURRING DEFECT IN ITS SHORTEST FORM, and it now has four measured faces on this workstation. A budget hold observed at 09:22 with no reset time held a lane for five hours and forty-nine minutes while the account served. Four of eight live pointers were held by processes that no longer existed, stranding thirteen write paths for over twenty-one hours. A watch seat's version stamp names the install rather than the code, so it cannot detect the staleness it exists to detect. And a seat record answers liveness questions after its process is gone. In every case a stored answer outlived the thing it described, and in every case the correction is the same: a record that cannot be re-derived must carry when it was written, and a consumer must consult that before treating it as current. WORTH RECORDING SEPARATELY THAT THE FINDING CAME FROM HOUSEKEEPING. It checked resumption.py and found the auto-resume sweep at line 735 acts ONLY on runs classified blocked. It kept refusals blocked so the sweep still reaches them, and used paused only where something actually enacts the wake. The naive taxonomy would have made recovery worse while looking calmer, which is the failure mode this whole plan is about. The evidence guard is inverted: a manifest declaring complete with no evidence at all is accepted, while one with partial evidence is refused. promotion.py:504:

if (status != "complete"
    or not manifest.get("changed_paths")     # returns when changed_paths is EMPTY
    or _prose_changed_paths_name_no_paths(manifest)
    or manifest.get("commits")):
    return
raise CrewError(...)

The refusal fires only when changed_paths is non-empty and commits is missing. So a worker that names its changed paths but forgets the commit is caught, and a worker that names nothing — no commits, no paths, no tests — falls through the second clause and promotes. The guard's own message explains the reasoning that defeats it: "promotion cannot verify changed repository paths without the commit that contains them." With no declared paths there is nothing it thinks it needs to verify, and it stops asking.

Live reproduction in this session. a-finished-run-is-not-stalled-retry delivered a manifest reading status: complete with commits:, changed_paths: and tests: all empty, while its worktree holds two commits and 156 insertions across three files, tree clean. Real work, no declared evidence, and the guard has no objection.

This is the same shape as the gate that records passed beside a log that failed, one level up: the check is aimed at a malformed record rather than at an absent one, and absence is the cheaper failure for a worker to produce. The condition to add is that a complete status asserts some evidence — the refusal belongs on the empty case first and the mismatched case second.

Queued: reckon/crew/promotion.py carries five live claims across three sessions.

The positive-marker repair admits only the green-base case, which is the case that needed it least. Reported by a peer session that tested the landed fix against a held run and found it still refuses.

promotion.py:462 reads if recorded != 0 and re.search(r"\bcommand not found\b", log_text). A terminal EXIT=0 capture suppresses the substring scan; any other exit leaves the scan governing. But this repository's own discipline is judge by delta against a stated base, never by absolute green — and a delta-judged pass on a red base carries a non-zero exit by construction. The peer's run is a merged-head verification: 20 failures at the merge first parent, the identical 20 ids at the merged head, zero added and zero fixed by set difference, 505 passing before and 518 after. Pytest exits 1 regardless. So the scan fires, and a log that merely quotes the diagnostic in assertion output refuses a correct pass.

Their framing is the finding: the scan is answering "did this pass" while claiming to answer "did this run".

Their proposed predicate — trust a terminal EXIT capture of any value — readmits the case the guard exists for. A harness shaped cmd > log 2>&1; echo EXIT=$? emits EXIT=127 when the command is not found, so a log proving the command never ran does carry a terminal capture. The original incident survives only because the contradiction check catches a worker asserting exit status 0; one that honestly records 127 and still asserts --gate passed walks through — and by the argument above, a passing gate with a non-zero exit is legitimate here, so nothing downstream finds 127 suspicious. Treating agreement with --gate-exit-status as the positive evidence has the same hole, since 127 agreeing with 127 is agreement.

The predicate that covers both: trust a recorded EXIT as evidence the command ran, except 126 and 127 — the two POSIX codes asserting the shell could not execute it. Those are the only exits that answer the question the check asks. Everything else means it ran, and the verdict belongs to --gate and the delta arithmetic. This drops the substring scan entirely rather than making it conditional.

A quoted diagnostic no longer refuses a log that shows the command ran, and the capture status is read by value.
