This session's own follower died at 16:04Z and the incident belongs here, because the follower is the signal this section is about. Cause, established by falsifier rather than by inspection: a commit added budget.coordinator_reserve_pct to the defaults file AND to the generated schema in the same commit, so every fresh process accepts it - verified, the key is present at that commit and absent at its parent, and the working tree is byte-identical to the committed file so nothing was regenerated. The follower had been running since 12:43Z holding the PRE-commit model in memory. Loading that older generated module directly and asking it shows the field absent, so the standing process rejected a defaults file that had since gained a key it could not know about. A schema change is safe for fresh invocations and unsafe for standing ones, and nothing tells a standing process that a config contract moved underneath it. HOW IT PRESENTED, which is the part this section owns. The crash surfaced only through the watch stream: crew list and crew dispatch worked throughout, and a peer's promotion succeeded through the very code path that killed the follower, because those are fresh processes. So the one long-lived process on the project was the only casualty and the fleet looked healthy from every command. The two signals that reached this coordinator were a dispatch refused with watcher-required - the arming guard working exactly as designed, before a worktree existed - and the host harness's own notice that the monitor exited non-zero. Neither came from reckon. A session whose harness does not report an exited monitor would have held a dead follower and read the resulting silence as a quiet fleet, which is this section's opening sentence. FOUR SESSIONS DIAGNOSED IT WRONG BEFORE ANYONE DIAGNOSED IT RIGHT, this one included: it was first read here as a repository breakage, then corrected to staleness, while a peer independently reported it as a missing regeneration from a grep truncated by head -6 against a count of 8 they did not reconcile. The instrument that settled it was loading the old model and asking whether the field existed - a positive control on the absence rather than an argument about it. Also recorded: this repository has no check that regenerating the flight schema produces no diff, so the failure mode the peer wrongly diagnosed is one nothing would catch if it did occur.
A blind spot whose size is proportional to wave width, contributed by a peer coordinator and confirmed first-party here. THE FOLLOWER REPORTS ARRIVALS, NOT OUTCOMES. A dispatch refusal creates no run, so it emits no transition, so the stream is silent about it. Their instance: five triage partitions dispatched in a loop, one refused on per-member serialisation - the refusal correct and working as designed - and the pane showed five dispatched rows and nothing else. They caught it only by opening each dispatch result individually; had they trusted the pane, forty-two plans of an EXHAUSTIVE triage would have been silently uncovered, and the next chance to notice was a tiling check several nodes later. Measured in this session independently: 19 dispatched rows in the transition stream against roughly 25 dispatch attempts, so six refusals - watcher-required, member-in-flight twice, plan-unavailable, scope-conflict and unreconciled-runs - left no trace. No refusal-shaped state exists in the vocabulary at all, and the refusal strings appear nowhere on disk except in documents discussing them. Roughly a quarter of this coordinator's dispatch attempts are invisible to the surface built to report its fleet. THE SHAPE IS THE ONE THIS PLAN KEEPS FINDING, with a new twist: not a wrong answer and not a dropped field, but a question the stream cannot express, because a refusal genuinely has no run to hang a transition on. So the fix is not a synthetic row - inventing one may be worse than the gap, as they argued. What is owed is that refusals be countable somewhere a coordinator can compare against what it intended, and failing that, that the documentation say plainly that the stream cannot carry them. Harmless for a coordinator dispatching one node at a time, because the refusal is in the return value they are already reading; proportional to width for one dispatching a wave - and wave width is exactly what three sessions spent today trying to increase.

Every status the crew tooling runs against a worker's worktree takes index.lock in that worktree, and a worker commit starting inside the window fails. Reported from a third session after one of its workers failed two commits in a row on a zero-byte, unheld lock and hit its two-failure fence. Verified here.

git status performs an optional index refresh that rewrites the index and holds the lock for the duration. Four call sites run it against trees the caller does not own, and the package contains no occurrence of --no-optional-locks or GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS anywhere:

dispatch.py:3993   status --porcelain=v1 --untracked-files=all --no-renames
routing.py:353     status --porcelain=v1 -z --untracked-files=all --no-renames
routing.py:414     status --porcelain
node.py:744        status --porcelain -- <paths>

With a watcher polling every few seconds across thirty-plus worktrees, the race is routine rather than exotic, and its symptom is a worker failing its own commit for a reason that has nothing to do with the worker.

The coordinator is a source too, and that is the part available to fix today. The untrust checks this plan's own discipline requires — a clean status --porcelain before believing a manifest — are run by hand against live workers' trees. Every one of those takes the same lock. So the verification routine that exists to catch a worker's defects can manufacture one, and it leaves no trace connecting the two: the coordinator sees a clean tree, the worker sees a failed commit, and nothing correlates them.

Two repairs, and the second needs no code. In the tooling, pass --no-optional-locks (or set GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0) on every status run against a tree the caller does not own — one node, but it needs dispatch.py, currently claimed, so it queues. In practice, every coordinator adds the flag to its own untrust commands immediately, because the cost is a keystroke and the alternative is breaking the run being inspected.

The obvious repair for the lock race — set GIT_OPTIONAL_LOCKS=0 once at the git subprocess boundary — has no single place to go. There is no shared helper.

routing.py:242    _git()               subprocess.run(["git", *args], cwd=repo)   no env
node.py:650/670   _git_answer/_output  its own subprocess.run                    no env
dispatch.py:3948  _worktree_git_read   its own subprocess.run, timeout=10        no env

Three wrappers, no common boundary, and 32 subprocess.run( calls across six crew modules — resumption, routing, node, promotion, recovery, dispatch. That is almost certainly why the idiom is absent everywhere rather than missed at one site: there has never been one place to put it.

So the choice is not flag-versus-env. It is:

  • Env at the three worktree-reading wrappers — lands now, and leaves exactly the decay the env was meant to design out, since a fourth wrapper is what the history of six modules predicts.
  • One shared git-invocation helper carrying the env, with the status calls routed through it — what the reasoning actually asks for, and a refactor across six modules rather than four lines.

Recorded because the smaller version is the tempting one and it would let a coordinator report the race closed while the next added status reopens it. If the stop-gap is taken first for severity — the reporting session has now hit this twice in an hour on freshly dispatched nodes — it must be recorded as a stop-gap with the shared-helper node still open, not as the repair.

The general shape, which this plan has met several times today: a rule with nowhere to live decays into a rule nobody applies, and the absence of a boundary is the defect rather than the missing flag.

The in_flight list every plan renders counts dead runs as running — 46% of its rows on this project, and the worst-affected plan is this one. Reported by another session and reproduced here with the project interpreter:

in_flight rows total         26
...with process_alive False  12   (46%)
   crew-observability-truth      7
   a-live-worker-can-be-reached  2
   history-persists-as-detail-washes-out  1
   roadmap-answers-both-shapes   1
   figures-are-allocated-by-topic-directory  1

mcp_views.in_flight_by_plan:986 takes crew.list_live() and groups by node.plan, copying out run_id, member, section and started_at. It consults neither liveness, nor disposition, nor classification. A live pointer survives all three, so it keeps rendering as in-flight work.

The fact it needs is in the record it is already holding. crew.list_live at runs.py:435 re-derives process_alive per record, including the pid-start-time comparison that defends against pid reuse, and its own comment states the principle: a read must not report a worker as live merely because the last observer did. The grouper iterates those records and drops the field. One filter, not new machinery — and the guard worth landing with it is a case asserting a process-dead pointer does not appear, because otherwise the two drift apart again in silence.

One caution, from the reporting session and worth keeping in the node: in_flight is load-bearing for coordinators deciding whether to dispatch, so an over-aggressive filter would hide genuinely running work. Filter on liveness only. A dispositioned or terminally-classified run can still have a live process, and the pid-start-time check already handles reuse.

Recorded with an admission: this is the surface this session has been reading all afternoon. Every read_plan returns an in_flight list, and it was used to judge what was running. On this plan, seven of those rows were dead.

A sharper instance of two surfaces disagreeing about what is permitted, measured on this plan's own fleet at 13:53Z, and this one is a cycle rather than a contradiction. A run that completes with a manifest now classifies scoring, and the recovery surface's next action is to dispatch its independent review. That same run is also an unreconciled run, and once it passes the five-minute grace, dispatch refuses ALL new work for the session with unreconciled-runs, listing each blocking run beside the command that resolves it. The command it printed for the blocking run was, character for character, the review dispatch being attempted - the refusal quoted the attempt back as its own remedy. So the only exit is to waive the very run the new node exists to service, with a flag whose name says the backlog is being left deliberately when in fact it is being cleared. Worth stating as its own shape: the earlier instance had one surface prescribing what another refused, which a reader can resolve by picking a side; here neither side is wrong and the pair has no fixed point. A gate that admits a run into a state only reachable by a dispatch, while a fence refuses dispatches because the run is in that state, is a deadlock however correct each half is alone. The scoring state should not count toward the unreconciled backlog that gates its own review, or the review dispatch should be exempt from the fence. Recorded with the waiver on run r-20260914T135359878507. A refusal that names a true fact about the wrong thing, hit twice today and diagnosable only because it was hit twice. Promotion writes TWO tracked files - the ledger row and an idempotent landing comment appended to the plan HTML by _record_landing_comment at promotion.py:1083 - and commits neither. The next dispatch then reads the plan at its base revision, finds the file dirty, and refuses plan-unavailable naming the section. The section is fine. The operator learns about promotion's side effect from a later verb, in a message about a plan, minutes after the verb that caused it returned success. REORDERING DOES NOT FIX IT and the question is worth answering precisely: the defect is not the sequence of the two writes but that a tool writes tracked files and leaves the caller holding uncommitted state with no signal. Either order leaves the tree dirty. Three properties would each fix it, and they are not equal. Committing what it writes is the strongest - promotion already knows the run, the commit and the gate, and a verb that mutates two tracked stores atomically also closes the interruption window its own docstring worries about between the ledger append and the pointer removal. Refusing to run against a dirty plan is the cheapest and is symmetrical with the dispatch refusal, bounding the dirty window to one landing and reporting at the verb that causes it. Returning the comment as data for the coordinator to apply is the most orthodox, since the landing beat is already mandatory, but it removes a safety net deliberately built against a coordinator that skips the beat - which is the silent-bypass failure this repository's own guidance was written after, so it trades a known hazard for an older one. NOTE THE DOUBLE WRITE EITHER WAY: the plan is written once by the tool and once by the coordinator's mandatory landing beat, so every landing produces two comments on one section. That duplication is invisible until someone reads a section's comment list and finds the same landing described twice in two voices.

promotion commits the tracked files it writes: the ledger row and the plan landing comment land in one commit naming the run and its gate verdict, staged by explicit path and never a whole-tree add, with staging or commit failure restoring both paths and refusing rather than half-writing; removes the hanging side effect that made the next dispatch refuse plan-unavailable naming a section that was fine

Landed at ddd16eb and merged: promotion commits the tracked files it writes. The ledger row and the plan landing comment land in one commit whose subject names the promoted run and its gate verdict; paths are staged explicitly and never by a whole-tree add; a staging or commit failure restores both paths and refuses rather than half-writing. That removes the hanging side effect recorded above, where an operator learned about promotion's write from a LATER verb - a dispatch refusing plan-unavailable and naming a section that was fine - minutes after the causing verb returned success. It also narrows the mis-attribution window measured earlier today, where one commit from this session carried 37 rows of which 29 belonged to three other coordinators' plans: a landing now commits at the moment it happens rather than whenever someone next remembers. ONE RESIDUAL, NAMED RATHER THAN DISCOVERED LATER. The implementation stages by explicit path and then runs a bare commit, so anything ANOTHER session has already staged in this shared checkout is swept into the promotion commit. The test suite covers an unrelated DIRTY file and asserts it stays uncommitted; it does not cover a pre-STAGED one, and dirty is not staged. The window is the gap between a peer's git add and its git commit, which is sub-second where those are one command and unbounded where they are not. The remedy is one flag: committing the named paths only, which git treats as commit-only regardless of index state. Recorded as a follow-on rather than held against the landing, because the change is strictly better than the state it replaces and the residual is narrower than the defect it fixes - but it is the same defect one layer in, and this plan should not let that pass unnamed twice in one day.
A NODE-CONTRACT DEFECT contributed by a peer against themselves, and it is the sharpest form of a malformed node this sprint has seen because the worker did nothing wrong. They fenced a node to the surface file while its done-when said the surface renders from the payload - so the write scope and the measure pointed at different layers. The worker obeyed the fence, removed a working derivation, and wired the surface to a key nothing serves. It executed the contract exactly; the contract was incoherent. Their remedy is the right one and matches the rule this plan already carries about proving combined state: they are holding the commit unmerged and landing it with the node that serves the key, so what gets proven is THE PAIR rather than either half. Worth stating as a checkable property of the eight-property contract, since neither single-goal nor scoped nor demonstrable catches it: A NODE'S WRITE SCOPE AND ITS MEASURE MUST NAME THE SAME LAYER. A measure asserting behaviour at one layer while the fence permits writes only at another is satisfiable only by breaking something, and the worker is the one who discovers that. AND A CORRECTION TO THIS SESSION'S OWN ACCOUNT of why the manifest exchange worked. This coordinator recorded it as a peer diligently re-testing work already called done. They corrected it: the first re-run happened because it was ASKED FOR, and the miss fell out of it; only the second was diligence, and that one happened because the first had taught them the surface could still be wrong after a fix. So the transferable move is not keep re-testing - it is THE FIRST RE-TEST IS CHEAP AND SHOULD BE ASKED FOR. Recorded because the generous version of the story is the one that does not transfer: it credits a habit nobody has, where the accurate version names an action any coordinator can take in one message.

THE CHECK THAT VERIFIES THE FIX IS ITSELF THE DEFECT, and four sessions were misled by it today including this one. Reported by a peer coordinator who measured it in one beat, reproduced here independently. Node-contract validation stops at the first failing property, so a node that fails demonstrable never reaches the scope computation and the landing grant is never applied. Same role, same section, same write path, same spec level, varying only the done-when:

weak   ("it works")                findings ['demonstrable']       node.write_paths 1
strong ("...asserts 3 cases...")   findings ['scoped','scoped']    node.write_paths 3

This session was the first of the four and did not notice. The opening dry-run of the session returned findings: ['demonstrable']; the done-when was strengthened, and only then did the two scoped findings appear. That was recorded at the time as fixed the measure, then found the scope defect. The true sequence is that the short-circuit concealed the scope defect from the first probe and the discovery was luck. Three other sessions reached the opposite conclusion from the same behaviour — that the read-only regression was already fixed — one from weak probes, one from a placeholder goal, and one from a write-path count that two coordinators then spent several rounds failing to account for.

The misleading value is the path count, not the verdict. A weak done-when can never report clean, because it always fails demonstrable; nobody reads validation true, zero findings off one. What misleads is write_paths: 1 — a reader sees a single declared path, concludes the grant is no longer being appended, and calls the regression fixed. So the defect is not that validation stops early; it is that it emits a partial node as though it were a complete one. That is the same shape this plan already records for a manifest returning eight keys and no status: a partial result is worse than a refusal because it manufactures a plausible wrong answer, and plausible wrong answers are the ones that survive review.

The requirement, in the peer's own framing. Report every failing property rather than stopping at the first, so this class cannot be made unmeasurable by accident. The argument for it is specific rather than general: a dry-run probe is exactly how anyone verifies the landing-grant repair has landed, and today a sloppy probe reports success. Both defects share one cause — a check that never runs — which is why the repair and the probe that confirms the repair must not be the same node's work.

REFINEMENT TO THE REQUIREMENT ABOVE, and it changes what the node must build. The comment above names report every failing property rather than stopping at the first as the fix. The peer who found the defect corrected that after reading this framing back, and the correction is right: a node that never reached grant computation must not report a write-path list at all. Reporting a truthful-looking short list is the deception; enumerating every failing property is the second half, not the whole.

The distinction matters because the two repairs have different failure modes. Enumerating properties alone still emits write_paths: 1 beside the extra findings, so a reader skimming the path count draws the same wrong inference with more text around it. Suppressing the list — absent, not short — makes the incomplete computation unreadable as a complete one, which is the only thing that stops the inference. Do both, in that order of importance.

Stated in one sentence with its sibling, because they are one defect in two surfaces: a node whose scope was never computed and a manifest returning eight keys with no status both manufacture a plausible answer from an incomplete computation, and a plausible wrong answer is worse than a refusal because nothing prompts a second look.

Dispatch accepts a claim pattern the resume sweep treats as a conflict, and that pattern is the normal case rather than an edge one. Reported from a third session whose resume-ready sweep skipped three parked runs with scope-claimed-elsewhere, and measured here across the project's live pointers:

measurecount
live runs40
declaring docs/plans/<plan>.html or docs/evidence/archive/<plan>-landed.html34 (85%)
plans carrying more than one such claimant6
claimants on this plan alone7

The dispatch template adds both paths to every node on a plan, because every node records its own landing. Dispatch then accepts the overlap without complaint — correctly, since those two files are append-only in practice and the landing beat is serialized by the coordinator. The sweep applies an exclusivity rule to the same declarations and refuses.

The consequence is that a plan with two live nodes can never have a parked node auto-resumed. Manual crew resume goes through, so the parked work is recoverable by a coordinator who notices; nothing recovers it on its own. With 6 of 18 live plans already in that state, the sweep is disabled for exactly the plans busy enough to need it.

The repair is a shared understanding rather than a threshold: either the plan and evidence HTML are exempt from the collision check, or they are declared append-only and the check reads that. Two components currently disagree about whether the same two paths are exclusive, and only one of them refuses.

A second, smaller report of the familiar shape. The same sweep skipped a run with condition-declaration-invalid — missing resume_brief, a specific and actionable reason, while that run's classification showed only unreadable. The reason was computed and did not reach the follower row, so the reader saw the generic state and not the field to fix.

A review dispatch named a destination keyed to a run id that does not exist, and every check downstream passed. The reviewer ran, read the correct commits, scored 99, found a real defect — and wrote its record where nothing will ever look for it, while its subject correctly reported awaiting review.

dispatched --write-path  reviews/reckon/r-20260915T084936000000-a-commit-identifier-…json
actual run               r-20260915T090617465885-a-commit-identifier-…
run directory for 084936000000   does not exist

The trailing zeros are the tell: a real run id carries microsecond precision. But the value is well formed in every respect a reader checks — correct prefix, correct node suffix, correct length, plausible date — and --write-path accepts any string. The reviewer's own reviewed_commit is d7a7dc52 against base 3332b9d3, which are the right commits, so the work was sound and only its address was fiction.

This is the identifier-shaped member of the family the rest of this plan documents, and the first instance where the coordinator originated the bad value rather than misreading an existing one. The repo already carries the rule for commits — verify existence, not shape, git rev-parse --verify <sha>^{commit} — and there is no counterpart for a run id in a dispatch path. A dispatch that resolved its --write-path against the live pointer set would have refused this in one comparison.

Disposed by re-dispatching rather than renaming the file. A copy and one edited field would have been cheaper, and it would have made the coordinator the author of the review evidence that gates its own promotion — which is the arrangement §21 exists to prevent, wearing a different hat. One worker on an unmetered lane is the cheaper thing to spend. The orphaned record stays on disk as corroboration.

one line in dispatch.py plus 37 test lines: observe now writes the throughput block it already computed to every CLI live pointer. Agreement falsifier met on a recorded stream, 664.405 s against 664405 ms; the unmeasurable branch records an explicit reason rather than an absent key. Base 6 and head 6 identical failures. Coordinator verified on a real pointer after merge: the key is present and carries detail 'turn tokens recorded, but no span was supplied to rate them', so a reader can now tell unmeasured from unrecorded

A caution that arrived with the takedown and bears directly on this section's first measure. The lane ran today at a median 8.6 tokens per second against 17.5 to 101.4 on the ten prior days, and the cause was neither contention nor node shape: the serve's own log shows 1,353 of 1,453 prefill batches with zero cached tokens, so every agent turn re-prefilled roughly 100k tokens from scratch. The KV pool never exceeded 10 percent occupancy, so nothing was evicted for space, and back-to-back identical requests still cached at 9.8 times - a defect in the serving build rather than a tuning state. All three of this session's scouts overran their 40-minute budgets by 150 to 160 percent. Under this section's first measure that overrun must say whether the node was too large or the lane too busy, and the honest answer here is neither of the stated alternatives: the lane was generating normally by request count and abnormally by rate, against a broken prefix cache. Two consequences. Any throughput or burn figure recorded today measures a serve generation rather than the lane, so it must carry the serve identity before it lands anywhere as a lane characteristic - the same defect as a banner figure read across a configuration change, which this session already got wrong once. And the node-size conclusion this session might have drawn from three consecutive overruns would have been false: the briefs were not too large, and re-sizing them on that evidence would have shrunk future nodes for a reason that no longer exists once the serve is replaced.

THIS SECTION'S DEFECT MEASURED LIVE, ON A NODE OF THIS PLAN, WHILE A PEER WAS WAITING ON THE ANSWER. The landing-grant repair node passed its 1,500-second budget and nothing said so. It was alive and generating, 57 assistant records and 7,661 stream records, with a clean worktree — no diff and no commit — having spent the entire budget on diagnosis. Nothing was wrong with it; its own reasoning had reached the correct fix, both halves including the prompt side. But the coordinator learned all of that by opening the stream file and reading the last fourteen assistant records by hand, which is the same instrument this plan exists to replace.

The cost was paid by someone else, which is what makes it more than an inconvenience. A peer coordinator asked whether the node was close or had hit something, because their lead needed to choose between waiting and starting other work, and their closure is hard-gated behind it. The honest answer existed — close, no obstacle — and reaching it required a reader who knew which file to open. Both measures on this section name that gap precisely: an overrun that says whether the node was too large or the lane too busy, and one that messages the worker and terminates nothing. A third reader is implied by this instance and is not currently written down: the overrun should also be legible to a coordinator answering for the run, not only to the worker inside it.

A calibration figure worth keeping separate from the defect. Four of five nodes in this wave were still in diagnosis at twenty minutes on the locally served lane; one had committed. The role default for implement is eight minutes and this wave was dispatched at twenty-five, which the first node exceeded before writing anything. That is a budget set from impression rather than from this lane's measured distribution — the plan already records a median local review wall of 812 seconds over nine runs, and implementation is longer. The figure is a starting point for the agent-time population this section's threshold measure asks for, not a number to write into a default: the point of that measure is that the threshold is derived from the stream_events subset rather than chosen, and a hand-set twenty-five minutes is the same class of error as the hand-set completion fraction this plan corrected twice yesterday.

THE OVERRUN THIS SECTION DESCRIBES WAS MEASURED TODAY, AND ITS CAUSE WAS NOT THE NODE. Recorded earlier in this section as a node that passed its budget with nothing reporting it. That reading was incomplete and the completion changes the measure. A peer coordinator counted every live worker on the host and found the lane divided many ways; counted independently here minutes later at 37 live workers on one locally served lane, across 9 sessions and 4 projects. Aggregate serve was healthy — about 347 tokens per second, above the figure the lane's redeploy targeted. Divided 37 ways it is single digits each. Measured on this session's own five nodes, output tokens over real elapsed:

the-landing-grant   12,938 tok / 4,282 s = 3.02 tok/s
a-second-key        10,190 tok / 4,080 s = 2.50 tok/s
promotion-stops      6,244 tok / 3,973 s = 1.57 tok/s
an-endpoint          9,452 tok / 3,438 s = 2.75 tok/s
review-of-reason     2,390 tok / 1,714 s = 1.39 tok/s
                                  five combined = 11.2 tok/s of 37 workers

Six coordinators each diagnosed their own slow nodes, and none of them saw the contention. This one widened its time budgets and recorded the lane as slow for this model. A peer read three nodes as slow for half an hour before thinking to measure the neighbours. Both conclusions were wrong in the same way: a per-node instrument attributes a fleet-wide cause to whichever node the reader happens to own. That is worse than an unreported overrun with a node at fault, because the per-node reading is not merely absent — it is available and misleading, and every coordinator independently reaches for it.

What the measure must carry, sharpened by the peer who found it. An overrun report naming only the run's own elapsed against its budget will be read as a node defect by default, because that is the only frame it offers. One that also carries the lane's concurrent-run count at the time of the overrun would have pointed six sessions at the real cause in one line. This is the first measure of this section — an overrun says whether the node was too large or the lane too busy — and the concurrency figure is precisely the quantity that separates the two. Today nothing surfaces it: crew(view="flight") reports headroom, which is a question about quota, while the question that mattered was offered load.

A consequence for fleet sizing that is not obvious from the instruction. A coordinator told to run a lane at full width will keep widening while every surface reports the lane healthy, because aggregate throughput is healthy and no surface divides it by the worker count. Past the point where aggregate saturates, each additional worker subtracts from every other worker on the host, including other sessions' — so the honest reading of spare local capacity is not the aggregate figure but the aggregate divided by offered load, and that second number exists nowhere.

THIS SECTION'S DESIGNED RESPONSE TO AN OVERRUN CANNOT BE DELIVERED, AND THE SECTION WOULD SHIP DESCRIBING A PROTECTION THAT IS NOT THERE. Raised by a peer coordinator against this plan. The section records a lead direction: a worker is never killed at its runtime gate, and the response to an overrun is to message the worker. Measured this morning and independently reproduced by a census node since: zero messages delivered into 3,971 crew worker run streams, against a positive control of 1,204 delivered records in interactive transcripts. A CLI-launched worker is a headless process whose permission mode holds the message for an approval no such process can give, and it expires unread. Resume is refused while the process is alive — measured, exit 1, still has a live process; observe or stop it before resuming. So for a worker mid-turn and overrunning, which is precisely this section's case, there is no channel at all.

The direction is right and is not what is being questioned. Not killing a working worker is correct, and this session is its own evidence: the landing-grant node ran 109 minutes against a 25-minute budget and was healthy throughout, and a peer's two nodes passed three hours while starved rather than runaway. What is missing is a delivery mechanism for the thing the lead asked for, not a change of mind about the thing.

The consequence is this section's own title. A soft limit that speaks to nobody is not one — and if the designed response is a message that cannot arrive, the section describes a protection that does not exist. That is the defect class this plan exists to find, arriving in the section that names it, which is the third time today a measure of this plan has been failed by this plan's own tooling.

Disposition: this section's second measure is blocked on a mechanism owned elsewhere, and is recorded as such rather than quietly shipped. A peer's plan builds the delivery — a worker reads an inbox in its own run directory at named checkpoints, since a file dropped there is never picked up passively: the worker's inotify watches the harness config directory, not the run directory. Until that lands, the measure an overrun messages the worker and terminates nothing is satisfiable only in its second half, and this section must not claim the first. The other half of the section — an overrun that says whether the node was too large or the lane too busy — is unaffected and is where the load figures of §22 land.

Three written instructions across this repository tell a coordinator to message a worker, of which this section is one; the others are in the sprint-orchestration reference, at its commit-rework step and at a coordinator authority grant. All three were verified by reading rather than taken from the report. They are wrong in the same way and should be qualified together when the channel exists.

Baseline re-measured before dispatch over the whole watch corpus on this workstation, 6,844 transition rows of which 554 carry the spend schema. Promoted rows: 121, and every spend figure is null on all 121 - wall seconds, charged tokens, model seconds, generation rate, notional cost and machine seconds all at 0.0 percent. The section was authored against 90 of 90; it is now 121 of 121, and the figure moves because the corpus grows, so a node must read it at dispatch rather than quote this one. The mechanism is no longer inferred: spend_folded_run_count and spend_measured_stream_count are present on all 121 promoted rows and both are zero on every one, which is the fold running against a pointer list the promotion has already emptied, reported as an honest zero rather than as an absence. That is the first measure's negative half made concrete - a run that generated nothing and a run whose evidence was deleted are today indistinguishable, both reading zero. Verified independently of the peer report that raised it.
The 121-of-121 population is superseded as this section's primary evidence by a controlled pair inside a single run, which settles what the node must change. Run local-flag-refuses-a-disagreeing-member emitted two transitions 46 seconds apart. At 12:49:26 into completed_unpromoted: wall 6,143.3 seconds, charged tokens 8,700,767, folded run count 1, measured stream count 2 - every figure present and correct. At 12:50:12 into promoted: wall null, tokens null, model seconds null, rate null, folded run count 0, measured stream count 0. Same run, same day, one minute apart, and its committed ledger row carries the full record throughout - wall 5,726 seconds, generation 2,568.2, machine 137.3, 10.33 tokens per second, peak input 142,037. So the derivation is not broken and promotion does not lose the data: the ledger path keeps it and the transition path recomputes it after promotion has emptied the pointer list it reads from, then reports zero folded runs and zero streams as an honest measurement of nothing. This section's subject is therefore the TRANSITION surface specifically, not promotion and not the ledger, and the node needs no stratified design over whether a run was observed first - a caution raised and then withdrawn by the peer who raised it, on finding all thirteen of their own rows carried figures either way. Note the two surfaces disagree even where both are populated: the transition read 6,143.3 seconds and 8,700,767 tokens where the ledger read 5,726 and 6,826,813, so a reconciliation is owed beyond restoring the nulls.
Sequencing, and it reverses the obvious order. The transition-versus-ledger disagreement is to be repaired BEFORE the nulls, not after, and the argument is that restoring the nulls first would make the fleet worse rather than better. Today the two surfaces disagree only where both happen to be populated, which is a minority of rows; fill the nulls without reconciling and every run carries two different answers instead of a few. A null announces itself and sends its reader elsewhere. Two populated figures that disagree announce nothing, and both coordinators on this lane spent the afternoon quoting whichever surface was to hand - one of them passed 6,826,813 tokens across this channel from the ledger with no idea a second figure of 8,700,767 existed for the same run. That is the failure this plan is named for, produced by the repair rather than prevented by it. So the node's first deliverable is a stated authority between the two paths with the other derived from it or marked as the estimate it is, and the nulls follow from that rather than preceding it.
Correction to this section's scope, from the reuse map and verified at the base revision before accepting it. The second measure is PARTIAL rather than absent, and the half already satisfied is the ledger path: promotion resolves wall, budget and throughput BEFORE the pointer is unlinked, and the code says so in its own comment at promotion.py:2495 - 'it has to be captured before the pointer goes' - immediately above the unlink. Measured on a sibling project's ledger, 1,088 of 1,127 rows carry wall_seconds and 351 carry throughput. So the emptiness is confined to the rendered watch transitions, whose spend is re-derived from the live-pointer list after promotion has emptied it, at recovery.py:2561 through the callers at 2714 and 2744-2753. Two consequences for the repair brief, both from the map's own follow-ons. It must state which promoted row it means, or a node will implement the already-satisfied ledger half a second time. And the first measure needs a decision locked before dispatch that this plan does not currently make: the discard path writes no ledger row at all today, so a disposition field on the row has nothing to sit on for the very case that motivated it - either discard begins appending a row, or the disposition travels on the transition instead. That is a design choice with a real cost on a ledger already large enough to matter, and it is not the node's to make.

reuse map for the lane-event, promoted-row and grid sections: 9 measure rows with verdicts and citations, per-section write-path sets naming asserting tests; narrowed the promoted-row spend measure to the transition surface by establishing the ledger path already resolves before pointer unlink; review 78/100, two defects recorded (one citation resolving to a blank line, one enumeration summing 393 against a stated 401 by omitting a seventh state)

Second reuse map promoted, gate passed against an independent scored review, no commit declared deliberately. And the map's own enumeration of non-landing states sums to 393 against its stated 401, omitting a seventh state carrying roughly eight rows, so its breakdown and its headline disagree. Neither touches the section's conclusions; both are recorded because implementation nodes will cite this map and must not inherit a dead line reference. Note for the third time today that this run's own promotion transition rendered five empty spend cells one minute after its complete transition carried a full wall, model time, token total and rate.

independent review of the promoted-row and grid reuse map: 78/100, re-measured the headline figures against the live corpus and held the zero at a third population, found one citation resolving to a blank line and one enumeration disagreeing with its own headline

This plan's own distinction found violated inside the record store, by a repair that was nearly walked past as pre-existing. Four failures in the routing-evidence module were carried as 'pre-existing, not mine' until the lead's correction that failures are fixed when met rather than stepped over. They were not stale tests: three of four were SOURCE defects, and the first is this section's subject exactly. build_record forced follow_on_paths onto every promoted row by setdefault to None, so a promotion with no manifest - never measured - became indistinguishable from a promotion whose manifest declared an empty list. The module's own docstring and the comment beside that code state the contract it violated. The tests were asserting the correct contract against a source that had stopped honouring it, which is why they read as noise rather than as a finding. Base 4 failed and 17 passed; after 0 failed and 21 passed, every failure id named and each attributed to source or test with its reason. Repaired by deleting the forced default: the existing emission already records a real list, possibly empty, when a manifest declared one. Worth keeping as the general lesson rather than the specific fix - a failing test that has been red long enough to be called pre-existing is the most likely place for a contract the code has quietly stopped meeting, because nobody has read it in a while and its noise is what makes it safe to ignore.
THE FIRST MERGE CONFLICT FROM THE WORKER-AUTHORED PLAN MODEL, and it validated the model rather than exposing a flaw in it. Merging a worker's commit left docs/plans/crew-observability-truth.html unmerged: the tool's auto-generated landing comment and the worker's own authored record collided on THE SAME COMMENT ID, with different authors and different bodies. That is the double write this plan recorded when analysing promotion's side effect - every landing producing two comments in two voices - arriving as a real conflict the first time a worker authored its own. Resolved in favour of the worker's record on the evidence rather than on principle: 1,118 characters against 607, and it quotes both conflicting clauses verbatim because it was written by the agent that read them. The coordinator's was an outcome string. Verified rather than assumed: comment count 274 to 273, exactly the one duplicate removed, the worker's text retained, no other content touched, audit-doc clean, and a pre-resolution copy kept before editing. WHAT IT PROVES AND WHAT IT OWES. It proves the argument for worktree edits over version-paired writes: git made a collision visible and resolvable that a version-paired write would have settled silently by last-writer-wins, with the shorter record winning on timing rather than on merit. It owes the repair it names - the tool must stop writing a comment the worker now authors, or every landing conflicts on the same id forever. That is a change to promotion rather than to the contract, and it is unlanded.

TWO PROMOTION DEFECTS MEASURED IN ONE BEAT, BOTH CONSEQUENCES OF THE LANDING GRANT REACHING SURFACES THAT WERE NOT RECONSIDERED WITH IT.

First: a running node's uncommitted plan edit blocks promotion of every other node on that plan. Promoting the reason-clip node was refused:

...crew-observability-truth.html in peer worktree
   .../ship-s18-20260915/promotion-stops-authoring-the-record-the-worker-writes

That peer is a live run of this same session, mid-flight, with M docs/plans/crew-observability-truth.html in its worktree because the landing contract told it to author its record there. Promotion checks declared paths for uncommitted changes including in peer worktrees, and the grant makes the plan file a declared path of every node on the plan. So while any worker on a plan is still composing its landing record, no other node on that plan can be promoted. Promotion serialises behind the slowest worker on the plan, and the wait is unbounded because a worker authoring prose has no deadline. Measured cost here: one verified, merged, independently reviewed node held out of the ledger by an unrelated sibling that happened to still be writing.

Second: a review run cannot be promoted at all. Its deliverable is a JSON record in the review store, outside every git tree, which the manifest correctly lists under changed_paths. Promotion then refuses:

has a complete manifest with changed_paths, but the manifest field 'commits'
is missing. Promotion cannot verify changed repository paths without the
commit that contains them

and --no-commit, which exists to declare exactly this, does not clear it — the check reads changed_paths as repository paths without asking whether any of them is inside a repository. So every review node terminates, delivers its record, and then sits unpromotable. Four are in that state now. This is the scoring lifecycle unable to close itself: reviews gate promotion, and reviews cannot be promoted, so a fleet that uses the gate accumulates pointers it can never reconcile.

Both are the same shape as the defect this session opened with. A change correct in its own surface — grant the landing paths, verify changed paths against a commit — meets a second surface that was written under the old assumption, and the contradiction only appears at the seam. The grant's tests proved dispatch admits a second node and promotion accepts a run that wrote both paths; neither asked what happens when a third node is still holding the file, or when the changed path is not in a repository at all.

The conflict this section's final record names is repaired in promotion rather than in the contract. Promotion previously appended its own landing comment for a run whose manifest carried a worker-authored record under the same run-derived comment id, so the merge that brought the record in collided on a duplicate id. The landing path now reads the plan at the run's submitted commit (via git show against the shared object store) and, when that committed plan already carries the run's comment id, appends nothing and leaves the plan file untouched. A run without a worker-authored record still lands exactly one comment, asserted in both directions: the worker-authored case records no second comment and a landing commit carrying only the ledger row, and the silent-worker case records exactly one comment plus the plan write. Gate 40 passed at base to 42 after with two tests added and zero added failures, run in-tree against the shared environment.

THREE LEDGER ROWS RECORD NO CHANGED PATHS BECAUSE THIS COORDINATOR EDITED THEIR MANIFESTS TO GET PAST A DEFECT, AND THE SEAM IS RECORDED HERE RATHER THAN REPAIRED AWAY.

r-20260915T071924465132-rev-reason-clip           changed_paths=None  commits=[]
r-20260915T072152674252-rev-manifest-second-key   changed_paths=None  commits=[]
r-20260915T072157380062-rev-endpoint-handle       changed_paths=None  commits=[]

What happened. Promotion demands a commit for any non-empty changed_paths without asking whether the path is inside the repository, so a review run whose only deliverable is a JSON record in the review store could not promote. Each of these three had that path correctly listed; this coordinator moved it out of changed_paths and left it under artifacts, where the worker had already recorded it, and promoted. The reasoning at the time — that the record genuinely is an artifact rather than a changed repository path — is the same judgement the dispatched predicate repair now encodes. The difference is that it was asserted in three manifests instead of in the check, so it applied to three rows rather than to everyone, and it left the ledger saying something the workers had not said.

Why it is documented rather than corrected. crew repair-completion re-derives completion measurements and does not reach changed_paths; the rows store None rather than an empty list, so there is no in-place repair that does not amount to editing committed evidence a second time. Editing the ledger to match what the manifests originally said would be the same act that produced the seam, applied again with more confidence.

What a later reader needs to know, which is the whole point of writing it down. Once the predicate repair lands, a review run promoting through the corrected check will record its deliverable path. At that moment these three siblings will differ from every later run of the same shape, in the same store, with nothing in the ledger explaining why — because the explanation is that a coordinator hand-edited them at 07:19, 07:21 and 07:22 on 2026-09-15 to clear a queue. That is the sentence a future reader would otherwise have to reconstruct, and could not.

Raised by the peer who declined to do it. They hit the identical defect, refused to reword a worker's record so a check would pass, and held two runs unreconciled instead — which is what forced the predicate to be fixed rather than absorbed. They then pointed out that the window for documenting this closes as soon as the fix lands and the set stops being obvious, and that the only person who still knew which three rows they were was the one who edited them.

THE DUPLICATE-COMMENT GUARD MISSES SILENTLY WHEN A WORKER'S RECORD COMMIT IS NOT LAST, AND THE MERGE THAT LANDED IT ADDED A LINT FINDING. Fourth independent review of this session, fourth real defect in already-merged work. Scored 89 — goal fidelity 19, evidence 18, fit 19, scope discipline 17, durability 16. Both findings reproduced here:

promotion.py:1119   shown = _git(tree, "show", f"{revisions[-1]}:{path}", check=False)
test_crew_promotion.py:1796:5   F841 Local variable 'path' is assigned to but never used

The first is this plan's own subject. _worker_authored_landing_record reads only revisions[-1] — the last commit a manifest declares. A worker that lists its record commit anywhere but last, or omits it, makes the guard find nothing, promotion writes its own comment anyway, and the duplicate-id collision this section exists to prevent recurs with no signal at all. A guard that reports absence when it simply did not look is the defect this plan opened with, arriving in the fix for a different instance of it. The repair is to scan the declared commits rather than the last one, or to state in the docstring why last is sufficient — and nothing currently states it, which is why the reviewer could not tell a decision from an oversight.

The second is a rule this repository writes down and this coordinator merged past. Your commit adds no findings — judged by delta, not by absolute count. The merge introduced exactly one: an assignment in an added test helper that is never read. Small, and the point is not its size: the delta check exists precisely so that a backlog does not absorb new debt invisibly, and it was not run before merging.

Both are queued rather than dispatched, because the files are claimed. A node repairing the changed-paths predicate is in flight against promotion.py and tests/test_crew_promotion.py — the same two files. Dispatching a second worker onto them would produce exactly the collision this session has spent the day resolving, so these wait for that landing and go to the same member, whose session will still hold the file's context.

Four reviews, four findings, all in work this coordinator verified and merged. An unauthorised silence checked off against the wrong clause; a regex never probed against prose; a naming rule not applied to a subject line; and now a guard that looks at one commit and a lint finding that went unmeasured. None was a careless worker. The measure that separates this mechanism from the saturated gate it replaced is that it has yet to return nothing.

A promoted row asserts the effort the node was dispatched with, not the effort its resumed attempt actually ran with — and the resume path carries no effort flag at all. Verified on this session's own row.

run r-20260915T101223859925-a-finished-run-is-not-stalled-retry
  agent.effort   high          ← from the node definition
  agent.model    deepseek-v4-flash
  attempt        2
  attempt_kind   resume

That run was resumed after the serve restart, and _backend_settings reconstructs a backend as {"launch": "cli", "command": argv[0]}, so the relaunch carries neither --effort nor --model. The agent block records intent; the process ran without it.

The row is not silent about this, which narrows the finding. It carries attempt: 2 and attempt_kind: resume, so a consumer can tell a resumed run from a fresh one. capabilities.py:581 already computes exactly that, including an attempt > 1 fallback, and emits it at :766 as a reported redispatched field.

What is missing is the join, not the data. The effort grouping at capabilities.py:739-747 reads agent.effort and buckets by it without consulting either field, and ledger.measurement_exclusion_reason excludes for contamination, scope change, stall and unusable completion — with no clause for a resumed attempt. So resumed runs feed effort calibration carrying an effort they may not have used, while the fact that would exclude them sits computed in the same module.

This is the third finding today with that exact shape: in_flight dropping the process_alive its own records carry, the classifier ignoring a liveness fact its comment promises to honour, and now an effort bucket ignoring a resume flag the same file computes. In each case the repair is a join rather than new machinery, and in each case nothing fails while the two halves stay apart.

Correction: I recorded above that the effort bucket does not consult the exclusion path. It does. And the two rows cited as evidence — one mine, one the reporting session's — are both already excluded, for a reason neither of us checked.

capabilities.py:717 calls _routing_outcome_exclusion(run) and continues on a reason before any effort bucketing. So the join exists. Both example rows carry stalled: True and are excluded by it — a different fact that correlates, because a resume usually follows a stall. I verified the finding on a row that contaminates nothing, and the peer asserted it about another.

The contamination is real and it is elsewhere: 46 rows of 983, 4.7% — retried runs carrying no exclusion reason at all, so they do reach effort calibration with an effort their attempt may not have used.

resume       38
redispatch    6
lane-change   2

Which decides the filter, and rules out the obvious instrument. _redispatched() returns True for all three kinds, because its attempt > 1 fallback catches anything retried. But a redispatch re-runs with its own fresh argv, so its recorded effort is accurate — filtering on _redispatched would discard 8 sound measurements to repair a 38-row problem. The clause must discriminate on attempt_kind == "resume". The field is named for redispatch and its value means retried, which is the stable-name-unstable-referent shape recorded elsewhere on this plan.

The shape is not the one I filed it under. in_flight and the classifier are both an authoritative value one call away and unjoined. This is joined, and the protection is incidental: resumed rows are excluded for being stalled, never for being resumed. That is more fragile than an unjoined value, because it reads as correct from outside and lapses silently for precisely the population that is not stalled.

My own count came to 55 before it came to 46, because I ran measurement_exclusion_reason against a claim about _routing_outcome_exclusion — the shadow check the latter applies first is what differs. Same instrument error as the rest of today, inside the correction of an instrument error.

Population re-measured over the same 554-row spend-carrying corpus. The two dead columns hold: model seconds non-null in 1.3 percent (7 of 554) and generation rate in 1.3 percent (7 of 554), against the 1.9 percent over 366 rows this section was authored from - the rate fell as the corpus grew, so the columns are getting emptier rather than filling. Charged tokens 53.2 percent, notional cost 26.0 percent, wall time 63.5 percent. On dispatched rows specifically, wall time is populated in 88.2 percent (120 of 136) and is noise in every one of them by definition, which is the section's sharpest case: the most-populated cell on the most common row carries no information. Machine seconds is worse than either dead column at 0.5 percent. The ordering and the cut proposed in this section are read against these figures.
Sequencing constraint from a peer coordinator, recorded because it expires. The row renderer this section rewrites is also the delivery half of a lane-advisory endpoint whose delivery-point decision was locked today to the dispatch-and-follower option, so that endpoint's work lands in the same file. It was described as two waves out; its leading node was dispatched at 12:53Z, one minute after that message was written, so the gap is smaller than either of us thought. This section should land first, which suits both: its measures are stated and its baseline is measured, while the endpoint is still at its own reuse-map stage. If it does not land first, the two changes contend for one file and the later one inherits a renderer it did not design.
A sharper version of the derived-figure measure above, and it corrects an assumption this plan's staleness design was carrying unexamined. Measured on the lane budget: it collapses within the FIRST MINUTE of a wave dispatching, not over twenty. Three consecutive polls at unchanged running count of 19 read mean context 15,586 then about 62,865 then 73,121, and headroom 122 then 16 then 14. Nothing joined or left; the same nineteen requests went from their first tokens to their working context and the figure fell fivefold in thirty seconds. A freshly dispatched worker's first request carries almost no context, so a reading taken immediately after a dispatch catches the fleet at its lightest and is roughly eightfold too generous - which is precisely when a pre-flight reader would take it. The consequence for any staleness rule, including this plan's: A VERY FRESH READING CAN BE LESS TRUSTWORTHY THAN AN OLDER ONE, so age alone is not quality and newer-is-better is false in a measurable direction. The resolution is that age and settling are two orthogonal qualifiers and the error is asking one field to carry both. Age governs staleness - has the world moved since we looked. Settling governs validity - was the quantity steady when we looked. A reading can be one second old and invalid or ninety seconds old and sound, which is why no age bound can express it. That is the figure-cannot-carry-its-own-validity rule applied one level up, and it is detectable only where the polling happens: a producer holding consecutive samples sees mean context climbing at constant running, while a reader holding one sample cannot distinguish that from a steady state. Proposed to the lane's owner as a published settled-versus-settling qualifier whose headroom is an upper bound rather than a figure while settling. Recorded here because the same distinction governs every derived figure this surface renders, not only this one. NOTE ON SCOPE: the pre-flight node dispatched at 16:18:32Z names state, headroom and the binding flag and does NOT name this qualifier, which did not exist when its measures were written - it lands as the core reader and the settling qualifier is a follow-on, recorded so the gap is visible rather than assumed closed.

A rendered timestamp defect in this section's own surface, measured first-hand rather than taken on report. The ticker pane prints LOCAL time and labels it nowhere, while every durable record is UTC. Verified on one event of this session: the pane rendered 21:44:15 for a promotion whose stored transition reads observed_at 2026-09-14T19:44:15Z. A two-hour offset, no marker, no zone. The cost is not that a reader gets the wrong minute - it is that a two-hour gap DOES NOT LOOK LIKE A FORMATTING DIFFERENCE, it looks like a real discrepancy, and a reader comparing a pane against a record will try to explain it. Contributed independently by the lane's owner, who first attributed the trap to scheduler output and then corrected themselves: the pane is the surface a peer actually hit, and two sessions quoted a local time with a Z suffix inside one hour, from two different surfaces. This whole plan turns on that class of error - a figure that is arithmetically fine and wrong in its frame, undetectable by inspection because nothing about it looks wrong. The rule to implement: a rendered timestamp carries its zone or is rendered in UTC, and where a pane must be compact the record it can be compared against must use the same frame. The general discipline for a reader until then: pair from run ids and from date -u, never from a rendered pane or a quoted scheduler line.

ONE PERCENTAGE, FOUR DENOMINATORS, AND NOTHING SAYS WHICH ONE A ROW USED WITHOUT READING ITS PROSE. Reported by a peer coordinator from a single promoted row — throughput.input_budget_tokens reading 200,000 while agent.usable_input_window in the same record read 1,016,576, so input_utilisation_pct recorded 84.2 where the configured window gives 16.6. Measured across the whole committed ledger here, it is not one row:

rows where input_budget_tokens != usable_input_window:  114

distinct denominators actually used:
  1,016,576 tokens  ->  130 rows
    200,000 tokens  ->  111 rows
  1,000,000 tokens  ->   30 rows
    492,288 tokens  ->    1 row

And it disagrees in both directions, which rules out a single stale constant: some rows divide by a budget larger than the agent's declared window (1,016,576 against 492,288), others by one five times smaller (200,000 against 1,016,576).

The consequence is that the column is not comparable to itself. Two rows reading 40% and 60% may describe the same load, or opposite loads, depending on which of four denominators each inherited. Any aggregate, any threshold, any sort over input_utilisation_pct is meaningless across the ledger, and a reader has no way to know without opening each row's detail string.

This section's measure names exactly this and the row half-satisfies it. A derived figure renders with its vintage and its denominator — and the detail string does name the window it used, which is why the peer could diagnose it at all and why the row is honest rather than wrong. What the measure did not anticipate is that naming the denominator per row is insufficient when the denominator varies between rows: honesty at the row level produces incomparability at the column level, and the surface that aggregates them inherits the incoherence silently.

It bears directly on §22's routing metric. An overrun report or a lane-load reading that quotes input utilisation inherits whichever denominator its row happened to record. The figures §22 asks for are meant to decide whether to dispatch to a lane; a denominator that varies five-fold between rows would make that decision on noise. The repair wanted is that the utilisation is computed against the declared window for the agent that ran, with the stream's own window recorded beside it rather than substituted for it — a second field, not a corrected single one, because the two answer different questions and today one is quietly standing in for the other.

Moved to a-monitor-row-reads-at-a-glance, whose §4 drops the dead rate and age cells and whose §6 cuts the reason clause at a word. The lead asked on 2026-09-22 for the destination state only and no unreadable cells. This section's 366-row measurement is cited there. Deferred here so the work is counted once.

One lane, one afternoon, four surfaces and four different figures for its context window - found while checking something else, which is how all of these arrive. The routing config declares 172,800 usable input. The engine enforces a 204,800 cap, from which that figure is derived by subtracting the launcher's 32,000 output reservation. The launcher's own banner prints 1,016,576 on every stderr, including runs launched against the capped serve, so a worker reading its own banner sizes itself 5.9 times too large. And the throughput block written onto every committed row computes input utilisation against a fourth number, 200,000: the pair measured here read 61.1 and 71.0 percent against peak inputs of 122,144 and 142,037, which is 200,000 exactly and matches none of the other three. Only the first is what dispatch fences on. The banner is the dangerous one because it is the surface a worker sees without asking, and the utilisation percentage is the quiet one because it is arithmetically consistent with itself and therefore never looks wrong. Both are this plan's subject in the same shape as the lane event: a confident figure describing a configuration that is no longer running. Corrected reading of how it arrived, since the first attempt got it wrong twice: the serve was replaced with one enforcing a smaller cap, the declared figure was updated to match with a dated reason, and nobody edited a shared value out from under a wave. A file mtime says something changed, never which value, and a pre-edit copy held by a peer showed 172,800 already present forty minutes before the mtime this session had pinned the change to.
Five figures, not four, and two of them sit on a single committed record. Verified on the row for local-flag-refuses-a-disagreeing-member, dispatched 11:07:03Z: agent.usable_input_window reads 1,016,576 while throughput.input_budget_tokens on the same row reads 200,000, and the utilisation is computed against the second - 142,037 peak input at 71.0 percent implies a denominator of 200,052. Neither equals the 172,800 the config declares nor the 204,800 the engine enforces. So a reader wanting that run's headroom can take either field off one row and be wrong in two different directions without leaving the row. The utilisation figure is the one that can never be caught by inspection, and that is worth stating as the reason rather than as an aside: 71.0 against 142,037 is arithmetically perfect, so it survives every consistency check that could be run on it, and it is wrong only because its denominator is a constant matching no window this lane has ever had. Internal consistency is what makes it undetectable, which inverts the usual assumption that a self-consistent record is a trustworthy one. A third ordering correction travels with it, the same trap for the third time today: a dispatch records the window at launch, so the first run carrying a new value bounds the change from ABOVE, not below. This run carries the old figure at 11:07Z and a peer's file copy carries the new one at 12:09:55Z, so the change landed between those - earlier than the 12:49Z this session had inferred from the first dispatch that showed it.

Settled by probe rather than by reasoning, on the cheapest of three held runs, and the answer carries a defect. A resume after a deployment swap DOES cross models and does not consult the roster's model-keyed session map: the review run resumed at 14:22Z and its resume stream carries model deepseek-v4-flash on all thirteen records, so it is genuinely running on the replacement. Work is preserved and every turn survives, which is the outcome that matters and the reason to resume rather than redispatch. The defect is that no surface says the model changed. The live pointer's agent block still reads deepseek-v4.1-flash with alias dsv4.1-flash and window 172,800, all captured at dispatch; the ticker renders dsv4.1-flash on the very transition that reports the resume; and a promotion would commit that dispatch-time model onto the ledger row. So a run whose second half executed on a different deployment is attributed entirely to the first, and its wall time, token totals, generation rate and imputed cost all land under a model that produced only part of them. The ticker's model column is documented as read from the configuration persisted at dispatch precisely so a later config change cannot silently restate what ran - correct in intent, and here it produces the false statement instead of preventing one, because what changed was not the config but the run. This is the stamping caution recorded one section over, sharpened: the stamp exists, it is written once, and a resume is exactly the event that invalidates it. Any lane comparison drawing on today's rows must exclude or split the three resumed ones, and none of them should enter calibration as evidence about either deployment.
The second measure now has both of its cases measured 22 minutes apart on one lane, which is the strongest evidence available for it and was not obtainable before today. At 14:11:41Z three runs were held with the reason 'backend clive refused the turn on a rate-limit (consumer queue backpressure) after N retries' while the endpoints document held zero endpoints and the deployment was being replaced - the reason was wrong. At 14:33:48Z a review run was held with the SAME reason while the endpoints document listed one endpoint and the lane was serving normally, and this time the reason was right: its stream carries 11 records with error rate_limit and 10 api_retry system records, against 20 live pointers and 7 working across three coordinators sharing one serve. So the string is identical in both cases and the cause is not, which means the reason as emitted cannot discriminate and no amount of reading it more carefully would help. The discriminator is the endpoints document, which the routing pre-flight already reads and the classifier does not consult: zero endpoints means a catalog change, endpoints present with retry records means genuine backpressure. That is the measure restated as an implementation, and it is now falsifiable against two recorded runs rather than one. Note the terminal record's own subtype reads success with terminal_reason api_error in the backpressure case, so the subtype remains unusable as a signal on this dialect - the same trap recorded for the morning cluster.
A hypothesis this coordinator raised and a peer refuted with measurement, and the refutation exposes a gap in this section's third measure. The hypothesis was that six concurrent workers might be past what four cards serve at this context length, making fleet width a hardware question. It fails: the engine reports 70.01 GiB of KV per card and 2,557,835 tokens at ZERO PERCENT occupancy with 1,024 sequence slots, while the router refuses 48 percent of the last 120 requests and 37.4 percent over its whole log of 1,075 - 673 served, 252 refused 503, 112 refused 429. A serve with a thousand idle slots is not shedding for want of cards. The mechanism is an admission limit in the router, max_in_flight 2 and max_queued 4 with a 30-second queue wait, keyed per consumer - and the consumer id falls back to client host plus user agent when the identifying header is absent. Every agent process on this workstation presents the same pair, so three coordinators' fleets share ONE bucket of two in-flight requests. Verified here directly on the load-bearing half rather than taken on trust: the deployed wrapper carries zero occurrences of that header, with a positive control confirming the instrument finds the many other variables it does set. The fix is committed in the generator and was never regenerated into the deployed artifact, which belongs to another repository and another live session. THE CONSEQUENCE FOR THIS SECTION: the pre-dispatch probe reads the endpoints document and reports serving, and serving is necessary but NOT SUFFICIENT. A lane can list an endpoint, pass every health check this plan proposes, and admit two requests for the entire workstation. So the third measure as written - probe the document, report unknown when unreadable - would have passed throughout this episode while three coordinators queued behind each other. What it needs beside it is the admitted-concurrency figure the lane will actually serve, which is a property of the router rather than of the endpoint, and which nothing currently reads. Recorded also because it corrects a belief held across several sessions here that fleet width is bound by the dependency graph: today it was bound at two by a header fallback.
CORRECTION of consequence, from the session that owns the publisher: today's catalog disagreements were not three or four independent lane events. They share one mechanism. The endpoint registry's liveness check was stubbed to always true - read_registrations was called with job_is_running as a lambda returning True, while the real running-jobs lookup was imported in the same function and used only for routing origins - so a registration outlived its job indefinitely. A cancelled serve left its registration behind, the replacement took the port it released, both registrations resolved to one origin, both probes succeeded against the one live serve, and a duplicate check raised. Because that raise escaped the loop it abandoned the WHOLE document, publishing no endpoints for any model while a healthy serve answered. Both defects are now fixed: liveness is asked of the scheduler, and a duplicate resolves to the later job with a warning rather than costing unrelated models their entries. THIS IS THIS PLAN'S SECTION 2 IN ANOTHER SYSTEM. That section's measure is that liveness is derived from the process table on every read and never remembered, because a record that outlives its subject makes a guard report a state that is not there. The same defect, independently written, in a different repository on the same workstation, and it produced the same class of failure - a surface asserting with confidence something the machine contradicted. Worth carrying beyond either plan: an always-true liveness stub is not a corner case, it is the default shape a liveness check takes when nobody has yet had a reason to implement it, and it survives review because every reading it produces looks plausible. The scheduler-query fix also carries this plan's own absence rule explicitly: an empty query result is treated as UNKNOWN rather than as every job being dead, because the opposite would publish an empty document over a working lane. WHAT IS NOT FIXED, and the publisher's owner says so rather than closing it badly: a reader still cannot distinguish 'not yet published' from 'gone', because both render as an empty list. That is this plan's discriminating-value obligation sitting one system over, and it needs a published-at stamp or an explicit state to separate them.
CORRECTION TO THIS COORDINATOR'S OWN DESIGN, caught by the lane's owner before it landed rather than after. The carve-out argued here and adopted by two other sessions was that a fence may gate on observation and never on extrapolation, and that this is safe because it can only fire on evidence. The reasoning has a hole: it holds only if the observation is DURABLE, and half of the one implemented is not. The published binding flag is true when either preemptions have occurred or requests are waiting, and those are not alike. Preemptions are cumulative and monotonic and cannot be a sampling artefact. Waiting is instantaneous and routinely non-zero for a single poll under ordinary scheduling - measured on this lane the flag went true at waiting 2 with preemptions 0 and the next sample read waiting 1, and a separate event read waiting 4 against running 2. Nothing was saturated in either. So a gate on the field as built fires on the instantaneous half, which is the admission filter's false-refusal problem in a new costume: the precise failure the carve-out was written to prevent, reintroduced by the carve-out itself. Three things worth keeping. The remedy is producer-side - the refresher polls on a cadence and holds state between polls, so it counts consecutive waiting samples itself rather than three readers inventing three sustain rules, which is the same argument that made the flag producer-side to begin with and is now demonstrated twice in one afternoon. Until it lands the gate condition is preemptions alone, the signal with no artefact in it. And the general rule the episode yields is sharper than the carve-out it corrects: AN OBSERVATION IS ONLY EVIDENCE IF IT CANNOT BE A SAMPLING ARTEFACT, so a fence must name which of its inputs are cumulative and refuse to fire on the instantaneous ones. This plan's own reader is unaffected - the dispatched pre-flight node reports the flag and builds no gate - which is accidental rather than foresight and is why the gating decision belonging to a different surface than the reading was the right split.

A live instance of this section's subject arriving from the opposite direction, measured on two runs across two sessions. Both workers correctly had nothing to commit - their write path lies outside every git repository - and both said so in the manifest's commits field in prose. The reader split that prose on commas and returned it as a list of commits. One run's manifest_commits reads ['none - the assigned write path is outside every git repository (git rev-parse --show-toplevel refuses in ~/.config/reckon/crew/reports; its .git entry is an empty directory)', 'so there is nothing to commit to; the durable write is the report file itself'] - two entries, neither a sha, the second a sentence fragment beginning 'so'. The peer's run carries one entry only because its sentence happened to use a semicolon. This section's third measure holds that a terminal phase carrying a PARSED COMMIT beside an unusable status is the cheapest detector; the inverse is now measured, a parsed commit that is prose, and it defeats the same detector by satisfying it. A promotion resolving these against the integration branch would find nothing and record non-reachability for a run that correctly produced no commit, which is a false negative manufactured by the reader rather than reported by the worker. The remedy is this plan's standing rule: a reader that cannot parse a field refuses and names what it expected, rather than returning a shape that type-checks. A commits field whose entries are not commit-shaped is exactly the half-success the second measure forbids.
