THIS SECTION'S DEFECT REPRODUCED IN THIS PLAN'S OWN FLEET, and the instance REFUTES this section's third measure rather than confirming it. A node that finished cleanly was classified abandoned with the detail 'the process is gone without a complete manifest'. Every fact contradicted that: the manifest existed at 5,805 bytes, the worktree was clean, two commits sat beyond base carrying 310 insertions across exactly the six declared paths, the gate recorded 84 passed at base and 90 after, and the terminal stream record read stop_reason end_turn. The manifest's status line reads 'status: complete' on a line of its own. Parsed, it returns 'complete, `9f3c0f4` feat(flight): read a declared lane document beside the serving verdict, `c54a82d` test(flight): cover lane document declaration, staleness and bounds' - the reader continued the value across later lines containing a colon-space, which is this section's documented trigger. THE NEW FACT IS WHERE THOSE LINES CAME FROM. They are the commit lines, so the run-on did not merely corrupt the status, it CONSUMED THE COMMITS FIELD, which parsed to empty. This section's third measure holds that a terminal phase carrying a parsed commit beside an unusable status is the cheapest detector and needs no new instrument. That detector CANNOT FIRE HERE: there is no parsed commit to sit beside the unusable status, because the same run-on ate it. The measure is not wrong, it is incomplete - it describes the variant where the absorbed lines are prose and misses the variant where they are the very field the detector reads. A detector keyed on two fields disagreeing fails when one defect takes both. The stronger form, and the one to implement: a run whose worktree carries commits beyond its base is never classified abandoned, because that fact lives in git rather than in the manifest the parser mangled. Recorded before the fix so the refutation is not lost in the repair.
SECOND RUN REPORTED DEAD WHILE FINISHED, ninety minutes after the first, and it is a DIFFERENT defect - recorded carefully because this coordinator nearly filed it as another run-on before reading the file. The run finished with stop_reason end_turn, subtype success and terminal_reason completed, left a clean worktree, committed 8 files and 427 insertions at 0aa6681, and wrote a 3,973-byte manifest. It classified abandoned, 'the process is gone without a complete manifest'. The manifest is complete. It is written in MARKDOWN HEADING FORM - a '## status' heading with 'complete' beneath it, a '## commits' heading with the sha and subject as a list item, '## changed_paths' with per-file modification markers - and its orientation fields are list items prefixed with a dash rather than bare lines. Every required field is present and correct in substance; not one is in the colon-line syntax the reader requires, so the parse returns status None and commits empty and the classifier reads the absence as a dead worker. THE WORKER DID NOTHING WRONG. It wrote a more readable manifest than the format demands, and nothing in its contract told it the format was strict rather than conventional. This is the second measure of this section - a reader refuses rather than half-succeeds - failing in its purest form: the reader did not raise naming the path and the format it expected, it returned a partial mapping with the two fields that matter missing, which is indistinguishable from a worker that omitted them. TWO INSTANCES, TWO MECHANISMS, ONE VERDICT. The first had the fields present and the value corrupted by a run-on that ate the commits; this has the fields present in the document and absent from the parse. Neither is caught by the two-fields-disagreeing detector, because in one a single defect took both fields and in the other neither field arrived. The remedy proposed against the first instance covers both and should be implemented as the primary rule rather than the fallback: A RUN WHOSE WORKTREE CARRIES COMMITS BEYOND ITS BASE IS NEVER CLASSIFIED ABANDONED, because that fact lives in git and cannot be destroyed by any manifest format. Both runs would have been correctly classified by it without the parser being touched at all.
ROOT CAUSE for a run reported dead while alive, from a peer fleet's monitor pane and measured here. The pane shows one node going working to abandoned at 21:11:04 and abandoned to working at 21:30:27 - the same node, flapping, so the verdict was never true. Two compounding causes, both counted rather than inferred. FIRST, the declared-wait facility exists and nothing reaches it: recovery.py references wait_condition, wait_probe, wait_terminal, wait_started_at and resume_brief across 24 sites, fully built, and the number of manifests that have ever used it across every project and all of time is FIVE, against nineteen parks on one project in one day. A worker submits a detached scheduler job, its process exits, nothing declares the wait, and the classifier says 'the process is gone without a complete manifest' - a true sentence about a live run. SECOND, the non-terminal vocabulary is one word. NON_TERMINAL_MANIFEST_STATUSES is exactly in-progress, while ten live manifests on that project carry five spellings: pending, in-progress, in_progress, running, and one that parses to nothing. Four of five are unrecognised, so the status drops to none and the classifier is left with process liveness alone, which for a parked worker means dead. The two compose: an unusable status plus an undeclared wait leaves nothing but a dead process, and the display reports the only thing it can see. A facility that exists and is never reached is not a facility, which is the rule-with-no-check pattern in a third form - here the rule is reachable, documented, and the template does not make it the path of least resistance.
The sharpest statement of this section's subject, contributed by the lane's owner and arriving from the opposite failure direction to every instance recorded here so far. PROCESS_ALIVE FALSE COVERS FOUR STATES AND NAMES NONE OF THEM: a worker with no live process may have finished, parked deliberately, committed and then died, or genuinely failed. Every reader is left to guess, and both guesses have now been measured. This session's instances are owners failing to wake finished or parked runs. Theirs is the inverse: a coordinator read a node as a casualty - blocked, no live process, dispatched eight hours earlier - and messaged its owner to triage it. The owner corrected them: it had been resumed fourteen times with fourteen commits, was parked on a scheduler job they were actively watching, and the eight-hour stamp was its DISPATCH time rather than when it last worked. No live process is the correct and expected state for a worker that submits a job and ends its turn by design. Same missing field, opposite failures, which is stronger evidence than either alone - it is not a monitoring gap on one side, the state is genuinely unreadable from outside. Their census is worth keeping verbatim in effect: across one day, every run classified abandoned had either committed cleanly or produced nothing, so the classification has never once meant what its name suggests. AND THE DECLARED WAIT MUST BE PROBED, NOT MERELY DECLARED. A field a run asserts and nothing checks is a value nobody can audit after the fact, and would let a run claim it is parked while its job is long gone - the same defect as an admission ceiling that lived only in a launch invocation, or a lane figure published with no observation time. The follower probing the declared job id itself is what turns the declaration into an observation, and it is the load-bearing half rather than the convenient half.

abandoned now means the worker died and nothing else: a run whose process exited while its manifest declares a wait classifies parked with its resume path, a non-terminal status in any spelling workers actually write classifies working with the vocabulary in one frozenset, a worktree carrying commits beyond base is never abandoned because that fact lives in git, and the negative holds so no manifest plus no wait plus no commits still abandons; gate 85 passed at base to 94 after, zero added failures, cross-checks 19 passed

AN ADMISSION ABOUT THIS SESSION'S OWN LANDED FIX, recorded because the next reader will otherwise trust it further than it deserves. The spelling vocabulary added at e662009 holds four words - in-progress, in_progress, running, pending - chosen from what workers had actually written. A peer's fourth instance, reported hours later, carries the status 'reported', which is a fifth. There will be a sixth. A VOCABULARY IS A PATCH ON AN OPEN SET: it converts the cases already seen and cannot convert the case not yet written, because the set it enumerates is authored by workers rather than by the schema. The two rules landing beside it are the durable half and neither enumerates anything - a worktree carrying commits beyond its base is never abandoned, which reads a fact in git, and a reader that refuses rather than half-succeeding, which is dispatched. Those hold for a word nobody has invented yet. Stated plainly: the part of that node most likely to be cited later is the part that will age worst. THE GENERALISATION IS A PEER'S AND IT IS THE BEST SENTENCE THIS SPRINT PRODUCED. Three variants of one shape recurred across every session: a check that cannot fire, a gate never shown to refuse, and a state that exists and cannot be reached. ALL THREE LOOK IDENTICAL FROM THE OUTSIDE TO A STATE THAT SIMPLY NEVER OCCURS. The unreadable classification is the third variant exactly - built, given its own recovery action, and unreachable for the only shape that needed it, because it sits behind an except clause and the parser it depends on does not raise. That is why six finished runs across three sessions read as dead while a state named for their condition sat in the vocabulary unused. Note also what could not be verified: the peer's fourth pointer was gone by the time this was checked, promoted or discarded, so whether the committed-work rule caught it is unknown rather than confirmed. Their offered re-run against the merged fix, from trees this session did not author, is what settles it.

the reader refuses rather than half-succeeding: a non-blank manifest body yielding no key-value field raises a named error carrying the path and the expected format, instead of normalising to eleven keys with no status that the classifier reads as a vanished worker; the classifier routes it to unreadable with a next action naming the manifest file, never abandoned naming the launch log; the audit call site tolerates the raise as a finding rather than an exception; the negative holds so a genuinely absent manifest still abandons; base and after carry the identical single environmental failure id, zero added

THE MERGED FIX WAS ONE SHAPE TOO NARROW, found by a peer re-running four of their own manifests against it from trees this session did not author - the verification that was asked for and the reason to ask. Two of four are fixed and raise as intended. One returns a fifth spelling. AND ONE IS A GENUINE MISS, reproduced here before acting: the predicate fires only when ZERO fields parse, and that manifest parses THIRTEEN with no status among them, so the body is neither empty nor unreadable and the reader half-succeeds exactly as before. What makes it parse at all is incidental prose - three narrative lines beginning Node, Worktree and Plan under review happen to be field-shaped, so a document with no field at all still yields a field set. That is the run whose misclassification nearly cost its owner two and a half hours. THEIR CONDITION IS THE RIGHT ONE AND IT SUBSUMES THIS SESSION'S VOCABULARY PATCH: the condition is not 'no field could be read', it is STATUS COULD NOT BE DETERMINED. Zero fields parsed is one way to reach that; fields parsed with no status key is the other, and the second is the one that produced the near-loss. Their further point is the elegant half - an unrecognised status WORD treated as undetermined rather than as a state converts the open set from something a maintainer must enumerate into something the reader refuses, which retires the four-word vocabulary added earlier today as the durable answer and leaves it only as a convenience. One hazard noted and deliberately not chased: the third prose line contains the word status inside a sentence about the plan under review, exactly the sort of thing a looser pattern would latch onto. The fix is the absent KEY, never a search for the word. Dispatched as status-undetermined-is-the-condition with the refusal naming both the word it found and the words it recognises, so a worker reading the error can act on it.

two of three behaviours landed, merged and verified: a zero-field body refuses, and a status word outside the recognised sets refuses naming the word it found. The headline measure is unmet - a body from which fields parse but no status key is among them still returns status None, verified against the peer's actual manifest which parses to 13 keys with status None after the merge. That is the run whose misclassification nearly cost its owner two and a half hours. The merge is kept because the landed behaviours are correct and add no failures; a corrective node follows. This run is also the first to follow the new landing contract, and it exposed that dispatch does not grant the paths the contract instructs a worker to write

the missed shape is closed: a body whose fields parse but carry no status key is refused, naming which fields it did read. Verified against the peer's actual manifest, which now refuses where it previously returned 13 keys with status None, and against all five other shapes: zero-field still refuses, an unrecognised word still refuses naming the word, well-formed terminal and non-terminal still parse, and a recovery artifact declared through the derived field is exempt so the recovery pipeline can still read its own file

A REGRESSION THIS SESSION INTRODUCED, caught by a peer's live observation rather than by any check here, and the detail they added is the sharpest thing in it. The unrecognised-status refusal merged an hour earlier fires on values the RUN-ON has corrupted, so a live worker reads unreadable while its process is plainly alive. Measured across every project on this workstation: 5 live pointers carry a manifest, 2 refused, and both are run-on corruptions - one an in-progress, and one a DECLARED WAITING STATUS, so a correctly parked run was refused by the strictness added hours after the wait facility was made reachable. The peer's framing is exact and worse than a cosmetic misread: abandoned was wrong about finished work and fired once per run; this is wrong about work in progress and fires continuously for its whole duration. THE DETAIL THAT REFRAMES THE DEFECT: the text the run-on absorbed into their status was the worker's restated grep reporting 5 where an earlier vacuous form reported 0 - the node doing exactly what it was asked, recording real evidence. A manifest line carrying evidence is what corrupts the status, so the failing shape is not rare or degenerate, IT IS WHAT A THOROUGH WORKER PRODUCES. The more a worker records, the likelier its status is unreadable, which is a perverse gradient and an argument for fixing the run-on at the parser rather than tolerating it downstream. The remedy dispatched is theirs: liveness outranks unreadability, because the evidence separating undetermined-at-rest from ordinary-mid-flight is not in the manifest at all - it is a live process with a fresh log, the same discriminator as the commits rule on the other axis. When the manifest cannot be trusted, read the machine. AND A POINT ABOUT THE TEST, NOT THE CODE, worth carrying to every state in this vocabulary: the assertion that unreadable is REACHABLE for a dead process belongs in the test NAME, not only in a plan comment, because the next reader of that file sees the name and not this record. The original defect was a state built with its own action and unreachable precisely because nothing asserted it could be reached.

liveness outranks unreadability: a worker whose process is alive is never reported unreadable, so strictness added for manifests at rest cannot misreport work in progress. Verified against the live fleet rather than a fixture - runs classified unreadable went from 2 to 0, and 0 of 3 live processes now read unreadable. The state stays reachable for a dead process with an unjudgeable manifest, asserted positively and named in the test so the next reader of the file sees it. Clears a regression this session introduced between two landings, found by a peer's live observation rather than by any check here

FOUR LINE FORMS DEFEAT THE MANIFEST READER, AND A TERMINAL STREAM EVENT HIDES HOW OFTEN IT HAPPENS. Third spelling reported by a peer coordinator against its own fleet; reproduced here, and a fourth found in the same pass:

status: complete          -> 'complete'      control
- status: complete        -> REFUSED         list prefix
- status: done            -> REFUSED         list prefix (the reported case)
**status:** `complete`    -> REFUSED         emphasis
  status: complete        -> REFUSED         INDENTATION  <- not previously reported
status: done              -> REFUSED         word outside the vocabulary

The accepted vocabulary is blocked, complete, derived, failed, in-progress, in_progress, pending, running, waiting. So done fails on the word and the other four fail on the line shape, which is a different defect wearing the same refusal.

The reader is behaving correctly in every one of these and that is not the problem. It refuses rather than half-succeeding, which is what this section landed. The problem is that it refuses on shapes workers actually emit — a markdown list, a bolded key, an indented block — and a manifest is a document a worker writes by hand under a prose contract, so these are the natural failure modes rather than exotic ones.

The masking is the finding, and it means the observed rate understates the real one. Measured on the peer's run, where every field carries a - prefix:

classification: running      phase: complete
manifest_status: None        manifest_error: None

Three surfaces disagreeing, and the discriminating value is dropped: the reader produced a precise refusal and crew list reports manifest_error: None. The phase reads complete because it comes from the stream's terminal event, so nothing announces that the manifest is unusable. Where the stream carries no terminal event — a worker killed at a ceiling, a process that simply ends — the same run classifies as running forever. So the stream is currently concealing this class, and every count of how often it happens is a lower bound.

This is the second independent sighting of the dropped error. The same manifest_error: None against a precise reader refusal was measured earlier today on this session's own unreadable run. Two sessions, two runs, same silent drop — so it is a property of the surface rather than a quirk of either case.

Two repairs, and the peer is right that the second half is theirs rather than the reader's. Strip list prefixes, emphasis and leading indentation before the split, so the reader is robust to what workers emit; and state the exact plain line form in the worker brief, which the template repair landed this session did for the fields and not for the line shape. Whether done should join the terminal vocabulary is a separate question and is not bundled here.

RETRACTION: THE CLAIM THAT THE SURFACE DROPS THE MANIFEST ERROR IS WITHDRAWN. IT WAS ASSERTED TWICE AND WAS NOT MEASURED. The comment above records manifest_error: None against a precise reader refusal as a second independent sighting of a surface discarding its discriminating value. That reading was taken from a crew list row, and the check that would have falsified it was never run.

rows in the listing: 13
rows with manifest_file_present populated: 0

Every manifest field is absent on every row, not merely the error, and not merely on the malformed run. So the view carries no manifest detail at all, and a uniform absence was read as a specific silence. The capture itself is correct: recovery.py:1173 sets manifest_error = str(exc) on a parse failure and :1845 exports it. Nothing was shown to drop anything.

This is the plan's own rule about absence, failed by the plan's own coordinator. An absence claim needs a positive control — one command asking whether any row carries the field — and it was skipped because the absence was expected and the expectation was the thing under test. It was then passed to a peer as confirmed corroboration of their finding, which is the worse half: an unverified reading travelling under two sessions' names carries authority neither of them earned. The peer's own framing from earlier today applies exactly — an under-specified measurement quoted by someone else is worse than no measurement.

What survives is unaffected and was measured directly. Six line forms defeat the reader, reproduced here case by case. The stream masks the consequence, because phase comes from a terminal event and a cleanly-exiting run looks healthy whatever its manifest says — measured on a real run showing classification running beside phase complete. The dispatched repair targets the reader and is not touched by this retraction.

What is now unknown: whether any surface a coordinator actually reads reports a manifest parse failure. That is a real question and it is open, rather than answered in the negative as stated above. It wants its own measurement against a surface that claims to carry the field, not against one that carries no manifest detail at all.

THE DISPATCHED READER REPAIR IS FENCED TO THE PARSE AND THE DEFECT A COORDINATOR SEES LIVES ONE LAYER PAST IT. Raised by a peer against this session's own node, and correct. Its done-when asserts what parse_manifest returns for six line forms. Nothing in it reaches the classification that consumes the parse, so a green node would leave the pane unchanged.

Measured on a peer's finished run, which is why this is not theoretical:

process_alive              False
phase                      complete      (from the stream)
classification             running       (the classifier)
manifest_reported_status   None          (the reader)
follower                   stalled — "investigate: stream quiet for 902s"
the work                   committed, merged, pushed, verified

A dead process classified running, and a pane directing a human to investigate a run that is finished. Four surfaces disagree about one run and the deciding input is a single markdown dash. Every earlier instance in this class was a record that read wrong; this one is a display telling someone to act, where acting is wasted.

This is the entry-point rule failing on the node written to honour it. A capability lands in the module owning the mechanism while the call site sits outside the fence, and a green suite covers a dead feature. The reader repair is necessary and is not sufficient: with the manifest readable the classifier would presumably see a terminal status against a dead process and say complete — and presumably is the word that marks an inference nobody measured. The assertion that fences what a coordinator actually sees is a dead process plus a terminal-but-decorated manifest classifies complete rather than stalled, and it cannot be written in the parse-level test file.

Disposition: a sibling node against the classifier, sequenced after the reader lands — not a widening of the node in flight, whose scope is fenced and whose worker is mid-run. Sequenced rather than parallel because the assertion only passes once the parse is fixed, and a test that fails on main to express an intention is a worse artefact than a node that waits.

The observation worth carrying past this instance: three coordinators spent this morning establishing that a starved worker and a stalled one are indistinguishable from outside. A finished one is indistinguishable from stalled too, for an unrelated cause, on the same pane. The pane's stalled verdict is not one defect — it is the place three different conditions arrive wearing the same word.

THE READER REPAIR MERGED WITH A GREEN GATE AND THE FORM IT WAS DISPATCHED FOR STILL FAILS. Measured at the merged head:

- status: complete          -> 'complete'
* status: complete          -> 'complete'
  status: complete          -> 'complete'
**status:** `complete`      -> 'complete'
- **status:** complete      -> 'complete'    colon INSIDE the emphasis
- **status**: complete      -> REFUSED       colon OUTSIDE
- **status**: done          -> REFUSED       <- the instance measured in the wild

Two composed spellings exist and they are different strings. The worker satisfied **status:**; the peer's observed manifest carries **status**:. Five of seven forms now read and the one that motivated the node does not.

The cause is the done-when, and it is the coordinator's. It said a composed dash-plus-emphasis prefix — a shape category — where the reporter had supplied a literal string. A category admits a member that is not the observed one, and the worker chose a reasonable member of it. The falsifier was satisfiable without covering the case, which is the same defect as a gate whose evidence does not bear on its verdict, moved into the contract that commissions the gate. The follow-on lists all eight lines as exact literals rather than describing them, because a literal cannot be satisfied by an adjacent case.

The rule, stated so it is checkable at authoring: when a reporter supplies the exact string they observed, the done-when carries that string, not a description of its shape. A description is the right form for a property that ranges over a domain; it is the wrong form for a reproduction, because the reproduction is the evidence and paraphrasing it discards the only part that was measured.

And it is the concrete cost of a principle this coordinator stated an hour earlier and did not enforce: that the fix should find the key through decoration generally rather than enumerating known constructs, because the enumeration keeps growing. It grew by one before the first enumeration finished merging. Saying the right thing in a message to a peer and writing the weaker thing into the contract is how a principle fails to reach the work — the same gap between a stated rule and a running check that this plan records in five other places.

What landed is kept rather than reverted. Five forms that refused this morning now read, including the plain list prefix behind the originally reported instance. The repair is incomplete, not wrong.

ATTRIBUTION CORRECTION, AND THE DECISION THIS SESSION DELIBERATELY EXCLUDED IS NOW THE ONLY THING BLOCKING A REAL RUN.

First, the correction. The comment above records - **status**: done as the instance measured in the wild by the coordinator who reported it. That is wrong: the composed-with-emphasis form is nova's, from dvf-target-current-digest-probe-at-main. The reporting coordinator's own form is - status: done — a plain list prefix, no emphasis. The attributions were transposed between their three-row table and this record, and the consequence is not cosmetic: it changes whose case the repair covers. Measured at the merged head:

- status: done        -> REFUSED "status 'done' is not a recognised manifest status
                         (recognised: blocked, complete, derived, failed,
                          in-progress, in_progress, pending, running, waiting)"
- **status**: done    -> REFUSED "carries no status key"

So the repair did cover the reported form. The prefix is handled, the key is found, and the refusal moved from reporting an empty value to naming the offending word and listing the vocabulary — which is the improvement this section asks for. The two forms still failing are nova's, not the reporter's, so the enumeration failed on a case neither party had in hand rather than on the reported one. That is a materially smaller charge against the enumeration than the one recorded above, and the recorded one overstated it.

Second, and this is now the live question. With the prefix handled, the reporter's run is held entirely on the word: a worker chose done as its terminal status and done is not in the manifest vocabulary. This session deliberately declined to bundle that question, on the reasoning that mixing it into a parser repair would let a real fix carry a judgement nobody had made. That reasoning stands and the situation has changed: it is no longer hypothetical — one run on main is blocked on it, and a worker unprompted chose the word, which is evidence about what workers write when the brief does not say.

The recommendation, and it argues against widening. §4 of this plan already carries the measure one condition has one name. Admitting done as a second spelling of complete violates it directly, and the vocabulary is deliberately narrow for the same reason the status set refuses an unrecognised word rather than carrying it forward as state. The alternative costs nothing and is already half-built: the brief states the exact word, and the refusal — which now lists the recognised set — teaches it at the point of failure. Note that done is terminal vocabulary for plans, where COMPLETED_STATUSES is {shipped, done}, so a worker borrowing it is making a reasonable cross-surface inference; that is an argument for stating the manifest's word in the brief, not for merging the two vocabularies.

Left open rather than decided, because it is lead-shaped: whether a worker may write done. The reporter has no view and neither does this coordinator beyond the measure above, and the half that is theirs they have already named — their brief did not state the exact plain form, which is why the worker invented both a word and a prefix outside the parser.

The liveness guard is repeated per branch, and one branch does not carry it. recovery.py:1459 against its two siblings:

elif manifest_error and manifest_present and alive is not True:   # guarded
elif wait is not None:                                            # NOT guarded
elif budget_hold and alive is not True:                           # guarded

So a live, working run whose manifest declares a malformed external wait is classified unreadable, with "repair the waiting declaration before resuming" as its next action. Reported from another session against a run reading process_alive: True and log_age_seconds: 6, and the citation verified here in the tree.

tests/test_crew_recovery.py:519 already asserts that a live process outranks every terminal manifest status. The rule is written, the test exists, and it does not range over this door — the same falsifier defect recorded elsewhere on this plan, where the cases enumerated are the ones already found rather than the input domain.

The trigger is common rather than exotic. A worker resumed after a mid-turn kill writes a checkpoint manifest setting status: waiting whose wait_condition reads "no wait — process was killed mid-turn by a deliberate server restart". The declaration is genuinely self-contradictory and the classifier is right to reject it; the run is healthy and will overwrite the file when it finishes. Every worker resumed after the restart at ~11:26Z is a candidate, and seven of those are mine.

A second shape on the guarded arm. One review run here went unreadable sixty seconds in while alive, and cleared itself — on re-observe the manifest parses, manifest_error is None, alive is True. That arm does carry the guard, so the file was read mid-write. Transient and cosmetic, but it widens the question: the falsifier belongs over every classification reachable with a live process, not over the one uncovered elif.

The repair is structural, not another guard. Evaluate liveness once, above the manifest-derived classification, rather than inside each arm — a guard repeated per branch is one the next branch will miss, which is the measured history here rather than a prediction.

Queued, not dispatched: reckon/crew/recovery.py and tests/test_crew_recovery.py are exclusively claimed by the live run a-finished-run-is-not-stalled-retry. The reporting session is holding its run alive as a reproduction for the repair brief to confirm against.

Correction to the comment above: the "second shape on the guarded arm" does not exist. It was the same unguarded wait branch, read through a surface that does not carry the field.

I reported a review run going unreadable while alive and then clearing itself on re-observe. reckon crew observe returns no classification key at all, and I read the resulting None as the condition lifting. From crew list, which does carry it: classification unreadable, process_alive True, phase working, log_age_s 1, next action "repair the waiting declaration".

So the measured evidence is two instances of recovery.py:1459 in two sessions, and zero instances of a guarded arm misfiring on a mid-write read. The wider falsifier — range over every classification reachable with a live process — remains the better shape, but it now rests on the argument that a per-branch guard is one the next branch misses, not on a second observed failure. A node scoped to the single branch loses no measurement.

Left as a correction rather than an edit to the paragraph above, because a plan about surfaces that cannot lie is the wrong place to silently repair having been misled by one. It is also the second instance today of the same error — reading a null from a surface that was never asked the question, the first being the session_id field. Both times the raw or wrong surface stood in for the one carrying the answer, and both times the claim was confident.

Measured rate, which settles the transient question against my reading: unreadable occurs only on live processes, it affects 20% of them, and it persists. Cross-tabulated by a peer session across reckon's 26 live pointers at 12:42:12Z:

processnclassifications
alive15running 11, unreadable 3, waiting 1
dead11scoring 7, promotable 2, abandoned 2

Not one dead run carries it. The same reading 28 minutes earlier returned the identical three run ids, all alive, all working or starting, with process ages of 35 to 55 minutes. They belong to three different coordinators across three plans.

So the self-clearing case recorded above is real but is the minority. The steady state is a live, working run sitting mislabelled for the better part of an hour while the display advises "repair the waiting declaration before resuming" — which is the cost profile of a coordinator misled into acting, on the branch at recovery.py:1458 rather than the mid-write one.

It closes the loop with the template defect. Four of five resume-ready candidates in a separate sweep reported missing or invalid wait_probe — the same population. The template gives a resumed worker no correct slot, the declaration comes out invalid, and this branch then calls the working run unreadable. Repairing the template stops new invalid declarations; it does nothing for the ones already in flight, so both halves are needed.

The assertion this yields is an invariant rather than a value: a run whose process is alive is never classified unreadable. No timing in it, nothing to re-read, and it fails today against three live runs. That is the measure, and it is stronger than anything keyed to a single observation — which is what my own earlier claim and retraction were both built on.

the decoration reader accepts emphasis before the field colon; review scored 100 with no findings

The node dispatched to restore the liveness invariant is, while alive and generating, reported as waiting, escalated to an alarm, and promised a recovery that cannot happen. Three false statements about one healthy worker, from three different surfaces, at the same instant.

what the fleet sayswhat is true
classification waiting, then wait-agedprocess_alive True, log_age 1s, 999s elapsed
! investigate: waiting 901s on awaiting orientation755 KB of stream, last written two seconds before the reading
next_action: the recovery sweep will resume this runthe sweep would skip it — this plan has seven claimants on its HTML, so the collision check refuses (§15); and it needs no resuming

Each layer is individually defensible. The worker wrote status: waiting in an orientation checkpoint, because the template offers no other checkpoint shape (§12). The classifier believed the manifest over the running process, because liveness is tested at one branch rather than above the chain — the condition this very node exists to repair. The ageing rule escalated a wait that was never a wait. The next-action named the sweep without consulting whether the sweep would act.

No single component is wrong and the composite statement is false in every part. That is the plan's thesis arriving on the plan's own work, and it is the clearest instance of it recorded here: a reader who trusted any one of those three surfaces would intervene in a run that is doing exactly what it was asked to do.

No intervention taken. The worker is healthy and the correct action is to let it finish — which is itself the point, because every affordance the display offered was an intervention.

43% of live workers carry a not-running classification, and every one of them was generating output within two seconds of the reading. Measured project-wide at 13:18:35Z, which turns the single instances recorded above into a rate.

One correction to the earlier reading. The 12:42Z cross-tabulation found unreadable on no dead process and concluded it occurs only on live ones. At 13:18Z a dead process carries it. So the observation "unreadable implies alive" is falsified and should not be built on; the invariant the repair needs — a live process is never unreadable — is untouched, still fails on three live runs, and remains the right assertion. Worth separating because the two sentences look interchangeable and only one of them is a property of the code.

At this rate a coordinator reading the pane sees roughly two in five of its working fleet described as not working. That is the cost this plan exists to remove, and it is now measured rather than argued.

the classifier already treated a dead process with a terminal manifest as finished whatever decorates the status; pinned with a test rather than changed. Delta-judged pass on a red base: 1 failed / 91 passed at head against 1 failed / 90 passed at base, same failure id both sides, zero added. Review scored 100 with no findings. recovery.py declared but untouched, and released from the live claim

the decoration reader finds a manifest key through markdown emphasis and bullets; review scored 93. Two findings recorded as follow-ons, the first a measured behaviour change: an indented continuation whose leading word is manifest vocabulary is now a top-level field (at base 3833e84c^ 'evidence_inputs: prose / - blockers: x' yields evidence_inputs with both lines and blockers empty; at head it yields a separate blockers field). blockers is read at promotion, so this is semantic. The falsifier that would have caught it is a corpus check: every manifest on disk parses identically before and after

liveness is evaluated once above the classification chain as a single process_gone verdict, making true the invariant recovery.py had asserted in a comment since it was written: a live process is never classified unreadable. Delta-judged pass on a red base - 261 passed / 1 failed after against 252 / 1 at base, identical failure id, +9 passing and zero added. LANDING RECORD — RECOVERY CLASSIFIES A FINISHED RUN IDENTICALLY WHATEVER DECORATES ITS MANIFEST STATUS, AND NOW HAS THE GUARD TEST THAT CONTRACT WAS MISSING. Reproduced first, per the brief: the motivating defect (a dead process holding a decorated terminal manifest read as running or stalled) is already retired by the reader-level repair this section landed, and the recovery classifier already classifies every neighbour correctly at the worktree head, so no guard was reimplemented and reckon/crew/recovery.py is untouched. The dead process that reads as complete classifies into the terminal family — scoring, snapshot state completed_unpromoted — identically for status: complete and - **status**: complete, field for field across classification, recovery, manifest status and next action. A dead process with an unreadable manifest keeps its own unreadable outcome and is never complete; an absent manifest is abandoned; a live process with a terminal manifest is still running/working with its status deferred, not terminal while it runs. The missing piece was the classifier-level pin, added as test_a_dead_process_with_a_terminal_manifest_is_finished_whatever_decorates_status: it builds a decorated and a plain pointer for the same run id and asserts they cannot diverge downstream while the three neighbour cases stay out of the terminal family. Coverage is honest — the two pointers hold distinct manifest files because a single shared path would have let the plain write overwrite the decorated file before either was read. Measure against base be162a17: base 90 passed / 1 failed, after 91 passed / 1 failed — zero added failures; the 1 is a pre-existing refusal-path promotion test in a file this node does not write, recorded under follow-ons.

review dimensions reach the committed ledger row: scores and total carried on the promoted row, an absent review distinguishable from a zero-scored one, dimensions queryable filtered on the parent run; 8 files all within the 10 declared paths, 427 insertions; gate base 169 passed / 4 failed to after 178 passed / 4 failed, 0 added failures, 9 tests added, the 4 pre-existing in a file the node declared unmodified; classified abandoned despite finishing cleanly because its manifest is written in markdown headings rather than colon lines, so every required field is present in the document and absent from the parse

the composed worker contract now requires a worker to append its landing record to its own plan section and its evidence anchor to the cumulative record, in its worktree and in its final commit, with a figure wherever a spatial plotted or sequential relationship is clearer shown than described and never an image of a table; the plan-version and plan-modified meta lines are forbidden to workers with the merge reason stated; role-scoped by a working-directory proxy and pinned by an explicit negative over review and investigate; 41 passed 0 failed on the fence, 75 on the wider prompt-composition gate

The worker contract landed at c4a8d1c and merged: a worker now appends its landing record to its own plan section and its evidence anchor to the cumulative evidence record, both in its worktree and both in its final commit, with a figure wherever a spatial, plotted or sequential relationship is clearer shown than described, under the figures directory convention with the project-absolute src form, and never an image of what is naturally a table. The plan-version and plan-modified meta lines are forbidden to workers with the reason stated in the contract text - every worker touching them makes every merge conflict there regardless of which sections they wrote. Gate 41 passed and 0 failed on the fence, 75 across the wider prompt-composition gate, 6 new tests, ruff delta 0. THE MEANS LANDED FIRST AND SEPARATELY, and that ordering was the finding: the figure mandate had been in the guidance all along while the plotting stack was absent from the project environment, so every figure in this repository to date was hand-authored SVG and this plan's own evidence record carried exactly one, eleven days old. A rule with no means is not a rule. ONE FRAGILITY WORTH RECORDING RATHER THAN ASSUMING AWAY. The clause is role-scoped by comparing the working directory against the worktree rather than by reading the role, which is a proxy for 'this node's deliverable is a repository change'. It currently holds and is pinned by an explicit negative asserting the clause is absent for both review and investigate. But the coupling is incidental: if a sandbox tier ever gives a read-only role its own worktree as working directory, the clause leaks to nodes that cannot act on it, and the test that pins it would still pass because it exercises the roles rather than the condition. Reading a proxy as the thing itself is this plan's most repeated finding, and here it sits inside the plan's own contract.
THE FIRST NODE DISPATCHED AFTER THE LANDING CONTRACT MERGED DID EXACTLY WHAT THE CONTRACT SAYS, AND ITS PROMOTION WAS REFUSED FOR DOING SO: 'changed paths outside its declared write scope: docs/evidence/archive/crew-observability-truth-landed.html, docs/plans/crew-observability-truth.html'. The contract instructs a write the fence does not permit, because dispatch does not add those two paths to a worker's scope and a coordinator naming them by hand was never part of the design. The refusal is correct at every step and the pair has no fixed point, which is the same shape recorded one section over this morning where the recovery surface emitted an action the dispatch validator refused - committed here by this coordinator, inside this plan's own contract, within hours of writing that finding down. Resolved for this run with the documented accept-path waiver carrying both reasons, and dispatched as its own node so the next worker is not refused for obeying. WHAT THE CONTRACT GOT RIGHT IS WORTH RECORDING BESIDE THE GAP, because this was its first live exercise. The worker authored a real evidence anchor with its own section, an anchors-table row, an updated summary and an updated plan-verifies list; and it did NOT touch plan-version or plan-modified, which is the single rule that makes concurrent worker plan edits mergeable rather than conflicting on two lines every time. It produced no figure, which on inspection is defensible - the content is a conditional predicate over four manifest shapes, and a table is the honest representation of that - but it is the first exercise of the figure clause and it yielded none, so the clause is watched rather than concluded. THE SCOPE FIX MUST EXEMPT THOSE PATHS FROM EXCLUSIVITY, not merely add them: two concurrent nodes on one plan both need them, and git merge rather than a claim registry is what resolves a shared append. Granting them exclusively would convert every second concurrent node into a scope conflict, which is the fence failing in the opposite direction.

the fence grants what the contract instructs: an implement role receives the plan and cumulative evidence paths in its effective write scope without the coordinator naming them, a read-only role does not, and both paths are exempt from the exclusive-claim refusal so two concurrent nodes on one plan hold them without conflict - git merge rather than a claim registry resolves a shared append. Closes the gap where the landing contract instructed a write the fence forbade, which refused the first node that obeyed it

the composed prompt no longer composes the landing clause against a blanket ban on editing plan state: an implement prompt once carried both “append your landing record to your own section of the plan and your evidence anchor to the cumulative evidence record” and worktree rule 4’s “Do not edit reckon plan or index state”, two instructions that cannot both hold after the fence granted those paths. Rule 4 is narrowed rather than deleted so its real intent survives - a worker still must not mutate the shared project index, sprint state, or a plan other than the one it is landing against - while the plan it is landing against is not also denied, asserted by composing an implement prompt and requiring the pair not to co-occur, the narrowed text to be present, and a read-only role to receive the narrowed prohibition and no landing clause. Composed-prompt gate 15 passed / 0 failed at base, 18 passed / 0 failed after, with the wider two-file gate carrying the identical pre-existing promotion-environmental failures (10 at base, 10 after), so the change adds tests and no failures.

QUEUED AND NOT YET DISPATCHED, recorded here so it survives this session. The landing contract tells a worker to append its record and says the files live in its worktree; it never says the plan MCP writes a DIFFERENT store. Grepped: edit_plan, MCP and main checkout appear zero times in reckon/crew/prompts.py. So a worker told to update plan state can reasonably reach for edit_plan, write the coordinator's checkout rather than its own tree, and land outside the merge model entirely - the coordinator merges from the worktree and the worker's edit is somewhere else, silently. Under the lead's ruling that the worker authors and the coordinator merges, this is the one wrong move available. It was found by a peer's worker, which announced the bypass explicitly in its manifest and cited the store distinction as its reason - better reasoning than either coordinator had at the time. THE CLAUSE TO ADD, with its reason and not only its prohibition: edit the plan and evidence files in this worktree; do not use the plan MCP, because it writes the coordinator's checkout and not your tree. SECOND CLAUSE FOR THE SAME NODE, requested by the same peer and agreed: a worker does not resolve its own driving followup and does not set a terminal status. Appending its landing record is its job; deciding the section closed is the coordinator's, because ONLY THE COORDINATOR SEES THE OTHER NODES. A worker knows its node landed, not whether the section did. Both clauses are prompt text in reckon/crew/prompts.py, which is why they are one node rather than two.

A THIRD INSTANCE OF THE CONTRACT-FENCE DISAGREEMENT, and this session's own repair makes it worse rather than better. Routed here by a peer coordinator who verified it in source rather than taking it on trust, after a second fleet hit it live. A test-role node is granted repository write access at dispatch and refused for using it at promotion: promotion.py:2405 raises when role_may_write_repository_paths is false and the cited commit changes any repository path, while nothing at dispatch consults that predicate. Confirmed here by reading both ends.

Why only this role can reach the state. review and investigate are read-only, so they cannot write the repository and the refusal is unreachable for them. test is worktree-full — correctly, since a verifier may need to run a build — so dispatch grants the access and promotion then refuses its use. The measured instance: a peer's worker did exactly what the landing-record rule asks, committed only its own declared docs/evidence and docs/plans paths, and was refused at complete; they finished with --no-commit and carried the evidence file across by hand. A second coordinator escaped only by accident, having given its verifier a report path under the config home because its deliverable happened to be a report, not because it knew the refusal existed.

THE COUPLING IS THE FINDING. The node in flight against this section moves the landing contract and its write-scope grant off the process-directory predicate and onto writability, because a read-only worker was being handed a contract its sandbox forbids. test is writable. So after that repair a test node receives the landing contract and the plan and evidence grant, and promotion refuses it — turning an accident two coordinators avoided into the default path for every verifier that obeys the rule. A repair that removes one instance of a defect while widening another is worth recording as such: the two ends were already disagreeing, and closing the gap at one pair made the other pair's gap load-bearing.

The repair, and why not the other one. Two candidates were offered: refuse repository write paths at dispatch, or accept a commit confined to the node's own declared paths under docs/evidence and docs/plans. The second, because the first makes the landing-record convention inapplicable to an entire role — and that convention is landing in prompts.py in this same session, so refusing at dispatch would put the prompt and the fence in direct conflict, which is the defect rather than a fix for it. One predicate read at both ends is the shape: dispatch grants what promotion will accept.

A smaller item for the worker template, same shape as two other findings today. A test role's manifest lists the commits of the window under test under commits:, so a first completion cited twenty-one implementation commits and the refusal named all fourteen changed paths. One field holding two quantities, with a name that does not say which — the template should say a verifier cites its own commits or none.

THE FOURTH INSTANCE OF THE CONTRACT-FENCE DISAGREEMENT, and the one that explains why this plan keeps finding unguarded records. Raised by the lead as a question — should a review or investigate worker not be able to create a research document? — and measured rather than reasoned about. It cannot. Every writable grant on the read-only tier lies outside the repository:

write path 'docs/research/gc-at-scale-prior-art.html' is unreachable in resolved
sandbox tier 'read-only'; writable grants:
  ~/.config/reckon/crew/reports
  ~/.config/reckon/crew/reviews
  ~/.config/reckon/crew/runs/<this run>
  /run/user/39486

The ratio is the finding. 638 files have accumulated in the crew reports directory, 40 of them reuse maps, against 15 tracked files in docs/research/. So substantially all investigative output this project has produced is machine-local, unversioned, outside git, invisible to any other checkout, and reapable by the same cleanup that clears run directories. Not because anyone chose that — because the only roles that produce investigation are fenced out of the only place the repository's own rules say such documents belong. The project's guidance is explicit that RCAs, incident reports and explainers are HTML under docs/; the tier makes that unreachable for the roles that write them.

This plan already named the defect one level up and did not see the cause. §21 records that reviewers raised an unguarded record that nothing reads against the reuse maps, and that the review store is six records, unversioned, machine-local. Both were read as storage choices to be corrected per-artifact. They are not choices at all: they are what the sandbox tier forces, and any future scout or reviewer will reproduce them exactly. A finding that recurs in every instance of a class is a property of the mechanism, not of the instances.

Why the obvious fix is wrong, and what the right one is. The read-only tier exists so a reader cannot edit the code it is grading, and that purpose is sound — widening the tier to the worktree would destroy it. But docs/research/ is not the code under grading. The tier is denying a category it has no reason to deny, because it is keyed on inside or outside the repository when the property it wants is source and tests, or not. The repair is to grant a read-only role its declared paths under the documentation tree while continuing to refuse source and tests — which is the same shape as the two disagreements already recorded against this section: a fence and a contract that answer different questions about what a role may write.

It also silently weakens the prior-art rule. A scout's reuse map is required to be cited by the nodes that follow it, and citation across sessions requires the map to be readable from a checkout. Today it is readable only on the machine that produced it, so the requirement is satisfiable in the session that dispatches the scout and unenforceable afterwards — which is why the four maps this plan cites are consulted by hand rather than linked from the evidence record.

the-landing-grant-follows-the-writable-worktree landed — commit 20e52db, merged at 72f198c. Gate: the read-only dry-run measure, passed. Measured at the merged head rather than in the worktree: --role review and --role investigate each validate with ok true, zero scoped findings and only their declared path, while --role implement keeps three paths including both landing files. _receives_landing_contract is replaced by _can_write_worktree, which resolves the sandbox write roots and asks whether the repository is writable — the same authority that scopes declared write paths — so the grant and the reachability judgement now read one source instead of two.

The node ran 109 minutes against a 25-minute budget and was stopped before writing its own landing record. Its code was merged and pushed; its remaining work was a plan record this coordinator had already written, and its worktree's copy of the plan was some sixty versions stale, so authoring there would have produced a large conflict rather than an append git could reconcile. Nothing uncommitted was lost — verified clean, and its commit already reachable from main — and the pointer is reconciled as stopped rather than promoted. Recorded because a stopped run that did land its work is a different outcome from one that failed, and the ledger should not have to infer which.

Cost of the regression it repaired: introduced at 04:37, found at 05:00, merged at 06:45. In that window every review and investigate node in this repository was undispatchable, which blocked the prior-art scouts this plan's own rules require and the independent reviews its promotion gate requires. Four sessions were affected and three reached the wrong conclusion about whether it was already fixed, for the separate reason recorded in §15.

SIXTH CONTRACT-VERSUS-FENCE INSTANCE, AND THE FIRST ENTIRELY INSIDE ONE PROMPT. Reported by a peer coordinator whose worker blocked cleanly rather than racing, and reproduced here from a live dispatch payload. The worker is handed both of these:

CONTRACT — LANDING YOUR RECORD
  Append your landing record to your own section of the plan and your evidence
  anchor to the cumulative evidence record; both live in this worktree...

PEER SCOPES
  a-second-key-on-a-manifest-line-is-reported:
      docs/plans/crew-observability-truth.html
      docs/evidence/archive/crew-observability-truth-landed.html

Measured on this session's own dispatch: 25 peer entries, and every same-plan peer discloses exactly those two paths as its claim. So the prompt instructs the worker to append to a file and, forty lines later, tells it the file belongs to somebody else. A careful worker blocks with a NEEDS-HELP, which is what the peer's node did and the right call on the information it had.

The disclosure is simply false, and that is what makes this fixable rather than a trade-off. The landing paths were deliberately exempted from the exclusive-claim refusal when the grant landed, on the stated reasoning that every node on one plan appends to the same two files and git merge reconciles the appends — which is true and was demonstrated twice today, once by five concurrent runs of this session holding the same plan file and dispatching normally, and once by a worker-authored record merging clean against ten intervening commits. A path that is shared by design is not an exclusive claim, so listing it under a peer's exclusive scope states the opposite of the rule the dispatcher is following.

The repair is to stop disclosing the shared landing paths as peer claims, rather than to explain them in the contract. Explaining would leave a worker reconciling two true statements at run time, every run; excluding them makes the peer-scope block mean one thing again — these paths are exclusively someone else's. The peer's proposed test (two concurrent nodes, same plan, disjoint write paths, must both dispatch) already passes and is not the falsifier: dispatch admits them today. The assertion that fails is on the composed prompt — a peer's disclosed scope must not contain the plan or evidence path that this node is simultaneously instructed to append to.

Why this was invisible to the session that introduced it. The grant's own tests proved the two things its author checked — that a second dispatch on the same plan is admitted, and that promotion accepts a run that wrote both paths. Both pass. Neither looks at the prompt, and the defect is only in the prompt. A change that is correct in the dispatcher and wrong in the text it composes will pass every test written against the dispatcher.

landed and merged at 72f198c; read-only roles validate with zero scope findings and implement keeps both landing paths; stopped before authoring its landing record, which the coordinator wrote

A REVIEW THAT RAN NINETY-TWO MINUTES AND PRODUCED NO REVIEW, FOR TWO REASONS THAT WERE BOTH THE COORDINATOR'S. Recorded because one of them was predicted in this session and then walked into anyway, and the other is a requirement nothing states.

First: an implement-role review receives the landing contract, and does landing work. While the read-only roles were undispatchable, reviews were dispatched at implement to obtain a writable tier. That workaround was flagged at the time with its own hazard — an implement-role review node also receives the landing grant, so it can author a plan comment claiming a landing; a review is not a landing — and the hazard was reported to a peer as something to watch for at merge. It then happened here. The node's own words near the end of its run: let me view the exact insertion points in my tree — end of §20 in the plan, and end of the evidence file. It was composing a landing record instead of its review record, because the prompt it was handed told it to. Predicting a failure mode does not prevent it; only removing the condition does, which is what the role repair did.

Second, and not previously written down anywhere: a review node's worktree must contain the commits it is reviewing. Its own report: my tree lacks the reviewed commits (they're the subject of this review). The node was dispatched while the diff was still unmerged, so its worktree was cut from a base that predates the work it was asked to judge. A reviewer with no access to its subject cannot refuse usefully — it has nothing to read — and this one spent its budget on the only writable thing in front of it. The requirement is a scheduling one: a review is dispatched after its subject is merged, or it is dispatched with the commit reachable in its tree. All six commits under review here were merged before the three replacements went out, and those three are running normally.

The cost, stated plainly: ninety-two minutes of a contended lane, no review record, and three verified landings held in scoring that could not promote. The replacement reviews went to the metered lane instead, which had 79% of its weekly window remaining against a local lane at twenty-plus concurrent — the first routing decision in this session taken from the figures rather than by default, and the one the load measures in §22 exist to make ordinary.

Shared landing paths no longer appear as exclusive peer claims. Commit 4477e1975de6700e0caf15c91486f84418275370 filters the resolved plan and cumulative-evidence paths after live and supplied peer scopes are merged, while keeping every genuinely exclusive peer path. The failing prompt reproduced the contradiction as docs/evidence/archive/fixture-landed.html, docs/plans/fixture.html, reckon/crew/ticker.py; after the repair both shared paths are absent and reckon/crew/ticker.py remains. The focused assertion passes 1 of 1, and the prompt-composition plus dispatch-guard gate advances from 24 passing cases at base to 25 passing cases after with zero failures. Full record: peer-scope landing-path evidence.

A delivered review enters scoring itself, so the lifecycle prescribes reviewing the review. rev-promotion-dedup read a landed diff, wrote its score to the review store, and went terminal with a manifest — which is the entry condition for scoring. The unreconciled-runs refusal then handed back, as its prescribed disposition, a crew dispatch --role review --node review-of-rev-promotion-dedup. Obeying it would produce a score of a score, whose delivery would enter scoring in turn.

The lifecycle in the table above reads terminal with manifest → scoring → review attached → promotable and does not distinguish the role that produced the terminal run. A review node satisfies every clause of it.

It is bounded in practice only because a coordinator notices and stops. That is the same shape as every other finding here: the rule is right, nothing checks the case it does not cover, and the protection is a person reading the prescription before running it. The condition to add is narrow — a run whose role is review has no review dimension to score, so its terminal disposition is reconciliation, not a further review.

Disposed by discarding the pointer rather than dispatching: the review it produced is on disk and was used to promote promotion-stops-authoring-the-record-the-worker-writes, so the run's evidence is intact and the pointer was the only thing outstanding.

A review that found no defects is indistinguishable from a review that never happened, and the node it cleared sits in scoring while the surface asks for a review that already exists.

the-endpoint-finding-silences-only-a-leaf has a stored review: status: parsed, all five dimensions scored, total 95. Its node stayed scoring, and its next_action read dispatch a review. The gate is recovery.py:196:

and not review.get("absent")

and that record's absent field holds ['no defect found in the fix; the only notes are below in the justifications, none blocking']. A reviewer reporting that it found nothing wrong populated the field whose truthiness means this review does not count.

The field is derived, and also accepted from the record. review.py:161 computes it as [dim for dim in REVIEW_DIMENSIONS if dim not in scores] — the dimensions carrying no recognised SCORE line — and the prompt describes it that way. But review.py:283 reads absent back out of the record and coerces each entry to a string, so an emitted value survives verbatim and overrides the computation. A field that is authored where it should only be calculated is the defect; the naming is what makes a reviewer author it, because "absent" reads as a place to say what was not found.

This is §21's own stated failure mode occurring to §21. The section requires that an unparsed review must not be stored as an absent one, for the same reason a stale hold and an honest unknown must stay distinct. What happened is the third case neither of us named: a fully parsed, high-scoring review stored as an absent one, and the distinction the section exists to protect collapsed from the other direction.

The cost is a node held out of promotion with no signal that anything is wrong — the review is on disk, readable, and scored, and every surface reports it missing. Repair: compute absent and never read it from the record, which also removes the naming trap.

Correction to the comment above: the absent defect misdirects a coordinator, it does not strand a node. I overstated the cost.

I wrote that a node is held out of promotion with no signal that anything is wrong. It is not held. _review_is_complete has exactly one caller, recovery.py:1325, which sets the displayed classification; promotion.py contains no review gate of any kind. A coordinator who simply runs crew complete succeeds. Verified here by grep over the package after a peer reported it.

The peer also measured the frequency, which I had not. Scanning all 33 stored review records for the signature — a non-empty absent beside a non-null total, a pair the computing path cannot produce because review.py:164 sets total = None whenever the computed absent is non-empty — returns 3 of 33, 9%. All three absent values are reviewer prose, which confirms the field name invites the authoring. Two of the three are from 2026-09-14 and both promoted anyway; the third is the one here, still in flight.

So the accurate cost: the surface reports scoring with next_action: dispatch a review for a review already on disk, and a coordinator who trusts the display spends a worker re-reviewing something that was never missing. Misdirection rather than a hold, and it has stranded nobody's work.

Worth keeping the reason the narrower claim reached me at all: the peer's first ledger parse returned zero rows and would have reported both 09-14 runs as unpromoted — "two nodes stranded since yesterday", false. A positive control against a known-promoted run caught it before the claim was sent. That is the same instrument discipline that has corrected four claims on this plan today, applied by its author before publication rather than by a reader afterwards.

A SECOND MANIFEST-KEY DEFECT, ROUTED FROM ANOTHER PROJECT'S COORDINATOR, AND IT IS THIS SECTION'S DEFECT ONE STEP LATER. A worker wrote commit: 61e85056 with its changed paths beneath — singular, where the schema reads commits. Promotion refused with manifest field 'commits' is missing (promotion.py:517). The statement is true of the key and false of the fact: the commit was reported, and reported accurately. Reproduced here against the live parser:

parse_manifest("node: n\nstatus: complete\ncommit: 61e85056\nchanged_paths: a.py b.py\n")
  commits      -> []            <- the refusal's subject
  unknown keys -> ['commit', ...]  <- the fact, parsed, retained, and read by nothing

So the near-miss key survives parsing and sits in the returned mapping while the refusal declares the fact absent. This is the same shape as the one-line form this section already owns — a manifest that parses into a wrong value rather than refusing — with the wrongness moved from the value to the diagnosis. The reporter's phrasing is the one to keep: the refusal names no near-miss.

The codebase already does the right thing one field over, which is what makes this a gap rather than a policy. reports.py:36 accepts in-progress and in_progress as the same status, deliberately, and a peer confirmed today that two workers on one lane wrote both spellings with no cost. So near-miss tolerance exists for status and not for commits, and nothing records why the two fields differ. Either accepting commit as an alias, or naming the near-miss key in the refusal text, closes it; the second is strictly better, because it turns a silent mismatch into a sentence a reader can act on without widening what the schema accepts.

How the reporter recovered it is worth preserving as the pattern. They verified the sha existed and carried exactly the three named files, preserved the original as manifest.md.orig, corrected only the key, and recorded that they had done so in the gate log — reasoning explicitly that crew discard would have destroyed more record than it protected. That is the right trade in both directions: the evidence survives, and the fact that a human hand touched it survives with it.

ROOT CAUSE OF THIS SECTION'S DEFECT, AND OF A SECOND ONE ROUTED FROM ANOTHER PROJECT: THE MANIFEST BLOCK THE WORKER IS HANDED DOES NOT MATCH THE SCHEMA THE READER ENFORCES. Both are in one twenty-line block in reckon/crew/prompts.py, and in both cases the worker did exactly what it was told.

First, this section's own defect is taught by the template. Line 220 of the block reads:

commits: <sha list>; changed_paths: <explicit list>

Two keys on one line, separated by a semicolon — the exact malformed form this section documents, printed in the contract as the example to follow. This section previously recorded that three of this session's workers wrote the one-line form and treated it as a worker-side habit worth guarding against. It is not a habit. It is compliance. The parser repair in flight is still correct and still wanted — a reader must refuse rather than misparse — but it repairs the symptom, and the template is the cause; landing only the parser would leave every future worker instructed to produce a manifest the parser now refuses, which is worse than today.

Second, the same block omits a status the reader accepts and four fields it requires. Reported by the nova handoff coordinator against a run that submitted a batch job and parked: the manifest declared status: waiting with the job id in its prose, and the follower emitted dispatched → unreadable — declares an external wait but is incomplete: missing or invalid wait_condition, wait_probe, wait_terminal, resume_brief. Consequences: crew resume-ready cannot resume it when the job ends, it counts as blocked in the fleet figures, and the coordinator hand-rolls a squeue loop and resumes by hand. Measured here:

prompts.py:222   status: complete | blocked | failed        <- 'waiting' is not offered
grep -c 'wait_condition|wait_probe|wait_terminal|resume_brief' prompts.py  -> 0
reports.py       WAITING_STATUS = 'waiting'                 <- the reader accepts it
recovery.py:1015-1025   requires all four fields for a wait to be readable

And the contract actively invites the incomplete form it then refuses. The time fence at line 209 tells the worker: a run that ends mid-wait is resumable, so if you run out of turns, leave a record naming exactly what you were waiting for. That instructs the behaviour in prose and never names the four keys that make it machine-readable. So a worker following the contract to the letter produces an unreadable wait, and the surface reports it as the worker's defect.

The class, stated once. This is the fifth instance today of a contract and a fence disagreeing about what a worker may or must write — after the read-only landing grant, the test role's repository paths, the read-only tier's documentation paths, and the worker-authored record. The others were a fence forbidding what the contract instructed. This one inverts: the fence requires what the contract never mentions, which is harder to see because the refusal names a field rather than a permission, and reads as a worker that got it wrong.

The repair, in the order that matters. The template carries the wait block — waiting in the offered vocabulary plus the four fields — and puts commits and changed_paths on separate lines, so both defects stop being produced. Then the reader degrades rather than refusing: a wait naming a batch job id gets a default probe and terminal condition, so an under-specified declaration still resumes instead of becoming unreadable. The first stops new instances; the second recovers the workers already in flight, which is why both are wanted and why the template is first.

THE REFUSAL THIS SECTION ASKED FOR OVER-MATCHES, AND THE INDEPENDENT REVIEW FOUND IT IN CODE THE COORDINATOR HAD ALREADY MERGED. Review of the parser node scored 89 with fit at 15, its lowest dimension, citing reckon/crew/reports.py:118: the key-on-line pattern treats any manifest-field word followed by a colon anywhere in an unquoted plain-text value as a second top-level key. Reproduced directly:

REFUSED  commits: abc1234; changed_paths: a.py                 <- correct
REFUSED  tests: ran the suite; artifacts: none were produced    <- FALSE POSITIVE
PARSED   tests: ran pytest -q -> EXIT=0, 34 passed             <- correct
PARSED   artifacts: see http://host:8080/x                      <- correct
PARSED   commits / changed_paths on two separate lines          <- correct

The exposure is wide because of which line it lands on. Every worker writes a tests: line of free prose, and the manifest vocabulary is ordinary English — artifacts, blockers, commits, status all occur naturally in a sentence describing a test run. So the guard built to catch a malformed manifest will refuse well-formed ones whose prose happens to name a field, and it has been live across the fleet since the merge.

It is the right kind of wrong, which is the only mitigation worth claiming. A false refusal is loud, names the line, and costs a coordinator one hand-correction; the defect it replaced was a silent misparse that disarmed a promotion guard. Merging ahead of the template repair was a judgement recorded at the time with that reasoning, and this is its cost arriving as predicted rather than as a surprise. The narrowing is dispatched; the section's original measure is unchanged.

Second finding from the same review pair, and the coordinator merged past this one too. The reason-clip node's commit subject carries the literal plan slug, which the dispatch contract forbids — commit messages must not name plan identifiers. Recorded rather than rewritten: the commit is pushed and shared, and a history rewrite to scrub a slug from one subject line costs more than the rule protects. The reviewer also caught that node's landing record reporting 88 tests at base against 88 after while its own commit adds one collected test, so the two figures cannot both be right. That is the same class as its 75-versus-69 column discrepancy already noted here: a record whose subject is figures losing their inputs, losing its own.

Three reviews, three scores of 89, and three findings in work this coordinator had verified and merged. Each was a distinction read past rather than a defect concealed — an unauthorised silence checked off against a different clause, a regex scope not probed against prose, a naming rule not applied to a subject line. The gate is not catching bad workers; it is catching a careful reader's blind spots, which is the case for it that the saturated worker-authored gate could never make.

parser now refuses a manifest line carrying a second key instead of misparsing it into a corrupt commit and an empty path list; 41 assertions pass, 4 added; independent review scored 89 and found the pattern over-matches prose, repaired under a follow-on node

Commit ac8621b narrows the refusal to the unambiguous machine-field pair commits followed by changed_paths, preserving the guard against the empty changed-paths misparse without treating ordinary prose as structure. The exact false refusal was reproduced first: tests: ran the suite; artifacts: none were produced raised and named artifacts as a second key. It now parses as the complete tests value.
