A COMMIT IDENTIFIER IS RUN THROUGH NUMERIC COERCION AND THE WRONG VALUE REACHES THE COMMITTED LEDGER, SILENTLY. Found by the imas-codex coordinator, relayed by a peer who reproduced it, and reproduced again here at HEAD with the trigger narrowed:

commits: [29937e892]            -> ['inf']              CORRUPTED
commits: [29937e8]              -> ['2993700000000.0']  CORRUPTED
commits: [1e5]                  -> ['100000.0']         CORRUPTED
commits: [29937e892, abc1234]   -> ['29937e892', 'abc1234']   safe
commits: ["29937e892"]          -> ['29937e892']        safe
commits: 29937e892              -> ['29937e892']        safe

The trigger is narrower than first reported and still live. It is a bracketed list carrying exactly one unquoted element that parses as a number. Two or more elements are not valid JSON as written, so the reader falls back to string splitting and is safe; quoted elements and the bare comma form are safe. A short sha of the form <digits>e<digits> is valid scientific notation, so 29937e892 becomes 2.9937e896, overflows, and lands as inf.

Rate, since a narrow trigger is not the same as a rare one. A seven-character hex short sha matches that shape with probability about 0.0186, or one in fifty-four, and a single-commit landing is the common case for a focused node. So roughly one landing in fifty carries a commit id the ledger records wrongly, and every one of them is silent: no refusal, no warning, and inf is not a plausible-looking value that a reader would question — it is one nobody will ever look at.

It is the exact inverse of this section's own subject and belongs beside it. The defect this section opened with was a manifest that parses into a wrong value rather than refusing, and its remedy was a reader that refuses rather than half-succeeds. Here the reader does not merely half-succeed, it succeeds confidently with a fabricated number — and a commit identifier is the one field where a fabricated value is unrecoverable, because nothing downstream resolves it: the coordinator supplies the sha at promotion, so no check ever asks git whether inf exists. The repair is that a commit identifier is never a number, which is a statement about the field rather than about the syntax, and is why fixing the scalar coercion generally would be the wrong shape.

Two further reports carried as reported-not-reproduced, from the same finder: a colon-less markdown bullet folds into the preceding key's value, so a 6,757-byte manifest was absorbed whole into its status string while a sibling of the same shape parsed only because every bullet happened to contain a colon; and two concurrent crew complete calls race on the plan HTML atomic rename, raising FileNotFoundError on the .tmp in os.replace and leaving both runs at scoring with the plan uncommitted. The second is worth noting as self-inflicted-but-recurring — the finder backgrounded two completes at once, which a fleet coordinator will keep doing, so the serialisation likely belongs in the tool rather than in a convention.

A tally worth keeping. A peer's fleet hit three distinct manifest-format failures in one session from three different workers — two keys on one line, a manifest with fields but no status key, and status: done outside the recognised set making a finished node with two commits and five passing tests read as unreadable. In every case the work was intact and only the record was unreadable, which is the good failure mode. Three in one day from three workers is the argument that the format is harder to emit than it looks from the template side, and it is why the template repair landed this session matters more than any single parser fix.

THE WAIT FIELDS THIS SESSION ADDED PRODUCED A PERMANENT PARK ON THEIR FIRST USE, AND THE TEMPLATE TEXT IS THE CAUSE. Measured on the first worker dispatched after the template repair merged. It wrote a structurally flawless declaration — all four keys, correct types, a valid argument-vector probe — and the run is unresumable:

status:         waiting
wait_condition: implementation and focused verification are in progress
wait_probe:     [pytest -q tests/test_roadmap.py]     <- its own gate
wait_terminal:  ["exit:0","exit:1"]

resume-ready -> skipped, reason condition-pending
  detail: condition probe reported '36 passed in 0.48s' with exit 0

Two defects, and the second is the trap. The first is semantic: the worker declared a wait on its own work rather than on an external event, which takes a working run out of the working count and into a resume queue. The second is that the wait can never end. The template text this session landed states that terminal values are matched against the probe's last line, with exit:<code> standing in when it prints nothing. This probe prints 36 passed in 0.48s, so the exit: forms never apply, nothing matches, and resume-ready will skip it on every sweep forever while the run holds a worktree, a member and a pointer.

The state looks healthy, which is what makes it this plan's own subject. A permanently parked run reports waiting — an ordinary, expected, non-alarming classification. Nothing reports that its terminal condition is unsatisfiable, and the sweep's condition-pending is indistinguishable from a wait that is merely not over yet. A run that can never resume and a run that will resume shortly present identically.

The template is the cause and the guidance is what needs repairing, not the schema. The field descriptions state the exit: semantics precisely and subtly, and a competent worker read them as exit codes that end the wait rather than a fallback for a silent probe. Nothing in the block says a wait is for an event outside the worker's own control, so declaring one on your own test run is not excluded by anything written. Both are one-line additions to the same block repaired this morning.

Recorded as this session's own change failing on first contact. The template repair was landed to stop a class of malformed manifests, and it introduced a new one within two hours — the same shape as everything else recorded today: a change correct in its own surface, meeting a reader whose matching rule was written under a different assumption, with the contradiction visible only when a real worker used it. The repair was still right; shipping it without a falsifier that exercised an actual wait declaration is what left this uncaught.

The run is not being killed. Its process is alive and generating, so it may yet overwrite the manifest with a terminal status of its own. Killing a working worker on the strength of a manifest field is the failure this plan recorded twice today from two directions, and the discipline applies to the coordinator that wrote the finding.

CORRECTION TO THE COMMENT ABOVE: THE RUN WAS NEVER PARKED, AND THIS COORDINATOR ASSERTED REALISED HARM THAT DID NOT OCCUR. That comment said the run is unresumable and would be skipped on every sweep forever. Four minutes later it transitioned waiting → dispatched, overwrote its own declaration, and is running normally. Its manifest has since read blocked and it is still generating.

What was wrong: the wait declaration was a mid-turn checkpoint, not a verdict. The worker wrote it while working, exactly as the contract instructs — write your manifest with what you know now — and replaced it as its own understanding changed. Reading a manifest field as a terminal state is the error this plan documents repeatedly, and it was committed here by the coordinator that documented it, against its own fleet, within an hour of recording the rule a third time.

What survives, and it is the part worth keeping. The trap is real and remains unclosed: a wait whose wait_terminal carries only exit: forms while its probe prints output can never match, because terminal values are matched against the probe's last line and the exit: forms stand in only for a silent probe. A worker that ends its turn holding such a declaration is parked with no sweep that can wake it. This run escaped by continuing past its own checkpoint; a worker out of turns would not have. So the exposure is latent rather than realised, which is a materially smaller claim than the one made above and the honest size of it.

The distinction is exactly the one this plan exists to draw. A latent trap and a realised failure justify different urgency, and collapsing them inflates a finding in the direction that feels responsible — which is how an over-stated defect earns a fix it did not need while a real one waits. The template repair still wants the two one-line additions named above; it does not want them as an emergency.

And the operational decision was right for the stated reason rather than by luck. The run was left alive because its process was generating, on the rule that a coordinator must not kill a worker on the strength of a manifest field. It then proved the rule by recovering itself. Had it been stopped when its manifest first read waiting, the work would have been destroyed and the finding would have looked confirmed.

COMMIT IDENTIFIERS NOW KEEP THE LITERAL TEXT THE WORKER WROTE. The base reproduction converted [29937e892] to ['inf'], [29937e8] to ['2993700000000.0'], and [1e5] to ['100000.0']. Commit a30af4f8 gives only the commits field a literal-token path, so those values return unchanged while the quoted, multi-item, and bare-comma forms remain unchanged and the non-identifier control artifacts: [1e5] still follows generic numeric decoding. The focused gate advances from 46 passing cases at base to 53 after, with all 46 pre-existing cases unchanged and zero failures. Full record: §12 commit-identifier evidence.

Two defects in the manifest template produce the malformed wait declarations the classifier then refuses. Both are in prompts.py, and together they outrank the classifier repair: that one stops a correct run being mislabelled, these stop the malformed input existing.

A placeholder that names a type teaches nothing. Line 236 reads wait_probe: <only when setting status to waiting: the shell-free argument vector that answers the condition, run in your worktree>. "Shell-free argument vector" is a description of a type, in prose, inside a placeholder. Three workers across three sessions have met it and all three wrote a shell string: git status --porcelain, /bin/true, and nova's earlier instance. Putting ["git", "status", "--porcelain"] in the slot would have produced the right shape from all three without a word of explanation, and would have been shorter. A placeholder that shows an instance teaches the type.

The second is a missing state, not a wording defect, and it is the one that explains "no wait". Line 221 tells a worker running out of turns mid-wait to set status: waiting and fill the four wait keys — correct, and the only checkpoint idiom the template offers. Nothing covers a worker that is resumed and not waiting. So a worker resumed after the serve restart reached for the only shape available and wrote wait_condition: no wait — process was killed mid-turn by a deliberate server restart. It was not misreading the template; it was using the one slot it had. A resume checkpoint is a different state from a wait and the block has no room for it.

That reframes the classifier finding: the declaration is genuinely self-contradictory and the classifier is right to reject it, but the worker was correct to write something and had nowhere correct to write it. Fixing only the classifier leaves every resumed worker producing the same shape.

Both fold into the-wait-fields-show-their-form rather than a second node — one file, one block, one write scope, and splitting them would put two workers on prompts.py. The node is queued behind the promotable run that holds that file.

manifest block matches the schema the reader enforces; focused gate 49 passed at head, and the 12 failures in test_crew_orientation/test_gate_evidence measured identical id-for-id at base b5480eef and head 2e550a8b, zero added by set difference

The same-line refusal keys on the first field only, and that composes with the inverted promotion guard into a path that promotes a run with no declared evidence and refuses nothing. Found by the independent review of the-second-key-check-does-not-fire-on-prose (scored 95) and reproduced here at the landed head:

commits   then changed_paths  ->  REFUSED (ManifestParseError)
tests     then changed_paths  ->  PARSED, changed_paths=[]
artifacts then changed_paths  ->  PARSED, changed_paths=[]

So tests: ran via pytest; changed_paths: a.py returns tests intact and changed_paths silently emptied. The guard fires for commits as the leading key and for nothing else.

The composition is the finding. Each defect alone reads as a narrow edge case. Together: a worker writes the two-key line, the parser empties changed_paths without complaint, and the promotion guard at promotion.py:504 — which refuses only when changed_paths is non-empty and commits is missing — returns early on the now-empty field. The run promotes carrying no declared changed paths, and nothing anywhere reports a problem. The parser's silence manufactures exactly the input the promotion guard cannot see.

The done-when was mine and it was too narrow. §12 asked that "a manifest line carrying a second key after the first value is reported rather than silently misparsed". The reviewer's verdict is that this is satisfied for the measured commits→changed_paths pair and not for the general shape — the worker built what was asked. That is the fifth instance recorded today of a falsifier ranging over the cases already seen rather than over the input domain, and the first where the narrow measure is the coordinator's own.

A second review finding, recorded as provenance rather than a defect of the node: the promotion guard converts a ManifestParseError into a return through its (OSError, KeyError, ValueError) catch, so the refusal is upheld at the parser boundary and the guard itself never speaks.

The decorated-field reader changed how an existing valid manifest parses: an indented continuation whose leading word is manifest vocabulary is now a top-level field. Found by the independent review of the-reader-sees-the-key-through-markdown (scored 93) and measured here against the pre-change parser rather than accepted on assertion.

input:  evidence_inputs: prose first
          - blockers: sort of a bullet

at 3833e84c^   evidence_inputs  ['prose first', 'blockers: sort of a bullet']
               blockers         []

at HEAD        evidence_inputs  ['prose first']
               blockers         ['sort of a bullet']

blockers is a field a promotion reads, so this is not cosmetic: a worker writing "no blockers were found" as a bullet inside its evidence prose now registers a top-level blockers value it never declared. The continuation form is the manifest's own idiom — every list field in a well-formed manifest is written as - item lines beneath its key.

It is the mirror of the same-line defect recorded above, and the two together say the parser has no settled notion of a field boundary. On one line, a second vocabulary key after a value is swallowed and its field silently emptied. On the next line, an indented bullet beginning with a vocabulary word is promoted out of its parent. One direction loses a field the worker wrote; the other invents one it did not.

A second review finding, recorded as provenance: the decorated matcher is not symmetric with the scalar reader for a JSON-literal value — baseline_suite: {…} parses whole when plain, while the mechanism comment claims decorated lines are recognised only within the manifest vocabulary, a claim asserted for prose bullets and not for an indented vocabulary-word bullet under a prior field.

The node delivered what it was asked and the regression is a side effect its own gate could not see, because the gate asserted the new decorated forms rather than the unchanged behaviour of the old ones. The measure that would have caught it is a corpus check: every manifest already on disk parses identically before and after.

THE RUN RECORD NOW REPORTS WHETHER THE RUN RESUMED, NOT WHETHER ITS LANE PERMITS IT. Commit 1f250057 adds session_resumed to the record and renames the copied setting to session_reuse_capable, so a reader can tell the lane's configuration apart from this run's act.

Reproduction, measured on this workstation rather than quoted. The base record wrote bool(backend.get("session_reuse")) straight onto the pointer, so the field stated the lane's setting. Live pointers: true on 51 of 51, and 47 of those carried no session_id at all; re-measured while the corpus moved, true on 50 of 50, 46 without a session id and 2 whose argv actually carried a prior session. The falsifier ranges over the input domain and fails at base on all five cases: a lane permitting reuse with nothing to reuse, the same lane with a captured session, a lane with reuse disabled and a captured session present, a lane change that keeps the harness, and one to a destination that cannot follow it.

Mechanism. session_resumed is derived from the launched argv through _launched_prior_session, which returns the session only when the plan's own command line carries it, so the fact comes from this run's launch rather than from the configuration. All three writers carry the pair: the fresh dispatch record, the lane-change move, and the resume attempt.

Two more reviews died writing no record, taking the measured count on this project from five to seven. Both carry the documented signature exactly — subtype: success, is_error: false, and terminal text stopping mid-sentence on work it was about to begin, with no error anywhere in the stream.

RunTurnsRecordsTerminal text ends on
review-of-the-auto-created-member-commits-like-the-declared-one283,870Running the gate at the merged head now
review-of-the-manifest-is-read-through-a-declared-schema428,298Checking whether it escapes audit_manifest, and re-running the gate with the log dir created

Both are recorded as failed runs with classification infrastructure-failure rather than discarded, so the rate stays countable. The death is shorter, not longer — 28 and 42 turns against 49 tool uses for both prior successes — so a tighter time budget is the wrong lever and was not pulled.

The second death carried a claim about merged code, and the claim does not hold. Its last sentence asserted that _decode_yaml_node crashes with an unhashable dict key on a real manifest, escaping the declared ManifestParseError. That would have been a defect in the reader repair merged at e88d2fcb an hour earlier, and it was one edit away from being written onto this plan as a finding.

It was tested instead. Four synthetic shapes that force a mapping or a sequence into key position — a flow mapping as a top-level key, an explicit-key mapping, a flow sequence as a key, and a mapping key nested under a field — all parse cleanly, so the instrument was aimed at the real population rather than trusted on synthetics: 3,704 real manifests on this host, 2,963 parsed, 741 declared ManifestParseError refusals, and zero escaped exceptions of any type. The 741 are the positive control, since an unaimed scan and a genuine absence return the same empty result. The merged repair stands.

The general rule this establishes, which is worth more than the claim was: a dead review's last sentence is a hypothesis, not a finding. The signature of this failure is a process ending mid-investigation, so its final text is by construction the thing it was about to check rather than the thing it concluded. Reading it as a result inverts the reliability of the most suspect sentence in the stream. Recover the claim, then range an instrument over the real population before recording it either way.

A second lesson, from getting it wrong. The first of these two was promoted before checking whether it could be resumed, and it could: its live pointer carried a captured session, and promotion deletes the pointer crew resume depends on. The second had session_id: null, so no resume path existed and promoting it was correct. The discriminator is the live pointer's own session_id, and it is not visible in the classification — both rows render identically as a dead review. Read that field before reconciling any run, because the two cases look the same and only one is recoverable.

Two more self-satisfying wait declarations, measured on imas-codex and reported by its S14 coordinator on 2026-09-18. They matter because they are a different backend-and-role pairing from the instances recorded here, so the defect is not particular to one lane's prompt: both were clive implement nodes, both wrote wait_probe: ["true"] with wait_terminal: exit:0, and their condition strings read nothing yet; orientation only and in-progress placeholder — reproduction running. A probe returning zero immediately can never lift the wait, so the recovery sweep never resumes them.

What it cost there. The first sat 19,000 seconds holding roughly 200 lines of uncommitted work with no commit behind it, and was resumed by hand. That is the dangerous shape rather than the annoying one: a reclaimed worktree would have taken the work outright with nothing to recover from.

The second was still live while its coordinator wrote, and it is the cleanest specimen of the classifier half. At 27 minutes of a 35-minute budget it was alive with a 0-second log age, writing files, with both deliverables on disk, and classified wait-aged against a condition it had itself declared to be a placeholder. Its coordinator was watching it by hand because no automatic signal could be trusted about it. That is the case the repair in flight addresses directly — reading liveness before aging a declared wait — and it is worth keeping as the acceptance specimen, because it separates the two halves cleanly: the declaration is a fiction the worker wrote, and the aging is a verdict the classifier reached about a run that was demonstrably working.

Both halves reproduce here. an-orientation-stub-declares-no-wait displayed the same fiction for eleven hours while landing four commits, and this coordinator misread it as a stall before its owner corrected the reading. A node repairing false wait markers displaying one while it works is the most direct demonstration of the defect anyone has produced, and it is the reason the marker cannot be trusted as evidence of anything about the run's state.

The eighth review death is the first that left something behind, and that is the whole finding. review-of-the-auto-created-member-commits-like-the-declared-one ran 13,000 stream records, captured no session, left a manifest the reader refuses for want of a status key — and wrote a 2,623-byte review record at 12:36 before it stopped. Every earlier death in this census wrote nothing at all.

So the write-early mitigation works, and that is the problem. It was adopted because seven reviews had done substantial work and produced no record; it did exactly what it was asked to do. What it produced here is a record scoring every dimension at precisely 12 with findings: [] — an initialisation that a gate keyed on existence-and-parse accepts as an independent review. The failure mode was not removed; it was converted.

The conversion is a loss, not a wash, and the reason is asymmetric. A dead review that wrote nothing is honest: the run sits in scoring, promotion is refused, and a coordinator sees exactly what happened. A dead review that wrote a skeleton is not: the run advances to promotable and the next reader sees a verdict. Absence announces itself; a placeholder does not. Measured across both projects today, the two states are indistinguishable at every surface a coordinator or a classifier reads.

This is the same shape this plan exists for. A true statement — the review record exists and parses — standing in for the one that was needed, which is that a reviewer considered the work. The census figure to carry forward is eight deaths, of which one left a skeleton; expect that ratio to invert as the instruction propagates, because the instruction is working.

Recorded as a failed run with classification infrastructure-failure rather than discarded, so the death stays countable and the skeleton stays inspectable. Its parent run is not promoted on that record; a re-review is queued.

review record delivered for r-20260917T173739257673-the-audit-test-carries-its-own-specimen and parsed all five dimensions, total/findings 97 1; the parent run was promoted on it at 97

review record delivered for r-20260918T081711944084-the-declared-schema-reads-what-the-old-reader-read and parsed all five dimensions, total/findings 97 3; the parent run was promoted on it at 97

The self-declaration worked on existing machinery

Every pointer that leaves is reported as promoted, so the display cannot tell work banked from work erased. reckon/crew/recovery.py:2838:

changes.append((departed, str(known[run_id]["state"]), "promoted"))

Measured rather than reasoned. A peer coordinator discarded the pointer from a failed dispatch with crew discard — whose own help text says it removes a pointer without recording promoted evidence. The ticker emitted abandoned → promoted and the unpromoted counter fell by one. Verified here against the committed ledger: 0 rows for that run id, against 1,072 total. Nothing was promoted, no evidence exists, and the display said the work was banked.

promoted there means the pointer disappeared, which is the one inference a departure cannot support. A pointer departs on promotion, on discard, and on any rollback that manages to unlink it — work banked, work erased, and work lost. The ticker renders all three identically, and the counter falls the same amount for each.

The direction of the error is what makes it serious. An orchestrator clearing a backlog by discarding watches its inbox drain while the ledger stays still, and is congratulated at every step. That is the same reward shape as a review scoring higher for finding nothing: the display pays out for the action that destroys the evidence.

The repair is the rule this sprint keeps arriving at, on a fourth surface. Do not infer the verdict from the free-running observation; require the declared record. A departure resolves to promoted only when a ledger row for that run id exists, and otherwise to a state with its own word — discarded where the pointer was removed deliberately, something else again where it simply vanished. Two declared integers rather than a substring search; findings counted rather than prose; a ledger row present rather than a pointer absent. Same move each time.

The file is held by a-scoring-run-dispatches-its-own-review and this is adjacent to that node rather than inside it, so it is sequenced behind it rather than folded in.

It also answers the question the calibration pair left open, from the opposite direction. That pair showed a skeleton and a finished review summing identically at 84 while a dimension fell. So the total moves when the work changes underneath a review and stays still when the review changes above the work — which is precisely backwards from what a reader would assume, and makes the total useless for detecting either condition.

--time-budget is not a ceiling. It is exceeded in the majority of runs, and nothing enforces it. Flagged by a peer coordinator from a single 58% overrun; measured here across the committed ledger.

MeasureCount
ledger rows carrying both a stated budget and a measured duration398
overran on wall time219 (55%)
overran on worker time, so not wall-clock idling217 (55%)
overran on a first attempt, so no resume accumulation168
worst first-attempt overrun7.38× — 11,063s against 1,500s

The resume caveat was checked and does not rescue it. Sixty-one of the 398 rows are resumed runs whose wall time legitimately accumulates across attempts, and the worst raw figure — 20.9×, 62,714 seconds against 3,000 — is one of those. Excluding every resumed run still leaves 168 first-attempt overruns, and the worker-time count matches the wall-time count almost exactly (217 against 219), so the overruns are work rather than waiting.

What this invalidates is not the budget but everything written on top of it. The orchestration rules about bounded slices, the recovery reasoning that treats a long-running node as anomalous, and every brief either coordinator has written carrying a --time-budget value all assume a bound the surface does not provide. A worker that finishes inside its budget is keeping a convention; it is not being held to anything. A coordinator reading an overrun as a signal of trouble is reading the ordinary case — it happens more often than not.

It also changes what an overrun means as evidence. The review that died at 66 turns ran 58% past its budget, and the first objection raised against reading its terminal text as a mechanism was that a run killed at a ceiling shows its last words wherever the axe fell. That objection dissolves twice over: the terminal record carries stop_reason: end_turn with subtype: success, so nothing terminated it — and there is no axe, because 55% of runs pass the same mark untouched.

Recorded rather than repaired, deliberately. Whether the budget should bind is a design question with real costs on both sides: enforcing it would have killed 168 first-attempt runs that went on to deliver, and leaving it advisory means the word budget promises something it does not do. What must not continue is the third state, where the surface accepts a figure, records it on every row, and neither enforces nor marks it.

RETRACTION of the figures in the comment above. wall_seconds is wrong on roughly a third of rows, every number in that comment was built on it, and the operational conclusion reverses. Caught by the peer coordinator who had flagged the original symptom; reproduced here from the timestamps, which depend on no duration field at all.

retracted (from wall_seconds)correct (from completed_at − dispatched_at)
rows measured3981,074
overran219 — 55%244 — 23%
median ratio, all rowsnot stated0.39×
median among overrunsnot stated2.10×
worst20.9×27.56×

The contamination, with its specimen. r-20260826T050636932486-reuse-map carries time_budget: 4m, wall_seconds: 633062.8 — 7.3 days — and worker_seconds: 140, against its own stamps of 05:06:39 → 05:08:59. A 140-second run holding a seven-day wall figure. Measured across the ledger: 328 rows carry a wall_seconds exceeding their elapsed time by more than half, the worst by 18,520×. Agreement with elapsed is 69% for wall_seconds and 98% for worker_seconds, and the specimen's own worker_seconds_source reads wall_fallback, so the bad value is what the fallback path produced.

What survives is the design point, unchanged. 244 runs exceeded a stated budget with no consequence. The surface accepts the figure, records it on every committed row, and neither enforces nor marks it. That third state is the defect and 23% is ample to establish it.

What does not survive is the operational advice, and it was the dangerous half. The retracted comment said a coordinator reading an overrun as trouble is reading the majority case. It is the minority case at 23%, and the typical run finishes in 39% of its budget. So an overrun is a genuine signal worth attending to, and a coordinator who treats one as notable is right rather than naive. Left standing, that sentence would have retrained two coordinators to ignore a working signal — which is a worse outcome than the unenforced budget it was describing.

And the methodological lesson is sharper than the figure. I did check for an artifact: I tested whether accumulation across resumes inflated the number, found 61 resumed rows, excluded them, and reported 168 first-attempt overruns as the resume-proof residue. That check was sound and aimed at the wrong suspect. The inflation was not accumulation across attempts; it was a stored field that is simply wrong on a third of rows, and excluding resumes could not touch it. Verifying against a second derivation of the same quantity would have caught it; verifying against a plausible confounder did not. The timestamps were available the whole time and share nothing with the field under suspicion.

worker_seconds is safe to build on, and the disagreements all fall the harmless way. Asked by the peer coordinator before either of us starts trusting the field, on the correct grounds that 98% agreement is good and is not 100%.

MeasureResult
rows compared against completed_at − dispatched_at1,064
disagreeing by more than 10% or 60s11 (1.0%)
disagreements that overshoot elapsed0
range of the disagreements0.88× down to 0.12× — all under
source of every disagreeing rowstream_events

Every disagreement is worker time below wall elapsed, which is the expected direction — launch overhead and idle gaps between turns belong to the clock and not to the worker. The field never inflates. That is the property a calibration needs, and it is the opposite of wall_seconds, whose failures are all overshoot to the tune of 18,520×.

One thing the source breakdown settles that neither of us had assumed correctly. Across the ledger worker_seconds_source reads wall_fallback on 525 rows and stream_events on 456. So the fallback path is the majority source and is not the broken one — on the contaminated specimen it produced worker_seconds: 140, which is right, beside a wall_seconds of 633,062, which is not. The fallback is not copying the bad field. Whatever writes wall_seconds is a separate site from what fills worker_seconds when the stream is unavailable, and the investigation in flight should not assume they share a path.

Agreement between two readings of one field is not independent evidence

The methodological rule, which is the peer's phrasing and is the durable part of the whole episode. Two coordinators measured budget overruns independently, by different routes, on different row sets, and reached 55% and a comparable figure. Both were wrong, because both read wall_seconds. The disagreement that exposed it was luck: one of us happened to compute a worst case absurd enough to be disbelieved.

Had the two passes agreed, the contamination would have shipped carrying a corroboration stamp — two independent derivations, matching, both false. That is strictly worse than one unchecked number, because it converts a wrong figure into a confirmed one and closes the question. The check that works is a second derivation of the same quantity from a different input: here, timestamps, which share nothing with the field under suspicion and were available throughout.

This is the positive-control rule with the loophole named. Requiring evidence is not sufficient if the evidence and the claim are drawn from the same source — which is also why a test fixture quoting the string its guard greps for defeats the guard, and why a review record cannot vouch for its own completeness.

A gate selected from a node's declared write paths reports zero added failures while its change reds a file the gate never ran. The manifest's gate is pytest -q tests/test_sprint_push_status.py tests/test_mcp_roadmap.py, EXIT=0, 24 passed. The review reports an added failure: _sprint_drift_returned raises KeyError 'state_drift' in tests/test_surface_parity.py:126, because the change alters sprint-state derivation and the parity harness fixtures a stored-planned sprint whose middle member has started. test_surface_parity.py is not in the gate's selection. So zero added failures is true of the two files the gate ran and false of the tree, and nothing on either surface marks the difference.

This is the plan's own thesis arriving in the gate rather than the display. The gate's claim is a true statement about a selection, read as a statement about the tree. The selection is derived from the node's write paths, which describe what the change touches and not what it reaches — and a derivation is reached by everything that consumes it.

The worker was not careless and that is the sharpest part. Its own follow_ons open with a scope-extension request, highest priority, naming the adjacent breakage in tests/test_roadmap_sprint_derivation.py:66 with the measurement beside it — 1 failed, 1 passed, the diff isolated to the derived label at one line. It knew its change reached past its fence, said so in the manifest, and asked. Its session ended before anyone answered, so the request sat in a field nothing reads at promotion time.

Four findings hold this row unpromoted, and the review's own score tracks them — 71 total, durability 12 and evidence 12, the lowest of the day: the invisible added failure; reckon/roadmap.py:967, where the suppression set {planned, open, active} includes planned so a stored-planned sprint with started members reports in-progress and no state_drift; reckon/roadmap.py:1962, where a new top-level open_sprints takes the name of an existing schedule-readiness field meaning a different set, so a payload reader cannot tell the buckets apart by name; and lint findings added against this repository's own delta rule, PT011 and W292 with three of five landed files unformatted.

The repair is not a bigger gate. Running everything on every node is what the focused-gate rule exists to prevent, and it would have cost this node an hour to catch one file. The gate needs to include what imports what the node changed, which is derivable rather than remembered — the same move as every other repair recorded here: compute the discriminator from a fact rather than from the author's account of their own scope.

THE TOKEN DEFINITION IS CORRECT AND CONSISTENT, AND THIS SECTION'S OWN FIGURE WAS MEASURED ON THE WRONG POPULATION. The lead asked whether tok/s counts thinking. It does, and the empirical test settles it rather than the field names. On one completed run the terminal record reports output_tokens: 26,690, against an emitted-character estimate of 7,327 tokens for text and tool input alone and 24,284 with thinking included. The reported total tracks the second within ten percent, so thinking is inside output_tokens. The sibling detail field output_tokens_details.thinking_tokens reads 0 and means only that this wrapper leaves the breakdown unpopulated — it is not an exclusion, and reading it as one would invert the conclusion.

The per-dialect asymmetry is correct, not a defect. _CODEX_OUTPUT_FIELDS sums output_tokens and reasoning_output_tokens because that dialect reports reasoning separately; _CLAUDE_OUTPUT_FIELDS takes output_tokens alone because that shape already folds thinking in. Both count the same thing. The two field lists look inconsistent and are the only way to be consistent.

Correction to the measure row above. The 1.44 tok/s recorded there is emitted text only — a character proxy this coordinator computed by hand, excluding thinking, which is 73% of emitted characters on the run measured. It understates generation by roughly a factor of four and should not be read as this lane's throughput. The measured figures are below.

The lane is logged, contrary to the premise the section was written under. The committed ledger carries 262 local-lane rows with a rate, spanning 2026-08-26 to today, each with both denominators:

window     n    per-worker median   wall median   NET (sum gen / sum gen-seconds)
all time  262        22.54 tok/s     19.49 tok/s        18.47 tok/s
last 50    50        15.36           15.13              19.74
last 20    20        53.11           50.88              40.34

So the gap is narrower and sharper than stated: the history exists and the live reading does not. Measured on a run in flight, all 190 assistant usage blocks report output_tokens: 0; the true total appears only in the terminal result record. Throughput is therefore not computable for a running worker from the usage stream at all — which is the mechanical reason no live figure exists, and it means this section's live-reading measures cannot be satisfied by aggregating a field that is zero until the run ends. Either the wrapper populates per-block usage, or a live estimate is derived from emitted characters and declared as an estimate, which is this plan's standing rule about a figure naming the population it counted.

And a selection effect that makes the logged history flatter than the truth. The ledger holds only runs that completed. Under contention the slow runs are still running, so recent completions are biased toward the fast ones — which is why the last twenty rows read 53 tok/s per worker while today's live fleet crawls. A lane's logged throughput therefore over-reports exactly when contention is worst, and any routing rule read off the history without that caveat will widen a lane at the moment it should narrow it.

THE LIVE TICKER IS THE RIGHT INSTRUMENT, AND THE LANDED HISTORY IS NOT BROKEN — IT IS THE WRONG STATISTIC FOR THROUGHPUT AND THE RIGHT ONE FOR CALIBRATION. Asked by the lead, and the design falls out of three measurements rather than a preference.

First, the engine will not tell us. The locally served endpoint answers /v1/models with 200 and returns 404 on /metrics, /v1/metrics, /status, /health and /stats. There is no Prometheus surface, no running-request gauge, no queue depth. So §17's measure a saturated lane is read from the engine's own counter cannot be satisfied as written on this deployment; the counter does not exist to read.

Second, the protocol will not tell us either. Every assistant usage block on a run in flight reports output_tokens: 0, with the true total appearing only in the terminal result record. So no token-denominated live rate can be derived from the stream's own accounting.

Third, characters are live, and the conversion to tokens is stable. Measured across 72 completed streams carrying a terminal total, emitted characters over reported output tokens:

median 3.65   mean 3.64   stdev 0.19
IQR    3.54 .. 3.74       range 3.14 .. 4.16

A ±5% typical error against a routing question that needs to separate 20 tok/s from 2 tok/s — an order of magnitude — is ample. The estimate is sound; what it must never do is present itself as a measurement.

So the design is a rolling sampler in the follower, which already reads every stream continuously. Per tick, per run: emitted characters since the last tick, over the interval, divided by the calibration factor. Summed per lane it gives net serve throughput; divided by the generating population it gives the per-worker figure; the waiting population is counted separately and excluded from the denominator, which is the distinction that ruled out a cap. No new plumbing — the follower is already a continuous reader of exactly this data.

And the landed history becomes the calibration loop rather than a discarded dataset. It is a survivorship statistic for throughput — only completed runs are in it, so under contention the slow ones are still running and the sample skews fast, which is why its last twenty rows read 53 tok/s while the live fleet crawled. But the chars-per-token ratio does not depend on whether a run was fast, so the same biased dataset is an unbiased estimator of the conversion. Every landing yields a fresh character count and a true token total, re-measuring the factor for free. The dataset that cannot answer how fast is the lane is exactly the one that answers what is a character worth, and wiring the second into the first is what makes the live figure self-correcting instead of a constant somebody has to remember to revisit.

What this changes in the measures above. The live-reading rows stand, with their source named: a sampled estimate from emitted characters, carrying its calibration factor, the sample interval, the generating and waiting counts, and its vintage — never a bare number. The engine-counter row is amended by measurement rather than deleted: it stays the preferred source and is recorded as unavailable on this deployment, so a future serve that does expose counters supersedes the estimate rather than duplicating it.

CORRECTION TO THIS SECTION: CONCURRENCY WAS THE SYMPTOM AND THE CAUSE IS A PREFIX-CACHE COLLAPSE. Relayed by a peer coordinator from the serve owner's findings, with an independent measurement of their own. This section recorded that the lane was divided 37 ways and that each additional worker subtracts from every other. The arithmetic is right and the diagnosis it invited is wrong.

Two independent measurements agree, which is what makes the correction trustworthy. This session measured per-worker output by counting emitted characters and converting at the session's own measured thinking share; the peer read the serve's aggregate directly:

this session   37 concurrent   5.41 tok/s per worker   (implied aggregate 200)
peer           36 concurrent   5.50 tok/s per worker   (aggregate 198 measured)
peer           20 concurrent   9.50 tok/s per worker   (aggregate 190 measured)

Aggregate is flat. Dropping the fleet from 36 workers to 20 moved it 198 to 190 tok/s — within noise — where a contention story predicts it roughly holding per-worker and halving total. So shedding workers does not recover throughput; it only redistributes a fixed aggregate, which is why per-worker rate rose while the fleet's output did not. The peer's own instrument says the same from the other side: 13.9 tool calls per ten-minute bucket at ~35 workers against 16.5 at ~19-23, a 19% fleet gain where halving the fleet should have cost half.

The actual constraint. Prefix hit rate measured at 21–25% against an expected ~90%, with 18,863 of 22,557 tok/s of prefill recomputing prefixes the engine had already built. The remedy is a serve restart at a larger KV pool, not a smaller fleet. No amount of dispatch discipline reaches it.

What survives from this section and what does not. The measures stand: a lane that reports no load, a per-row rate with no denominator, and six coordinators each blaming their own node are all real and unchanged. What is withdrawn is the implied remedy — that a coordinator reading a low per-worker figure should dispatch less. It should dispatch less when told the lane is recovering, and otherwise the figure tells it how long a node will take, not that the fleet is at fault. A load reading that invites the wrong remedy is this plan's own subject, so the correction belongs here rather than as a footnote.

A requirement passed with it, and a tension worth naming. The serve owner wants any cap applied at dispatch and never as an admission filter on requests — a request-level refusal killed a worker's compaction and turned a recoverable condition into a dead run reporting a misleading prompt-length error. The figure is derived, (pool_tokens × 0.5) / mean_context_tokens, giving 32 post-restart but 7 at the 167k mean context also seen today, so no constant survives a node-mix change. The tension: the lead has already refused a cap on the grounds that a run parked on a batch job holds a slot and generates nothing, and that objection applies unchanged to a derived cap, because a dispatch cap still counts runs rather than in-flight requests. Recorded as an open conflict between two well-argued positions rather than resolved here; it needs the lead, and it needs the lane document the clive backend does not currently declare.

The dispatch-time context check admits a request the backend then refuses, because its sum omits its largest term. Two local-lane runs died at 5 s and 6 s with terminal_reason: blocking_limit and one assistant line, Prompt is too long. The refusal is client-side: input_tokens 0, duration_api_ms 0, so nothing was sent. The dry run had reported allowed: true.

routing.py:1506 reads estimated_tokens = standing_tokens + file_tokens — 243,379 repository-file tokens plus a 50,392 standing-instruction floor, 293,771 against a 1,016,576 window. The third term is the member's resumed transcript: both dead runs carried --resume <session-id> for a long-lived roster member, and _context_fit_verdict is handed only resolution and repo, so the omission is structural rather than a missed line.

Three hypotheses were measured and refuted before the cause was found, each sufficient-looking on its own evidence. Plan-file size: refuted — six concurrent runs read the same 765 KB plan successfully. Composed prompt size: refuted — the dead prompts were 12,196 and 13,268 bytes against a working 15,147. Transcript size: refuted, and this is the one worth keeping — resumed transcripts sort orthogonally to the outcome (est. 288 K and 1.08 M tokens died; 692 K, 1.49 M, 2.66 M, 3.03 M and 5.86 M ran clean). The client compacts on resume, so raw transcript bytes do not predict the refusal and must never become the third addend.

That is what makes this the plan's own subject rather than an arithmetic bug. The counted terms are countable and the uncounted one is not — so the honest repair is not a better estimate but an honest verdict: name the resumed session as an input the check does not count, the way headroom: unknown is already, correctly, not read as exhaustion. A sum over two of three terms, reported as allowed: true, is a surface stating a condition it cannot verify.

Operationally the fence is avoidable now: dispatching without --member mints a fresh session. The retry of a-finished-run-is-not-stalled on a session-scoped member has run cleanly for 54 minutes where the roster-member dispatch of the same node died twice.

One function's deliberately narrow contract is now relied on by five callers, three of which need what it discards — and its docstring still asserts the narrow contract. dispatch.py:3840:

argv = record.get("argv")
if isinstance(argv, list) and argv:
    return {"launch": "cli", "command": argv[0]}

The short-circuit happens before the config lookup, so usable_input_window, model, effort and sandbox are dropped even at call sites that pass a full config. The docstring explains why and is correct about its original purpose: "Only the command matters for reading, and the recorded argv already holds it, so a run stays observable after its config layer changes." True for reading a stream. False for everything else it has since been handed.

A third consequence, which is this section's own subject. _context_fit_verdict is invoked from exactly one place, routing.py:1601, in the dispatch resolution path. crew resume does not reach it. So a resumed run gets no context check at all — not an honest unknown, not a refusal, simply nothing — and _backend_settings would give it no window to divide by even if it did. Seven runs were resumed in this session under that gap; all succeeded, which is exactly why it stays invisible.

This is distinct from the fit-check finding above and does not replace it. There the check runs and sums two of three terms. Here it does not run.

Found by a peer session working from the utilisation denominator; the resume_plan model consequence was recorded here earlier from the opposite direction. Same line, three consequences, three independent discoveries, none of which found the others. The repair is at the function, not at any caller: a reader that wants only the command can ask for only the command.
